Arithmetic duality for two-dimensional local rings with perfect residue field Thanks: Partially supported by JSPS Grant-in-Aid 18J00415.
Takashi Suzuki

Abstract
. We give a refinement of Saito’s arithmetic duality for two-dimensional local rings by giving algebraic group structures for arithmetic cohomology groups.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 11G45; Secondary: 14F20, 19F05, 11S25

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Aim of the paper

Let
A
be a complete noetherian normal two-dimensional local ring
with perfect residue field
F
of characteristic
p>0
.
When
F
is finite, Saito
[
Sai86
,
Sai87
]
gives
arithmetic duality and class field theory for
A

by combining Kato’s two-dimensional local class field theories
[
Kat79
]
for
local fields at all height one prime ideals of
A
.
On the other hand, Serre
[
Ser61
]
and Hazewinkel
[
DG70
, Appendix]
refine
local class field theory for one-dimensional local fields
by allowing arbitrary perfect residue fields
and giving pro-algebraic group structures for unit groups.
In
[
Suz22b
]
, we give a functorial reformulation of Serre-Hazewinkel’s theory
by using the so-called “rational étale site”.

In this paper, we refine Saito’s theory in the style of Serre and Hazewinkel,
by allowing arbitrary perfect
F

and giving ind-pro-algebraic group structures for arithmetic cohomology groups attached to
A
,
in the case where
A
has mixed characteristic.
We use the formalism of the ind-rational pro-étale site
[
Suz20b
]

and the perfect artinian étale site
[
Suz21
]
,
which are improvements of the rational étale site.

Our results are arithmetic in nature but, at the same time, also purely algebro-geometric,
since the residue field is a completely general perfect field.
They also reflect the intricacy of the singularity of
A
and its resolutions.
For these reasons, we hope that our work will be of interest for not only number theorists
but also pure algebraic geometers.

However, we have to admit that the results of this paper are not in the best possible form.
Saito’s theory treats the divisor class group of
A
,
the Brauer group of the punctured spectrum,

K_{2}
-idèle class groups, unramified or ramified abelian extensions of the fraction field,
homology of graphs of resolutions and Hasse principles.
Compared to this rich theory, our theory is more abstract and not so complete,
since it is still on the level of cohomology groups.
Also, we do not give finiteness statements in the ideal strongest form.
Deducing more concrete consequences in Saito’s style from our theory
as well as appropriate finiteness statements
is the subject of our next papers
[
Suz22c
]
and
[
Suz22a
]
.

1.2.
Notation

We need some notation to state our results.
Let
F
a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
Let
\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
be the category of perfections (inverse limits along Frobenius morphisms)
of commutative unipotent algebraic groups over
F

with group scheme morphisms (
[
Ser60
]
).
It is an abelian category.
Let
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
and
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
be its pro-category and ind-category, respectively,
and
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
the ind-category of
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
.
Let
D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)
be the bounded derived category of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
.

An
F
-algebra is said to be
rational
if
it can be written as a finite product
F_{1}^{\prime}\times\dots\times F_{n}^{\prime}
,
where each
F^{\prime}_{i}
is the perfection of a finitely generated field over
F
(
[
Suz22b
]
).
Let
F^{\mathrm{rat}}
be the category of rational
F
-algebras
with
F
-algebra homomorphisms.
Let
F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}
be the ind-category of
F^{\mathrm{rat}}

(viewed as a full subcategory of the category of
F
-algebras).
Let
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
be the category
F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}

endowed with the pro-étale topology (
[
Suz20b
]
).
Let
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
be the category of sheaves
of abelian groups on
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}

and
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
its derived category.
Let
R\Gamma(F,\;\cdot\;)\colon D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})\to D(\Ab)

be the cohomology functor for
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
.
Let
\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}=\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{L}
be the derived tensor product functor
for
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
.
Let
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}
be the derived sheaf-Hom functor
for
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
.
We denote
(\;\cdot\;)^{\vee}=R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(\;\cdot\;,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

The Yoneda functor
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F\to\Ab(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})

induces a fully faithful embedding

D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)\hookrightarrow D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})

(
[
Suz20b
, Proposition 2.3.4]
).
The functor
(\;\cdot\;)^{\vee}
restricts to a contravariant equivalence between

D^{b}(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)
and
D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)

(
[
Suz20b
, Proposition 2.4.1 (b)]
).
For the perfection of the additive group
\mathbf{G}_{a}
,
we have
\mathbf{G}_{a}^{\vee}\cong\mathbf{G}_{a}[-1]
.

Let
A
be a complete noetherian normal two-dimensional local ring with
p\neq 0
in
A

whose residue field is the above
F
.
Let
\mathfrak{m}
be its maximal ideal.
Set
X=\Spec A\setminus\{\mathfrak{m}\}
.
Let
U
be a dense open subscheme of
X
.
Let
R\Gamma(U,\;\cdot\;)\colon D(U_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(\Ab)
be
the cohomology functor for the small étale site
U_{\mathrm{et}}
of
U
.
Define the compactly supported cohomology
R\Gamma_{c}(U,\;\cdot\;)
to be
the composite
R\Gamma(X,j_{!}(\;\cdot\;))
,
where
j_{!}\colon D(U_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(X_{\mathrm{et}})

is the extension-by-zero functor.
For any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, set
H^{q}_{c}(U,\;\cdot\;)=H^{q}R\Gamma_{c}(U,\;\cdot\;)
.

Let
\overline{F}
be an algebraic closure of
F
.
Let
\overline{A}
be the corresponding completed unramified extension of
A
.
Set
\overline{U}=U\times_{\Spec A}\Spec\overline{A}
.
The above construction applied to
\overline{A}
and
\overline{U}
gives a functor

R\Gamma_{c}(\overline{U},\;\cdot\;)\colon D(\overline{U}_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(\Ab)
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
r
be integers.
For
r\geq 0
,
let
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r)\in D(U_{\mathrm{et}})
(or
D(\overline{U}_{\mathrm{et}})
) be
the Bloch cycle complex mod
p^{n}

or, equivalently up to quasi-isomorphism,
the
p
-adic étale Tate twist
\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)

(
[
Sch94
]
,
[
Gei04
]
,
[
Sat07
]
,
[
Sat13
]
).
Its restriction to
U^{\prime}=U\cap\Spec A[1/p]
is
the usual Tate twist
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r)
of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
.
For
r<0
, we understand
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r)\in D(U_{\mathrm{et}})
to be

j_{!}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))
,
where
j^{\prime}\colon U^{\prime}\hookrightarrow U
is the inclusion.

1.3.
Main theorems

We will prove that the cohomology and the compactly supported cohomology of
U
have
canonical structures as objects of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
:

Theorem 1.3.1
.

(1)

There exist canonical objects

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r)),R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))\in D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)

together with canonical isomorphisms

\displaystyle R\Gamma\bigl(F,R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))\bigr)\cong R\Gamma(U,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r)),

\displaystyle R\Gamma\bigl(F,R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))\bigr)\cong R\Gamma_{c}(U,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))

in
D(\Ab)
.

(2)

For any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
,
let
\mathbf{H}^{q}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))
and
\mathbf{H}^{q}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))

be the
q
-th cohomology objects
of
R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))
and
R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))
,
respectively.

Then the base changes of
\mathbf{H}^{q}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))

and
\mathbf{H}_{c}^{q}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))
to
\overline{F}

with the natural
\Gal(\overline{F}/F)
-equivariant structures
are canonically isomorphic to

\mathbf{H}^{q}(\overline{\mathbf{U}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))

and
\mathbf{H}_{c}^{q}(\overline{\mathbf{U}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))
,
respectively.

(3)

In particular, for any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{q}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))(\overline{F})\cong H^{q}(\overline{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r)),

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{q}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))(\overline{F})\cong H^{q}_{c}(\overline{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))

as
\Gal(\overline{F}/F)
-modules,

We will prove the following refinement of Saito’s arithmetic duality

[
Sai86
,
Sai87
]
:

Theorem 1.3.2
.

Set
r^{\prime}=2-r
.
Then there exists a canonical morphism

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r^{\prime}))\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}[-3]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
such that the induced morphisms

\displaystyle R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))\to R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r^{\prime}))^{\vee}[-3],

\displaystyle R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r^{\prime}))\to R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{U},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(r))^{\vee}[-3]

are isomorphisms.

The group
H^{1}(U,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})
classifies abelian
p
-coverings of
U
.
We have an exact sequence

0\to\Pic(U)/p^{n}\Pic(U)\to H^{2}(U,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(1))\to\Br(U)[p^{n}]\to 0.

The group
H^{3}_{c}(U,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}(2))
contains
the
K_{2}
-idèle class group of
U
(
[
Sai87
, §1]
) mod
p^{n}
.
The above theorems thus give ind-pro-algebraic group structures for these important groups
and show their duality.

1.4.
Ideas of proof

The basic idea lies in Saito’s computations
[
Sai86
, (4.11) (A), (B)]

of his duality pairing when
F
is finite.
He introduces a filtration on
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
by symbols
and writes the induced pairings on the graded pieces as the pairing

F[[t]]\times(\Omega_{F((t))}^{1}/\Omega_{F[[t]]}^{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{residue}}}{{\longrightarrow}}F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Tr}_{F/\mathbb{F}_{p}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}

and similar pairings with
F[[t]]/F[[t]]^{p}
,

F((t))^{\times}/F((t))^{\times p}
,

F((t))/(\mathrm{Frob}-1)F((t))
and so on.
Now we view
F[[t]]
as a pro-algebraic group
\prod_{n\geq 0}\mathbf{G}_{a}t^{n}

and
\Omega_{F((t))}^{1}/\Omega_{F[[t]]}^{1}
as an ind-algebraic group

\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\mathbf{G}_{a}t^{-n}dt
.
We replace the trace map
F\to\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
by the morphism

\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}[1]
to the shift of
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
in the derived category
coming from the Artin-Schreier extension class

0\to\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to 0
.
The multiplication morphism
\mathbf{G}_{a}\times\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}
followed by
the morphism
\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}[1]
is a perfect pairing in a suitable sense.

How do we perform such replacement for the entire
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
and cohomology groups
in a canonical manner?
For a perfect (possibly transcendental) field extension
F^{\prime}/F
,
define the “base change”, “
F^{\prime}\mathbin{\otimes}_{F}A
”, by

\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})=W(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}_{W(F)}A=\varprojlim_{n\geq 1}(W(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{W(F)}A/\mathfrak{m}^{n}),

where
W
denotes the
p
-typical Witt vectors of infinite length
and
\mathfrak{m}
the maximal ideal of
A
.
This ring
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
is the same kind of object as
A
,
except that the residue field is now
F^{\prime}
.
Now we can run Saito’s theory for the ring
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
instead of
A
.
Then everything becomes a functor in
F^{\prime}
.
The group
F[[t]]
above is now the functor
F^{\prime}\mapsto F^{\prime}[[t]]
.
By the theory of the perfect artinian étale site,
such functors in perfect field extensions uniquely determine ind-pro-algebraic groups of interest
together with their derived categorical information.

We need to treat functors in
F^{\prime}
étale locally in derived categories.
This treatment is best done
with Artin-Milne’s relative site constructions

[
AM76
, §3, “an auxiliary site
X/S_{\mathrm{perf}}
”]
.
Roughly speaking, we bundle the étale sites of
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
for all
F^{\prime}
together into a single site,
so that it admits a morphism of sites to the perfect artinian étale site.
The cohomology groups as functors in
F^{\prime}
are nothing but derived pushforward sheaves.
In Section
4
, we develop a general theory of relative sites
and its compact support cohomology and cup product formalism.

With this machinery, we can mostly follow Saito’s theory.
In Sections
5
,
6
and
7
,
we first develop the local theory,
refining Kato’s two-dimensional local class field theory
with ind-pro-algebraic group structures.
With Saito’s computations
[
Sai86
, (4.11) (A), (B)]
recalled in
Section
8
,
the case of “enough regular”
A
is done in Section
10.3
.
For the general
A
, we take a resolution of singularities of
A
just as Saito does.
An additional local theory shows up from generic points
of the special fiber (or the reduced exceptional divisor) of the resolution.
This local theory is treated in Section
9
.

1.5.
Technical difficulties

Our duality pairings are hard to calculate
since they are genuinely derived (as can be seen from the shift
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}[1]
above)
and we have to take care of higher Ext groups.
To overcome this, the key idea is to change the topology.
If we do not work étale locally but instead Zariski locally,
then higher Ext groups vanish in the situations at hand,
and we can explicitly calculate duality pairings using Galois symbols and differential forms.
We still need to ensure that
passage from the perfect artinian étale site to its Zariski topology version
does not lose much information.
This requires a detailed analysis of Eilenberg-Mac Lane’s cubical construction,
which is done in Section
3
.

In Saito’s theory, the additional local theory
coming from generic points of the special fiber of the resolution
is Kato’s class field theory
[
Kat82
]
for local fields
with residue field a one-dimensional function field.
Unfortunately, this does not translate nicely to our setting.
The reason is that a function field over
F
is not an object
functorial in (transcendental) extensions
F^{\prime}
of
F
:
if
k
is such a function field, then
k\mathbin{\otimes}_{F}F^{\prime}
is not necessarily a field.
What works instead is that for a smooth affine curve
V
with function field
k
,
the object
V\times_{F}F^{\prime}
is again a smooth affine curve (over
F^{\prime}
).
This means that we need to develop a duality theory
for
p
-adic tubular neighborhoods of smooth affine curves.
This is the most precise description of Section
9
.

This local theory has two difficulties.
First, we use
p
-adic nearby cycle constructions
to reduce the study of duality for
p
-adic tubular neighborhoods
to the classical duality theory for smooth affine curves.
But in the latter duality theory, we have to use the version of compact support cohomology
with local components given by cohomology of
complete
local fields
rather than henselian local fields
(since henselian local fields of positive characteristic do not satisfy a duality).
We have already encountered this type of compact support cohomology in
[
Suz20a
]
,
and it is very complicated and does not work well with relative site constructions.
For example, if one considers the additive algebraic group
\mathbf{G}_{a}

as a sheaf on the (small!) étale site of
V

and pulls it back to a complete local field
\hat{k}_{x}
,
the result is no longer
\mathbf{G}_{a}
.
We deal with this problem by considering the morphism of sites

\bigsqcup_{x\text{ a boundary point of }V}\Spec\hat{k}_{x,\mathrm{et}}\to V_{\mathrm{et}}

(or its relative site version) as a
fibered site
over the poset
\{\bullet\leq\bullet\}

and taking its total site as the main setting of compact support cohomology and cup product
for tubular neighborhoods.
The generalities on this setting are explained in Section
4.5
.
This difficulty also affects the step on resolution of singularities,
where we need fibered sites over more complicated posets.
Hence we need more on fibered sites in Section
4.6

and more on completions in Section
10.5
.

The second difficulty is that the calculations of the duality pairing
for cohomology of
p
-adic tubular neighborhoods of affine curves is hard and necessarily indirect
since affine curves are not local objects.
We first need to take the nearby cycle functor to the affine curve,
work Nisnevich locally over the curve to write the pairing,
and then push it down to the base field.
This step is explained in Sections
9.3
and
9.4
.

Acknowledgments
.

The author would like to thank Kazuya Kato for his suggestion of this project.

2.
Notation and general constructions

Throughout this paper, let
F
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.
All algebraic groups and group schemes are assumed commutative.
The symbol
\subset
includes the case of equality.

2.1.
Categories

The categories of sets and abelian groups are denoted by
\Set
and
\Ab
, respectively.
For an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
,
the category of complexes in
\mathcal{A}
in cohomological grading is denoted by
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
.
If
A\to B
is a morphism in
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
,
then its mapping cone is denoted by
[A\to B]
.
The homotopy category of
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
is denoted by
K(\mathcal{A})

with derived category
D(\mathcal{A})
.
The full subcategory of
\mathrm{Ch}(\mathcal{A})
consisting of
bounded below, bounded above and bounded complexes are denoted by

\mathrm{Ch}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathrm{Ch}^{-}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathrm{Ch}^{b}(\mathcal{A})
, respectively.
Similar notation applies to
K(\mathcal{A})
and
D(\mathcal{A})
.
If we say
A\to B\to C
is a distinguished triangle in a triangulated category,
we implicitly assume that a morphism
C\to A[1]
to the shift of
A
is given,
and the triangle
A\to B\to C\to A[1]
is distinguished.

2.2.
Sites and derived categories

A good reference for these is
[
KS06
]
.
All sites in this paper are defined by given pretopologies.
For a site
S
, we mean by
X\in S
an object of the underlying category of
S

and by
Y\to X
in
S
a morphism in the underlying category of
S
.
The categories of sheaves of sets and abelian groups on
S
are denoted by

\Set(S)
and
\Ab(S)
, respectively.
Denote
\mathrm{Ch}(\Ab(S))
by
\mathrm{Ch}(S)
.
Similar notation defines
K(S)
and
D(S)
.
Let
D_{\mathrm{tor}}^{+}(S)\subset D^{+}(S)
be the full subcategory
consisting of objects whose cohomology sheaves are torsion.
The sheaf-Hom functor for
\Ab(S)
is denoted by
\sheafhom_{S}

and its
n
-th right derived functor by
\sheafext_{S}^{n}
.
Denote
\mathbin{\otimes}=\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}=\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{L}
.
For objects
G,H,K\in D(S)
,
we say that a morphism
G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H\to K
is a perfect pairing
if the two induced morphisms
G\to R\sheafhom_{S}(H,K)
and

H\to R\sheafhom_{S}(G,K)
are both isomorphisms.

A premorphism of sites
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
between sites defined by pretopologies
is a functor
f^{-1}
from the underlying category of
S
to the underlying category of
S^{\prime}

that sends covering families to covering families
such that the natural morphism
f^{-1}(Y\times_{X}Z)\to f^{-1}Y\times_{f^{-1}X}f^{-1}Z

is an isomorphism whenever
Y\to X
appears in a covering family.
Its pushforward functor
f_{\ast}\colon\Ab(S^{\prime})\to\Ab(S)
sends acyclic sheaves to acyclic sheaves,
where a sheaf
G\in\Ab(S)
is said to be acyclic
if
H^{n}(X,G)=0
for all
X\in S
and
n\geq 1
.
Acyclic sheaves calculate
Rf_{\ast}
(
[
Suz20a
, Proposition 2.4.2]
).
The pullback functors for sheaves of sets and abelian groups are
denoted by
f^{\ast\mathrm{set}}
and
f^{\ast}
, respectively.
Note that
f^{\ast\mathrm{set}}
and
f^{\ast}
are not necessarily exact functors.
The left derived functor
Lf^{\ast}\colon D(S)\to D(S^{\prime})
of
f^{\ast}
exists
and is left adjoint to
Rf_{\ast}\colon D(S^{\prime})\to D(S)

(
[
Suz21
, the paragraph after Definition 2.3]
).
An object
G\in D(S)
is said to be
f
-compatible (
[
Suz21
, Definition 2.5 (a)]
)
if the natural morphism
L(f|_{X})^{\ast}(G|_{X})\to(Lf^{\ast}G)|_{f^{-1}X}

is an isomorphism for all
X\in S
,
where
G|_{X}\in D(S/X)
is the restriction to the localization
S/X

and
f|_{X}\colon S^{\prime}/f^{-1}X\to S/X
is the premorphism induced on the localizations.
The object
G
is said to be
f
-acyclic (
[
Suz21
, Definition 2.5 (b)]
)
if the natural morphism
G\to Rf_{\ast}Lf^{\ast}G
is an isomorphism.
If
f^{\ast\mathrm{set}}
is exact (that is, commutes with finite inverse limits),
we say that
f
is a morphism of sites.
In this case, we simply write
f^{\ast\mathrm{set}}=f^{\ast}
.

Let
f\colon T\to S
be a morphism of sites
defined by a functor
f^{-1}
on the underlying categories.
Recall from
[
Suz21
, Proposition 2.4]
(or
[
Sta21
, Tags 0B6C, 0B6D]
)
that there exist canonical morphisms

\displaystyle Rf_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{T}(G,H)\to R\sheafhom_{S}(Rf_{\ast}G,Rf_{\ast}H),

(2.2.1)

\displaystyle Rf_{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}Rf_{\ast}H\to Rf_{\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(S)
functorial in
G,H\in D(T)
.
The second morphism results from the first,
and the first morphism is the derived functor of the morphism

f_{\ast}\sheafhom_{T}(G,H)\to\sheafhom_{S}(f_{\ast}G,f_{\ast}H)

of functoriality of
f_{\ast}
,
which is a morphism in
\mathrm{Ch}(S)
functorial in
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(T)
.
Here we used the following fact (proved during
[
Suz21
, Proposition 2.4]
):

Proposition 2.2.1
.

Let
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(T)
.
Assume that
H
is K-injective.
Then the complex
f_{\ast}\sheafhom_{T}(G,H)
represents

Rf_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{T}(G,H)
.

2.3.
The ind-rational and perfect aritinian étale sites

The perfection of an
F
-algebra (resp. an
F
-scheme) is
the direct (resp. inverse) limit along Frobenius morphisms on it.
A scheme
Y^{\prime}
over an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme
Y
is said to be relatively perfect over
Y

if its relative Frobenius morphism
Y^{\prime}\to Y^{\prime(p)}
over
Y
is an isomorphism.
For a ring
A
complete with respect to an ideal
I
containing
p

and a relatively perfect
A/I
-algebra
B^{\prime}
,
its Kato canonical lifting over
A
(
[
Kat82
, §1, Definition 1]
) is
a unique complete
A
-algebra
A^{\prime}

together with an isomorphism
A^{\prime}/IA^{\prime}\cong B^{\prime}
over
A/I

such that
A^{\prime}/I^{n}A^{\prime}
is formally étale over
A/I^{n}
for all
n
.
If
B^{\prime}
is flat over
A/I
and
A
is noetherian,
then it is characterized as a unique complete flat
A
-algebra
together with an isomorphism
A^{\prime}/IA^{\prime}\cong B^{\prime}
over
A/I

(
[
Kat82
, §1, Lemma 1]
plus
[
Sta21
, Tag 0912]
).

We recall the ind-rational pro-étale site
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
from
[
Suz20b
]
.
An
F
-algebra is said to be rational if it is a finite direct product of
perfections of finitely generated fields over
F
.
An ind-rational
F
-algebra is a direct limit of a filtered direct system consisting of
rational
F
-algebras.
Let
F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}
be the category of ind-rational
F
-algebras
with
F
-algebra homomorphisms.
Then
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
is (the opposite category of) the category
F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}

equipped with the pro-étale topology.
We denote
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})=\Ab(\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
,
and use similar such notation as
\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}
.

We recall the perfect artinian étale site
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

from
[
Suz21
]
.
Let
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
be the category of perfect artinian
F
-algebras
(or, equivalently, finite direct products of perfect field extensions of
F
)
with
F
-algebra homomorphisms.
Then
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
is the category
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
endowed with the étale topology.

We also use the Zariski topology on
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
Define
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}
to be the category
F^{\mathrm{perar}}

endowed with the topology
where a covering of an object
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}

is a finite family
\{F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime}_{i}\}
of objects over
F^{\prime}

such that each
F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime}_{i}
is a projection onto a direct factor
and
\prod_{i}F^{\prime}_{i}
is faithfully flat over
F^{\prime}
.
We call it the perfect artinian Zariski site.

We recall the perfect pro-fppf site
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{fppf}}

[
Suz22b
]
.
A perfect
F
-algebra homomorphism
R\to S
is said to be
flat of finite presentation (in the perfect algebra sense)
if
S
is the perfection of a flat
R
-algebra of finite presentation.
It is said to be flat of ind-finite presentation
if
S
is the direct limit of a filtered direct system of perfect
R
-algebras
flat of finite presentation.
Define
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{fppf}}
to be the category of perfect
F
-algebras
where a covering of an object
R
is a finite family
\{R_{i}\}
of
R
-algebras
flat of ind-finite presentation such that
\prod_{i}R_{i}
is faithfully flat over
R
.

We recall the functor

\acute{\mathsf{a}}\colon D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})

[
Suz21
]
.
Let

(2.3.1)

\Spec F^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{fppf}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

be the premorphisms defined by the inclusion functors on the underlying categories.
Let
h=g\mathbin{\circ}f
.
Then
\acute{\mathsf{a}}=Rf_{\ast}Lh^{\ast}
.
For objects
G,H\in D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
such that
G
or
H
is
h
-compatible,
we have a canonical morphism

(2.3.2)

\acute{\mathsf{a}}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\acute{\mathsf{a}}H\to\acute{\mathsf{a}}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
functorial in such
G
and
H

(
[
Suz21
, Proposition 7.8]
).

2.4.
Quasi-algebraic groups

A (commutative) quasi-algebraic group over
F
is
the perfection of a group scheme of finite type over
F

(
[
Ser60
]
).
The category of quasi-algebraic groups over
F
with
F
-group scheme morphisms
is denoted by
\mathrm{Alg}/F
.
The identity component and the group of components of
G\in\mathrm{Alg}/F
are denoted
by
G^{0}
and
\pi_{0}(G)
, respectively.
By abuse of notation,
\mathbf{G}_{a}
,
\mathbf{G}_{m}
and
W_{n}
(
p
-typical Witt vectors of length
n
)
are the perfections of the corresponding group schemes of finite type.
We say that a quasi-algebraic group is unipotent if it is the perfection of such a group.
Let
\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F\subset\mathrm{Alg}/F
be the full subcategory of unipotent groups.

The procategory of
\mathrm{Alg}/F
is denoted by
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}/F
.
We consider its full subcategory
\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
consisting of objects isomorphic to
filtered inverse systems with affine transition morphisms.
Its objects are schemes.
The ind-category of
\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
is denoted by
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
.
The ind-category
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
of the pro-category of
\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F

is a full subcategory of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
.
The functors
G\mapsto G^{0},\pi_{0}(G)
naturally extend to
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
.
An object
G\in\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
is said to be connected
if
G^{0}\overset{\sim}{\to}G
.
The Yoneda functor gives a functor from
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F

to
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
,
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
or
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
The image of
G\in\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
by this functor is denoted by the same symbol
G

by abuse of notation.

2.5.
Étale Tate twists

For each
n\geq 1
, we set

\Lambda_{n}=\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z},\quad\Lambda=\Lambda_{1}=\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z},\quad\Lambda_{\infty}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

They are abelian groups, but also viewed as constant sheaves on the sites at hand.

For a regular
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme
Y
,
n\geq 1
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we denote the logarithmic Hodge-Witt sheaf on
Y_{\mathrm{et}}
by

\nu_{n}(r)=W_{n}\Omega_{Y,\log}^{r}

(
[
Shi07
, Definition 2.6]
) if
r\geq 0

and set
\nu_{n}(r)=0
otherwise.
We set
\nu(r)=\nu_{1}(r)
and
\nu_{\infty}(r)=\varinjlim_{n}\nu_{n}(r)
.

Let
X
be a regular scheme of dimension
\leq 1
such that

U:=X\times_{\Spec\mathbb{Z}}\Spec\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
is dense.
Let
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
be the inclusion.
For any
x\in X\setminus U
,
n\geq 1
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we have the Bloch-Kato boundary morphism

i_{x}^{\ast}R^{r}j_{\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)\to\nu_{n}(r-1)

in
\Ab(x_{\mathrm{et}})
,
where
i_{x}\colon x\hookrightarrow X
is the inclusion
(
[
Kat86
, (1.3) (ii)]
,
[
BK86
, Theorem (1.4) (i)]
).
Define
\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)
to be the canonical mapping fiber of the resulting morphism

(2.5.1)

\tau_{\leq r}Rj_{\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)\to\bigoplus_{x\in X\setminus U}i_{x,\ast}\nu_{n}(r-1)[-r]

in
D(X_{\mathrm{et}})
if
r\geq 0

and define
\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)=j_{!}\Lambda_{n}(r)
otherwise.
Set
\mathfrak{T}(r)=\mathfrak{T}_{1}(r)
.

2.6.
Two-dimensional local rings

When given a two-dimensional noetherian henselian normal local ring
A
with residue field
F
,
we use the following notation.
Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal of
A
.
Let
X=\Spec A\setminus\{\mathfrak{m}\}
.
Let
P
be the set of height one prime ideals of
A
.
For
\mathfrak{p}\in P
,
let
A_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
be the henselian local ring of
A
at
\mathfrak{p}
,

K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
its fraction field,

\kappa(\mathfrak{p})
the residue field of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
,
and
F_{\mathfrak{p}}
the residue field of
\kappa(\mathfrak{p})
with respect to the natural valuation.

3.
Derived sheaf-Hom for algebraic groups

In
[
Suz20b
, §2]
, we studied ind-pro-algebraic groups over
F

as sheaves over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}

and a duality operation for them.
In
[
Suz21
]
, we studied a similar theory over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
.
In this paper, in Section
3.1
,
we provide some more about a duality operation over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
.
After proving a technical result in Sections
3.2
and
3.3
,
we give a similar theory (including a duality operation) over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}
.
In later sections of this paper, most (not all) of the duality results will be proved
first over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}
.
They will then be brought to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

and finally to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
.
In Section
3.5
,
we study how the functor

\acute{\mathsf{a}}\colon D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})

recalled at (
2.3.1
) behaves under base change
and commutes with the associated equivariant structures (or descent data).

3.1.
Derived Hom in the perfect artinian étale site

Here is a class of ind-pro-algebraic groups over
F

that can be treated nicely with
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
:

Definition 3.1.1
.

Consider the following two types of objects of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
:

(1)

Objects isomorphic to
\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}G_{n}
,
where each
G_{n}\in\mathrm{Alg}/F
is connected unipotent
and the transition morphisms
G_{n+1}\to G_{n}
are surjective
with connected kernel.

(2)

Objects isomorphic to
\varinjlim_{n\geq 0}G_{n}
,
where each
G_{n}\in\mathrm{Alg}/F
is connected unipotent
and the transition morphisms
G_{n}\to G_{n+1}
are injective.

Define
\mathcal{W}_{F}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F

consisting of objects
G
that admit a subobject
G^{\prime}
of Type (
1
)
such that
(G/G^{\prime})^{0}
is of Type (
2
)
and
\pi_{0}(G/G^{\prime})
is finite étale
p
-primary.

These conditions mean that the connected part of an object of
\mathcal{W}_{F}
is
built up from
\mathbf{G}_{a}
by countable successive extensions in an “ind-pro” manner.
The category
\mathcal{W}_{F}
is an exact subcategory of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
.
It is also a full subcategory of
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

via the Yoneda functor
\mathcal{W}_{F}\to\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

by
[
Suz21
, Proposition 7.1]
.

In
[
Suz20b
, §2]
, we have established a fully faithful embedding

D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}/F)\hookrightarrow D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})

and a duality operation
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(\;\cdot\;,\Lambda_{\infty})

(Serre duality) for several kinds of objects of
D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}/F)
.
In this subsection, we will bring these results to
\mathcal{W}_{F}\subset\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

and its triangulated version.
This is a continuation of and complements to
[
Suz21
]
.

Let
f,g
and
h=g\mathbin{\circ}f
be the premorphisms given in (
2.3.1
).
With regards to objects of
\mathcal{W}_{F}
,
we can freely pass between
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
and
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
:

Proposition 3.1.2
.

Let
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
Then
\acute{\mathsf{a}}G\cong G
in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})

and
Rg_{\ast}G\cong G
in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

These are
[
Suz21
, Proposition 7.9 (b)]
and
[
Suz20b
, Proposition (2.4.2)]
.
∎

We will treat a triangulated version of this statement.
For this, we need:

Definition 3.1.3
.

Define
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

(resp.
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}
)
to be the smallest full triangulated subcategory of

D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
(resp.
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
)
closed under direct summands containing objects of
\mathcal{W}_{F}
placed in degree zero.

Unfortunately, cohomology objects of an object of
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

is not necessarily representable by an ind-pro-algebraic group.
That is why we will need to eventually pass to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}

where we have full control of
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}/F
.

The functor
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
applied to objects of this category preserves
(étale or pro-étale) cohomological information:

Proposition 3.1.4
.

Any
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
is

h
-compatible and
h
-acyclic.
In particular, we have

R\Gamma(F^{\prime},\acute{\mathsf{a}}(G))\cong R\Gamma(F^{\prime},G)

for any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Suz21
, Proposition 7.2 and Proposition 7.10]
.
∎

In particular, the product structure (
2.3.2
) is available
for objects of
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Here is the triangulated version of Proposition
3.1.2
:

Proposition 3.1.5
.

The functors
\acute{\mathsf{a}}\colon D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
and

Rg_{\ast}\colon D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

restrict to equivalences between

\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

and
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}

inverse to each other.
In particular,
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
maps

\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

into
D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)
.

Proof.

We have
Rh_{\ast}\cong Rg_{\ast}Rf_{\ast}

and
Lh^{\ast}\cong Lf^{\ast}Lg^{\ast}

by
[
Suz21
, Proposition 2.6]
.
Hence we have natural transformations

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}\to Rh_{\ast}Lh^{\ast}\cong Rg_{\ast}Rf_{\ast}Lh^{\ast}=Rg_{\ast}\acute{\mathsf{a}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{id}\to Rf_{\ast}Lf^{\ast}\leftarrow Rf_{\ast}Lf^{\ast}Lg^{\ast}Rg_{\ast}\cong Rf_{\ast}Lh^{\ast}Rg_{\ast}=\acute{\mathsf{a}}Rg_{\ast}.

For
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
, we have
Lf^{\ast}G\cong G
and
Rf_{\ast}G\cong G

by
[
Suz21
, Proposition 7.6]
.
Hence Proposition
3.1.2
shows that
the above natural transformations induce isomorphisms for
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
Therefore they induce isomorphisms for the mentioned generated subcategories.
∎

Cohomology objects of objects of
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

do not necessarily belong to
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
When they do, applying
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
preserves cohomology objects:

Proposition 3.1.6
.

Let
G\in D^{b}(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Assume that
H^{q}G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
for all
q

(which implies
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
).
Then
H^{q}\acute{\mathsf{a}}G\cong H^{q}G
in
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F
.

Proof.

This is
[
Suz21
, Proposition 7.9 (b)]
.
∎

We recall Serre duality in the particular case of
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}
:

Proposition 3.1.7
.

(1)

The functor
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(\;\cdot\;,\Lambda_{\infty})

gives a contravariant autoequivalence on

\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}

with inverse itself.

(2)

If
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
is connected,
then
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})
is concentrated in degree
1

whose cohomology is a connected group in
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
If
G
is of Type (
1
),
then it is of Type (
2
),
and vice versa.

(3)

If
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
is finite,
then
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})
is concentrated in degree
0

whose cohomology is the Pontryagin dual of
G
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Suz20b
, Proposition 2.4.1 (a), (b)]
.
∎

Now the functor
R\sheafhom(\;\cdot\;,\Lambda_{\infty})
can be considered
in either
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
or
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

as long as it is applied to objects coming from
\mathcal{W}_{F}
:

Proposition 3.1.8
.

Let
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

and set
G^{\prime}=\acute{\mathsf{a}}G
.
Then

(3.1.1)

\displaystyle R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})\cong Rg_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(G^{\prime},\Lambda_{\infty}),

(3.1.2)

\displaystyle\acute{\mathsf{a}}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})\cong R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(G^{\prime},\Lambda_{\infty}).

Proof.

We may assume
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
(so we identify
G^{\prime}=G
).
By the
h
-compatibility of
G
(Proposition
3.1.4
),

Lh^{\ast}G\cong G
(
[
Suz21
, Theorem 3.15]
) and

Rh_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty}\cong\Lambda_{\infty}
,
we have

R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})\cong Rh_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{fppf}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})

by
[
Suz21
, Proposition 2.8]
.
We have

Rf_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perf}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{fppf}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})\cong R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})

by
[
Suz20b
, Theorem (2.3.1), Proposition (2.3.2)]
.
Taking
Rg_{\ast}
of both sides, we obtain (
3.1.1
).

This implies that

R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})

is in
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

by Propositions
3.1.7
and
3.1.5
.
Hence (
3.1.2
) follows from (
3.1.1
) by
Proposition
3.1.5
.
∎

Therefore Serre duality is valid also in
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
:

Proposition 3.1.9
.

Proposition
3.1.7
also holds
with all the instances of “
F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
”
replaced by “
F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
”.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
3.1.8
,
3.1.5
and
3.1.2
.
∎

In this paper, we will prove duality statements first over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

and then bring them to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
.
This is possible because of the following:

Proposition 3.1.10
.

Let
G,G^{\prime}\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Let

G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}G^{\prime}\to\Lambda_{\infty}

be a morphism in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Then it is a perfect pairing if and only if the induced morphism

\acute{\mathsf{a}}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\acute{\mathsf{a}}G^{\prime}\to\Lambda_{\infty}

by (
2.3.2
) is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
3.1.8
.
∎

In some situations, the base field is not
F
but a finite extension
F^{\prime}
of
F
.
For example, consider residue fields of a curve over
F
at closed points.
We can bring duality statements over
F^{\prime}
to
F
.
To state this, let
\alpha\colon\Spec F^{\prime\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
be the natural morphism.
Set
\mathfrak{R}_{F^{\prime}/F}=\alpha_{\ast}
,
which is the Weil restriction functor (
[
BLR90
, §7.6]
).

Proposition 3.1.11
.

Let
F^{\prime}
be a finite extension of
F
.
Let
G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}G^{\prime}\to\Lambda_{\infty}
be a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\prime\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Then the composite morphism

\mathfrak{R}_{F^{\prime}/F}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\mathfrak{R}_{F^{\prime}/F}G^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{R}_{F^{\prime}/F}\Lambda_{\infty}\to\Lambda_{\infty}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
is a perfect pairing,
where the last morphism is the norm map.

Proof.

This follows from the duality for finite étale morphisms

\alpha_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{1,\mathrm{et}}}(G,\alpha^{\ast}H)\cong R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(\alpha_{\ast}G,H)

(where
H\in D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
),
which can be proven in the same way as usual
(
[
Mil80
, Chapter V, Proposition 1.13]
).
∎

3.2.
The cubical construction and Mac Lane’s resolution

We recall the cubical construction
Q(G)
and Mac Lane’s resolution
M(G)

of an abelian group
G
(
[
ML57
]
).
See also
[
Lod98
, Section 13.2 and Exercise E.13.2.1]
.
These constructions are useful for explicitly describing
\Ext
groups over sites
in terms of cohomology groups.
The goal is to show that,
in order to prove duality statements over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
,
it is enough to prove them over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}
(Proposition
3.4.4
).

For
n\geq 0
, set
2^{n}=\{0,1\}^{n}
,
whose elements are
n
-tuples
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

of numbers
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\in 2=\{0,1\}
.
Let
Q^{\prime}(G)
be the graded abelian group in non-negative degrees
whose
n
-th term is
Q^{\prime}_{n}(G)=\mathbb{Z}[G^{2^{n}}]
,
which is freely generated by functions
t\colon 2^{n}\to G
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n+1
and
t\in G^{2^{n+1}}
,
define functions
R_{i}t,S_{i}t,P_{i}t\in G^{2^{n}}
by

\displaystyle(R_{i}t)(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

\displaystyle=t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i-1),0,\varepsilon(i),\dots,\varepsilon(n)),

\displaystyle(S_{i}t)(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

\displaystyle=t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i-1),1,\varepsilon(i),\dots,\varepsilon(n)),

\displaystyle(P_{i}t)(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

\displaystyle=t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i-1),0,\varepsilon(i),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

\displaystyle\qquad+t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i-1),1,\varepsilon(i),\dots,\varepsilon(n)),

where the sum is the group operation in
G
.
Define
\partial t\in\mathbb{Z}[G^{2^{n}}]
by

\partial t=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}(-1)^{i}(P_{i}t-R_{i}t-S_{i}t),

where the right-hand side is a (formal) sum in
\mathbb{Z}[G^{2^{n}}]
(not sums in
G^{2^{n}}
).
Then
\partial\mathbin{\circ}\partial=0
.
We consider
Q^{\prime}(G)
as a complex (in the homological grading) with differential
\partial
.

For each
1\leq i\leq n
, a function
t\in G^{2^{n}}
is said to be an
i
-slab
if
t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0
whenever
\varepsilon(i)=0

or if
t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0
whenever
\varepsilon(i)=1
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n-1
(where
n\geq 1
),
a function
t\in G^{2^{n}}
is said to be an
i
-diagonal
if
t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0

whenever
\varepsilon(i)\neq\varepsilon(i+1)
.
Let
N_{G}\subset Q^{\prime}(G)
be the graded subgroup generated by all slabs and diagonals.
It is a subcomplex.
Each term
N_{G,n}\subset Q^{\prime}_{n}(G)
is a direct summand
and the splitting
Q^{\prime}_{n}(G)\cong N_{G,n}\oplus Q_{n}(G)
can be taken functorially in
G

(
[
Pir96
, Section 5]
,
[
JP91
, Proposition 2.6]
).
This splitting does not respect
\partial
, though.

Now define a complex by
Q(G)=Q^{\prime}(G)/N_{G}
.
It has a
G
-augmentation given by
Q_{0}(G)=\mathbb{Z}[G]/{\mathbb{Z}\cdot(0)}\twoheadrightarrow G
,
(g)\mapsto g
.
In particular, we have a
\mathbb{Z}
-augmented complex
Q(\mathbb{Z})
.
For
t\in G^{2^{m}}
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2^{n}}
,
define a function
tu\in G^{2^{m+n}}
by

(tu)(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(m+n))=t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(m))\cdot u(\varepsilon(m+1),\dots,\varepsilon(m+n)),

where the dot on the right-hand side is the
\mathbb{Z}
-action on the abelian group
G
.
The linear extension of this operation defines a morphism

Q(G)\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}}Q(\mathbb{Z})\to Q(G)
of graded abelian groups.
When
G=\mathbb{Z}
, this gives a differential graded ring structure on
Q(\mathbb{Z})
,
and when
G
is arbitrary,
it gives a differential graded right
Q(\mathbb{Z})
-module structure on
Q(G)
.
The augmentation
Q(\mathbb{Z})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
is a morphism of differential graded rings
when
\mathbb{Z}
is viewed as concentrated in degree zero.
In particular,
\mathbb{Z}
can be viewed as a differential graded left
Q(\mathbb{Z})
-module.

Define
M(G)=(\dots\to M_{1}(G)\to M_{0}(G))
to be the two-sided bar construction
B(Q(G),Q(\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})

(
[
GM74
, Appendix A]
;
the notation in
[
ML57
, Section 7]
is

Q(G)\mathbin{\otimes}_{Q(\mathbb{Z})}B(Q(\mathbb{Z}),\eta_{Q})
).
We do not review the definition of two-sided bar constructions.
It is a
G
-augmented complex.
As a graded abelian group (forgetting the differential),
the complex
B(Q(G),Q(\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})
as a functor in
G
is given by

Q(G)\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{B}

for some graded abelian group
\mathcal{B}
that does not depend on
G
.
(The group
\mathcal{B}
is given by
\bar{B}(0,Q(\mathbb{Z}),\eta_{Q})

in the notation of
[
ML57
, (7-5)]

and
B(\mathbb{Z},Q(\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})
as a two-sided bar construction.)
Each term
\mathcal{B}_{n}
of
\mathcal{B}
is free.
The augmentation
M(G)\to G
gives a resolution

\dots\to M_{1}(G)\to M_{0}(G)\to G\to 0
of
G
.

The above constructions are functorial in
G
.
Hence they extend to any sheaves over any sites.
That is, for any sheaf
G\in\Ab(S)
on a site
S
,
we have sheaves and complexes of sheaves
\mathbb{Z}[G]
,
Q^{\prime}(G)
,
Q(G)
,
M(G)
in
\Ab(S)

by the sheafifications of the presheaves
X\mapsto\mathbb{Z}[G(X)],Q^{\prime}(G(X)),Q(G(X)),M(G(X))
, respectively.
The complex
M(G)
is a resolution of
G
since sheafification is exact.

3.3.
The underlying set complexes

We need to describe
R\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{Zar}}}
from a connected group
G
to a constant group
H

in order to prove Proposition
3.4.4
in the next subsection.
We take Mac Lane’s resolution of
G
.
When describing
R\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{Zar}}}
from
M(G)
to
H
,
what matter are only the underlying sets of self products
G^{2^{n}}
of
G

and various maps between them.
Thus the problem is to describe the “underlying set version” of
M(G)

or, better, of
Q(G)
.

Let
\Spec F_{\mathrm{Zar}}
be the category of
F
-algebras (or
F
-schemes)
endowed with the Zariski topology.
Let
\Set
be the topos of sets.
The functor sending an
F
-scheme
X
to its underlying set
|X|

defines a premorphism of sites
f\colon\Set\to\Spec F_{\mathrm{Zar}}
.
The pullback
f^{\ast}\mathbb{Z}[X]
of the free abelian sheaf generated by a representable sheaf
X

is given by the free abelian group
\mathbb{Z}[|X|]
generated by
|X|
.
For a (commutative) group scheme
G
over
F
,
define
|Q^{\prime}|(G)=f^{\ast}Q^{\prime}(G)
,
|Q|(G)=f^{\ast}Q(G)
and
|M|(G)=f^{\ast}M(G)
.
They are complexes of abelian groups.
The
n
-th term
|Q^{\prime}|_{n}(G)
of the complex
|Q^{\prime}|(G)
is given by
\mathbb{Z}[|G^{2^{n}}|]
.
Here is the key technical result:

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Let
G
be an integral group scheme over
F
.
Then
|Q|(G)
is an exact complex.

This in particular claims that

\partial\colon|Q|_{1}(G)\to|Q|_{0}(G)
is surjective
(no augmentation considered).

Below we will prove the proposition.
Here is the idea of the proof.
Below we identify a point of a scheme and the corresponding irreducible closed subset
or the integral closed subscheme.
For an irreducible closed
X\subset G
,
consider
V^{\prime}(X):=G\times_{F}X\subset G^{2}
as an element of
|Q^{\prime}|_{1}(G)
.
Since the group operation map
G\times G\to G
restricts to a surjection

G\times X\twoheadrightarrow G
, we have
P_{1}(X)=G
.
Therefore

\partial V^{\prime}(X)=R_{1}(X)+S_{1}(X)-P_{1}(X)=G+X-G=X.

Thus any element of
|Q^{\prime}_{0}|(G)
is a boundary.
For any
n\geq 0
and irreducible closed
X\subset G^{2^{n}}
,
we can similarly define
V^{\prime}(X)=G^{2^{n}}\times X\subset G^{2^{n+1}}

and show
\partial V^{\prime}(X)+V^{\prime}\partial(X)=X
,
thus proving
|Q^{\prime}|(G)
is an exact complex.
The problem is that
V^{\prime}
does not preserve slabs and diagonals
and hence does not induce a map
|Q_{n}|(G)\to|Q_{n+1}|(G)
.
For example, if
X=G\times 0\subset G^{2}
is a
1
-slab,
then

V^{\prime}(X)=\begin{pmatrix}G&G\\
G&0\end{pmatrix}\subset G^{4},

which is neither a slab or a diagonal.
To overcome this, we modify
V^{\prime}(X)=G^{2^{n}}\times X

so that if
X
has a zero entry somewhere
(meaning the projection to that entry is the set
\{0\}
),
then we replace the corresponding entry of
G^{2^{n}}
by zero (see (
3.3.1
)).
This new map
V
gives a well-defined map
|Q_{n}|(G)\to|Q_{n+1}|(G)
.
Unfortunately, it no longer satisfies
\partial V(X)+V\partial(X)=X
.
Nonetheless, we can prove a slightly weaker property,
namely,
\varphi:=\partial V+V\partial-\mathrm{id}

is nilpotent in each degree (Proposition
3.3.5
),
since applying
\varphi
more and more makes
X
closer and closer to the regular shape
where entries are either zero or
G
(Proposition
3.3.4
).
This nilpotence is enough to conclude the exactness of
|Q|(G)
.

Now we start proving the proposition.
For any
n\geq 0
and any
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))\in 2^{n}
,
let
p_{(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}\colon G^{2^{n}}\twoheadrightarrow G

be the projection onto the product factor
corresponding to
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))
.
For an irreducible closed
X\subset G^{2^{n}}
,
define an integer
r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))\in\{0,1\}
to be

0
if
p_{(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}(X)=\{0\}

and
1
otherwise.
Define

(3.3.1)

V(X)=\biggl(\prod_{(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}\biggr)\times X\subset G^{2^{n}}\times G^{2^{n}}

where products are fiber products over
F
.
We view
V(X)
as a closed subscheme of
G^{2^{n+1}}
via the assignment

\displaystyle\bigl(t(0,\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)),t(1,\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))\bigr)_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}\in V(X)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\bigl(t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n+1))\bigr)_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n+1)}\in G^{2^{n+1}}.

This defines a map
V\colon|G^{2^{n}}|\to|G^{2^{n+1}}|
,
which linearly extends to a homomorphism

V\colon|Q^{\prime}|_{n}(G)\to|Q^{\prime}|_{n+1}(G)
.
If
X
is a slab or a diagonal, then so is
V(X)
.
Hence it induces a homomorphism

V\colon|Q|_{n}(G)\to|Q|_{n+1}(G)
.
Define an endomorphism
\varphi
of the chain complex
|Q|(G)
by

\varphi=\partial V+V\partial-\mathrm{id}
.
The terms of
\varphi(X)
are more “regular” than
X

in the following sense:

Proposition 3.3.2
.

Let
X\subset G^{2^{n}}
be irreducible closed.
Then
\varphi(X)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination
of the following types of irreducible subsets of
G^{2^{n}}
:

(3.3.2)

\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}G^{r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}\times\overline{P_{j}(X)},

where the integers
r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))
are either
0
or
1
,
the integer
j
satisfies
1\leq j\leq n
,
and the scheme
\overline{P_{j}(X)}
is the closure of
P_{j}(X)
in
G^{2^{n-1}}
,
satisfying the following property:

(3.3.3)

if
r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))=0
,
then
p_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}(P_{j}(X))=\{0\}
.

Proof.

We have

X\subset\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}

as a closed subscheme of
G^{2^{n}}

by the definition of
r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))
.
Hence the group operation map

G^{2^{n}}\times G^{2^{n}}\to G^{2^{n}}

restricts to a surjection

V(X)\twoheadrightarrow\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}
.
This means that

P_{1}(V(X))=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}
.
Therefore

\displaystyle\overline{P_{1}(V(X))}-\overline{R_{1}(V(X))}-\overline{S_{1}(V(X))}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}-\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}+X

\displaystyle=X.

Let
1\leq j\leq n
.
For each
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)\in 2
,
define
r_{j}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)))
to be
the maximum of the set

\bigl\{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),0,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)),\,r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),1,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))\bigr\}.

Then the the group operation
G\times G\to G

restricts to a surjection

G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),0,\varepsilon(j),\varepsilon(n-1))}\times G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),1,\varepsilon(j),\varepsilon(n-1))}\twoheadrightarrow G^{r_{j}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}.

Hence

\overline{P_{j+1}(V(X))}=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}G^{r_{j}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}\times\overline{P_{j}(X)},

which is of the form (
3.3.2
) satisfying Property (
3.3.3
).
We have

\displaystyle\overline{R_{j+1}(V(X))}=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),0,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}\times\overline{R_{j}(X)},

\displaystyle\overline{S_{j+1}(V(X))}=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}G^{r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),1,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}\times\overline{S_{j}(X)}.

On the other hand, we have

V(\overline{P_{j}(X)})=\biggl(\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}G^{r_{\overline{P_{j}(X)}}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}\biggr)\times\overline{P_{j}(X)}

by definition, which is of the form (
3.3.2
) satisfying Property (
3.3.3
).
Also,

\displaystyle r_{\overline{R_{j}(X)}}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))=r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),0,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)),

\displaystyle r_{\overline{S_{j}(X)}}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))=r_{X}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),1,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)).

Combining these with the above presentation of

\overline{R_{j+1}(V(X))}
and
\overline{S_{j+1}(V(X))}
, we have

V(\overline{R_{j}(X)})=\overline{R_{j+1}(V(X))},\quad V(\overline{S_{j}(X)})=\overline{S_{j+1}(V(X))}.

Now the result follows by the definition of
\partial
for
Q^{\prime}
.
∎

The map
\varphi
is zero on a most regular
X
:

Proposition 3.3.3
.

Let
r\in 2^{2^{n}}
.
Then

\varphi\biggl(\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}\biggr)=0.

Proof.

Let
X=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}
.
Then
r_{X}=r
.
From the proof of Proposition
3.3.2
,
we need to show that

\displaystyle\max\bigl\{r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),0,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)),\,r(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),1,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))\bigr\}

\displaystyle=r_{\overline{P_{j}(X)}}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)).

This follows from the definition of
r_{\overline{P_{j}(X)}}
.
∎

Now comes the induction step.
Let
n\geq 0
and
1\leq i\leq n
.
Consider the closed subschemes of
G^{2^{n}-2^{n-i}}\times G^{2^{n-i}}
of the form:

(3.3.4)

\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\text{ s.t.}\\
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i))\neq(1,\dots,1)\end{subarray}}G^{s(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}\Biggr)\times Y,

where the integers
s(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))
for
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\in 2

with
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i))\neq(1,\dots,1)
are either
0
or
1
,
the scheme
Y
is an integral closed subscheme of
G^{2^{n-i}}
,
satisfying the following property:

(3.3.5)

For any
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\in 2
,
if exactly only one of
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i)
is
0

and
s(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0
,
then
p_{\varepsilon(i+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}(Y)=\{0\}
.

We view them as closed subschemes of
G^{2^{n}}
via the restriction of the assignment

G^{2^{n}-2^{n-i}}\times G^{2^{n-i}}\leftrightarrow G^{2^{n}}

given by

\displaystyle\Bigl(\bigl(t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))\bigr)_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\text{ s.t.}\\
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i))\neq(1,\dots,1)\end{subarray}},\bigl(t(1,\dots,1,\varepsilon(i+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))\bigr)_{\varepsilon(i+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\bigl(t(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))\bigr)_{\varepsilon(i+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}

Also consider the closed subschemes of
G^{2^{n}}
of the form:

(3.3.6)

\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}G^{s(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))},

where
s\in 2^{2^{n}}
.
Define
C_{n,i}
to be the subgroup of
|Q|_{n}(G)
generated by
the closed subschemes of
G^{2^{n}}
of the form
(
3.3.4
) and
(
3.3.6
).
Also define
C_{n,0}=|Q|_{n}(G)
,
and define
C_{n,n+1}
to be the subgroup of
|Q|_{n}(G)
generated by
the closed subschemes of
G^{2^{n}}
of the form
(
3.3.6
).

Proposition 3.3.4
.

Let
n\geq 0
and
0\leq i\leq n
.
Then
\varphi\colon|Q|_{n}(G)\to|Q|_{n}(G)
maps

C_{n,i}
into
C_{n,i+1}
.

Proof.

The statement for
i=0
follows from
Proposition
3.3.2
.
Assume
1\leq i\leq n
.
The subschemes of the form (
3.3.6
) are killed by
\varphi

by Proposition
3.3.3
.
Let
X\in C_{n,i}
be a subscheme of
G^{2^{n}}
of the form
(
3.3.4
).
Then
\varphi(X)
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of terms of the form
(
3.3.2
)
by Proposition
3.3.2
.
Let
X^{\prime}
be any such term of the form (
3.3.2
)
(in particular,
1\leq j\leq n
).
We want to show that
X^{\prime}\in C_{n,i+1}
.

Assume first that
j\leq i
.
Define
s^{\prime}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))
to be the maximum of

s(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),0,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))

and

s(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(j-1),1,\varepsilon(j),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))

if
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i-1))\neq(1,\dots,1)

and

s(1,\dots,1,0,1,\dots,1,\varepsilon(i),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))

(where the
0
is in the
j
-th position) if

(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i-1))=(1,\dots,1)
.
Then

\overline{P_{j}(X)}=\prod_{\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1)}G^{s^{\prime}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n-1))}

by Property (
3.3.5
) for
s
.
Therefore
X^{\prime}
is of the form (
3.3.6
),
thus
X^{\prime}\in C_{n,i+1}
.

Assume next that
j\geq i+1
.
For
\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\in\{0,1\}
such that

(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(i+1))\neq(1,\dots,1)
,
define
s^{\prime}(1,\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n))
to be the maximum of

s(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(j),0,\varepsilon(j+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

and

s(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(j),1,\varepsilon(j+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)).

Set
Y^{\prime}=\overline{P_{j-i}(Y)}
.
Then

(3.3.7)

\overline{P_{j}(X)}=\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\text{ s.t.}\\
(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(i+1))\neq(1,\dots,1)\end{subarray}}G^{s^{\prime}(1,\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}\Biggr)\times Y^{\prime}.

For arbitrary
\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\in\{0,1\}
,
set

s^{\prime}(0,\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=r(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)).

Then

X^{\prime}=\Biggl(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\text{ s.t.}\\
(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i+1))\neq(1,\dots,1)\end{subarray}}G^{s^{\prime}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))}\Biggr)\times Y^{\prime},

We want to show that
X^{\prime}
is of the form (
3.3.4
)
(with
i
incremented by
1
) and hence
X^{\prime}\in C_{n,i+1}
.
We will check that
s^{\prime}
satisfies Property (
3.3.5
).
Let
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)\in 2
be such that
exactly only one of
\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(i+1)
is
0

and
s^{\prime}(\varepsilon(1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0
.
First assume that
\varepsilon(1)=0

(and hence
\varepsilon(2)=\dots=\varepsilon(i+1)=1
).
Then
r(1,\dots,1,\varepsilon(i+2),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0
.
By Property (
3.3.3
), we have

p_{1,\dots,1,\varepsilon(i+2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}(P_{j}(X))=0
.
Hence by (
3.3.7
), we have

p_{\varepsilon(i+2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}(Y^{\prime})=0
.
Thus Property (
3.3.5
) for
s^{\prime}
is verified in this case.

Next assume that
\varepsilon(1)=1

(and hence exactly only one of
\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(i+1)
is
0
).
Then

\displaystyle s(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(j),0,\varepsilon(j+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))

\displaystyle=s(\varepsilon(2),\dots,\varepsilon(j),1,\varepsilon(j+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n))=0.

Hence by Property (
3.3.5
) for
s
, we have

\displaystyle p_{\varepsilon(i+2),\dots,\varepsilon(j),0,\varepsilon(j+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}(Y)

\displaystyle=p_{\varepsilon(i+2),\dots,\varepsilon(j),1,\varepsilon(j+1),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}(Y)=0.

As
Y^{\prime}=\overline{P_{j-i}(Y)}
, this implies

p_{\varepsilon(i+2),\dots,\varepsilon(n)}(Y^{\prime})=0
.
Thus Property (
3.3.5
) for
s^{\prime}
is verified also in this case.

Therefore
X^{\prime}
is of the form (
3.3.4
) and hence
X^{\prime}\in C_{n,i+1}
,
proving the proposition.
∎

Proposition 3.3.5
.

For any
n\geq 0
, the
(n+2)
-times iterate
\varphi^{n+2}
of
\varphi

on the degree
n
part
|Q|_{n}(G)
is zero.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.3.4
,
we have
\varphi^{n+1}(|Q|_{n}(G))\subset C_{n,n+1}
.
But
\varphi
is zero on
C_{n,n+1}

by Proposition
3.3.3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3.1
.

This follows from Proposition
3.3.5
.
∎

Proposition 3.3.6
.

The complex
|M|(G)
is exact.

Proof.

Since
|M|(G)=B(|Q|(G),Q(\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})
and each term of
Q(\mathbb{Z})
is a free
\mathbb{Z}
-module,
we have the (homologically graded) Eilenberg-Moore spectral sequence

E_{ij}^{2}=\Tor_{i,j}^{HQ(\mathbb{Z})}(H|Q|(G),\mathbb{Z})\Longrightarrow H_{i+j}|M|(G)

by
[
BMR14
, Proposition 10.19 and the paragraph thereafter]
.
Since
H|Q|(G)=0
by Proposition
3.3.1
,
the result follows.
∎

3.4.
Derived Hom in the perfect artinian Zariski site

Here is a consequence of Proposition
3.3.6
:

Proposition 3.4.1
.

Let
G\in\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}^{\prime}\mathrm{Alg}/F
be connected.
Let
H
be a constant group over
F
.
Then
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(G,H)=0
.

Proof.

We may assume that
G
is a connected pro-algebraic group over
F
.
It is enough to show that

R\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(G,H)=0

(since
F
can be replaced by an arbitrary field in
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
).
Let
M(G)
be Mac Lane’s resolution of
G
in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
Then
R\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(G,H)\cong R\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(M(G),H)
.
For any
n\geq 0
, the sheaf
M_{n}(G)
is a direct factor
of a direct sum of sheaves of the form
\mathbb{Z}[G^{m}]
for various
m\geq 0
.
The sheaf
\mathbb{Z}[G^{m}]\in\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
is a direct sum of
sheaves of the form
\mathbb{Z}[\Spec F^{\prime}]
for various fields
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
We have

\Ext_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}^{j}(\mathbb{Z}[\Spec F^{\prime}],H)\cong H^{j}(F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{zar}},H)=0

for
j\geq 1
.
Hence
R\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(M(G),H)
is represented by
the complex
\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(M(G),H)
.
Since
H
is constant, we have

\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\mathbb{Z}[\Spec F^{\prime}],H)\cong\Hom_{\Ab}(\mathbb{Z},H).

Hence we have

\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\mathbb{Z}[G^{n}],H)\cong\Hom_{\Ab}(\mathbb{Z}[|G^{n}|],H).

Hence

\Hom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(M(G),H)\cong\Hom_{\Ab}(|M|(G),H)

as complexes in
\Ab
.
As each term of
|M|(G)
is free, this complex represents

R\Hom_{\Ab}(|M|(G),H)
,
which is zero by Proposition
3.3.6
.
∎

Let

\varepsilon\colon\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}

be the morphism of sites defined by the identity functor.

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Let
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Let
H
be a constant group over
F
.
Then

R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}G,H)=0.

Proof.

We may assume
G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
Let
G^{\prime}\subset G
be of Type (
1
)
such that
(G/G^{\prime})^{0}
is of Type (
2
)
and
\pi_{0}(G/G^{\prime})
is finite étale
p
-primary.
Then for any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
we have
H^{m}(F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{et}},G^{\prime})\cong H^{m}(F^{\prime}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}},G^{\prime})=0
for
m\geq 1

as in the proof of
[
Suz20b
, Proposition 2.4.2 (b)]
.
In particular, the exact sequence

0\to G^{\prime}\to G\to G/G^{\prime}\to 0
in
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}/F

remains exact in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})

and
R\varepsilon_{\ast}G^{\prime}\cong G^{\prime}
.
As the statement is true if
G=G^{\prime}
by
Proposition
3.4.1
,
we may assume that
G^{\prime}=0
.
The case where
G
is of Type (
2
)
follows from Proposition
3.4.1
.
Hence we may assume
G
is finite étale
p
-primary.
But then
G
embeds into a connected unipotent quasi-algebraic group.
Hence Proposition
3.4.1

again implies the result.
∎

The sheaf
\Lambda_{\infty}
is a nice dualizing object over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

by Proposition
3.1.9
.
It has the following Zariski counterpart.
Let
\mathrm{Fr}\colon W_{n}\to W_{n}
be the Frobenius morphism
on the group scheme of
p
-typical Witt vectors of length
n
over
F
.
Set
\wp=\mathrm{Fr}-1
.
Define
\xi_{n}=W_{n}/\wp W_{n}
in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})

and set
\xi_{\infty}=\varinjlim_{n}\xi_{n}
and
\xi=\xi_{1}
.
The exact sequence
0\to\Lambda_{n}\to W_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\to}}W_{n}\to 0

gives
R^{n}\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{n}=0
for
n\geq 2

and
R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{n}\cong\xi_{n}
.
Hence we have a canonical morphism

(3.4.1)

R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty}\to\xi_{\infty}[-1]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
The sheaf
\xi_{\infty}
is
p
-divisible with
p^{n}
-torsion part
\xi_{n}
for any
n
.
The functor
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\;\cdot\;,\xi_{\infty})
is a Zariski topology version
of
R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(\;\cdot\;,\Lambda_{\infty})

in the following sense:

Proposition 3.4.3
.

Let
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Then the morphism

\displaystyle R\varepsilon_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty})

\displaystyle\cong R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}G,R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty})

\displaystyle\to R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}G,\xi_{\infty})[-1]

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition
3.4.2
.
∎

Here is the promised result in this section:

Proposition 3.4.4
.

Let
G,G^{\prime}\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Let

G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}G^{\prime}\to\Lambda_{\infty}

be a morphism in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Then it is a perfect pairing if and only if the induced morphism

R\varepsilon_{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\varepsilon_{\ast}G^{\prime}\to R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty}\to\xi_{\infty}[-1]

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.4.3
,
we have

R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}G,\xi_{\infty})[-1]\cong R\varepsilon_{\ast}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\Lambda_{\infty}).

As
\varepsilon^{\ast}R\varepsilon_{\ast}\cong\mathrm{id}
, the result follows.
∎

We give two concrete duality statements in
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}
.
In this paper, a Tate vector space over
F
is
an ind-pro-object in finite-dimensional
F
-vector spaces
isomorphic to
F^{I}\oplus F^{\oplus J}
, where
I
and
J
are at most countable sets.
It can be functorially viewed as an ind-pro-algebraic group over
F
,
namely
\mathbf{G}_{a}^{I}\oplus\mathbf{G}_{a}^{\oplus J}
,
which we call a Tate vector group.

Proposition 3.4.5
.

Let
V
be a Tate vector space over
F

and
V^{\prime}
its dual.
Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be the ind-pro-algebraic groups over
F

associated with
V
and
V^{\prime}
, respectively.
Let
G\times G^{\prime}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}
be the evaluation pairing.
Consider the natural pairing

G\times G^{\prime}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\twoheadrightarrow\xi\hookrightarrow\xi_{\infty}

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
The induced morphism

G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}G^{\prime}\to\xi_{\infty}

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

Proof.

The composite morphism

G\otimes^{L}G^{\prime}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\Lambda[1]\to\Lambda_{\infty}[1]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
is a perfect pairing by Serre duality
(
[
Suz20b
, Proposition 2.4.1]
,
[
Mil06
, Chapter III, Lemma 0.13 (c)]
).
By Proposition
3.1.10
,
this implies that the same morphism considered in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

is a perfect pairing.
By Proposition
3.4.4
, we get the result.
∎

Proposition 3.4.6
.

The multiplication pairing

\Lambda\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\xi\to\xi\to\xi_{\infty}

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

Proof.

The isomorphism

\xi\overset{\sim}{\to}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\Lambda,\xi_{\infty})

is obvious.
We show

\Lambda\overset{\sim}{\to}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\xi,\xi_{\infty}).

We have isomorphisms

R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda\overset{\sim}{\to}R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda,R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty})\cong R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda,\xi_{\infty})[-1]

by Proposition
3.4.3
.
This isomorphism followed by the morphism

R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda,\xi_{\infty})[-1]\to R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\Lambda,\xi_{\infty})[-1]=\xi[-1]

is the natural morphism
R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda\to\xi[-1]
.
Hence
\Lambda\cong R\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\xi,\xi_{\infty})
.
∎

3.5.
Equivariant structures with respect to base change

When a sheaf on a site is base-changed to the localization at some object,
it naturally acquires an equivariant structure (or a descent datum).
In this subsection, we give some basics about this topic,
paying attention to functoriality with respect to
premorphisms of sites without exact pullback functors.
This is needed to understand the behavior of the functor
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
under base change
and the associated equivariant structures.
The proof of Statement (
2
) of Theorem
1.3.1

will be a straightforward consequence of the results of this subsection.

Let
S
be a site and
\Sigma
a group.
Assume an action of
\Sigma
on the underlying category of
S
is given.
This means that for any
\sigma\in\Sigma
,
we are given an equivalence of categories

\sigma^{-1}\colon S\overset{\sim}{\to}S
,
X\mapsto\sigma^{-1}X
,
such that the composite
\tau^{-1}\sigma^{-1}X\colon S\overset{\sim}{\to}S
is
naturally
(\sigma\tau)^{-1}
.
Assume moreover that
\sigma^{-1}
for any
\sigma
preserves covering families.
The equivalence of sites
S\overset{\sim}{\to}S
induced by the functor
\sigma^{-1}

is denoted by
\sigma

(so
(\sigma_{\ast}C)(X)=C(\sigma^{-1}X)
for a sheaf
C
and
X\in S
).

Let
S_{\Sigma}
be the category where the objects are the objects of
S

and the set of morphisms
\Hom_{S_{\Sigma}}(X,Y)
for objects
X,Y

is
\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\Hom_{S}(X,\sigma^{-1}Y)
.
A morphism is denoted by
(f,\sigma)
,
where
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
f\colon X\to\sigma^{-1}Y
in
S
.
The composite of
(f,\sigma)\colon X\to Y
and
(g,\tau)\colon Y\to Z

is
((\sigma^{-1}g)\mathbin{\circ}f,\tau\sigma)
.
A family of morphisms
\{(f_{\lambda},\sigma_{\lambda})\colon X_{\lambda}\to X\}

with fixed target is said to be a covering
if
\{\sigma_{\lambda}f_{\lambda}\colon\sigma_{\lambda}X_{\lambda}\to X\}

is a covering in
S
(where
\sigma_{\lambda}
is the inverse of
\sigma_{\lambda}^{-1}
).
This defines a pretopology on
S_{\Sigma}
,
and we consider
S_{\Sigma}
as a site with this pretopology.

The functor from the underlying category of
S
to the underlying category of
S_{\Sigma}

given by sending
X
to
X
and
f\colon X\to Y
to
(f,\mathrm{id})\colon X\to Y

defines a premorphism of sites

(3.5.1)

\eta^{\Sigma}\colon S_{\Sigma}\to S.

The pushforward functor
\eta^{\Sigma}_{\ast}
is called the forgetful functor
and denoted by
\For^{\Sigma}
.
Any sheaf on
S_{\Sigma}
thus defines a sheaf on
S
.
Note that
(\mathrm{id},\sigma)\colon\sigma^{-1}X\to X
is an isomorphism
for any
X\in S
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
.
Hence any sheaf on
S_{\Sigma}
can be identified with its forgetful image
C\in\Set(S)

together with a
\Sigma
-equivariant structure

\varphi_{\sigma}\colon C\overset{\sim}{\to}\sigma_{\ast}C

(satisfying
(\sigma_{\ast}\varphi_{\tau})\mathbin{\circ}\varphi_{\sigma}=\varphi_{\sigma\tau}
).
Thus
\Set(S_{\Sigma})
can be identified with
the category of
\Sigma
-equivariant sheaves on
S
.
Here are basic properties of the forgetful functor:

Proposition 3.5.1
.

The forgetful functor
\For^{\Sigma}\colon\Ab(S_{\Sigma})\to\Ab(S)
on abelian sheaves
is exact and conservative.
It sends the free abelian sheaf
\mathbb{Z}[X]
on an object
X

to
\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Z}[\sigma^{-1}X]
.

Proof.

The exactness and the conservativity are obvious.
For a representable presheaf
X
of sets,
the presheaf
\For^{\Sigma}X
is given by

\bigsqcup_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\sigma^{-1}X
.
The forgetful functor commutes with sheafification
since the underlying functor of
\eta^{\Sigma}
is cocontinuous
and by
[
Sta21
, Tag 00XM]
.
This gives the statement about
\mathbb{Z}[X]
.
∎

Let
S^{\prime}
be another site with
\Sigma
-action.
Let
f\colon S^{\prime}\to S
be a premorphism defined by a functor
f^{-1}
on the underlying categories
that commutes with the
\Sigma
-actions
(that is,
f^{-1}\sigma^{-1}X\cong\sigma^{-1}f^{-1}X

for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
and
X\in S
naturally).
It induces a premorphism
f^{\Sigma}\colon S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}\to S_{\Sigma}

with a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}S^{\prime}_{\Sigma}@>{f^{\Sigma}}>{}>S_{\Sigma}\\
@V{\eta^{\Sigma}}V{}V@V{}V{\eta^{\Sigma}}V\\
S^{\prime}@>{}>{f}>S,\end{CD}

where the vertical arrows are (
3.5.1
) for
S^{\prime}
and for
S
.
In particular, we have a commutative diagram

(3.5.2)

\begin{CD}D(S^{\prime}_{\Sigma})@>{Rf_{\ast}^{\Sigma}}>{}>D(S_{\Sigma})\\
@V{\For^{\Sigma}}V{}V@V{}V{\For^{\Sigma}}V\\
D(S^{\prime})@>{}>{Rf_{\ast}}>D(S).\end{CD}

A particular case of this situation is where
S
is the terminal site
and
f^{-1}
sends the unique object of
S
to a terminal object of
S^{\prime}
.
In this case,
\Set(S_{\Sigma})
is just the category of
\Sigma
-sets.
Denote the functor
f_{\ast}^{\Sigma}
by
\Gamma^{\Sigma}(S^{\prime},\;\cdot\;)
.
It sends a
\Sigma
-equivariant sheaf
C^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}

to its global section
\Gamma(S^{\prime},C^{\prime})
with the natural
\Sigma
-action.
Hence
Rf_{\ast}^{\Sigma}=R\Gamma^{\Sigma}(S^{\prime},\;\cdot\;)
is the functor

R\Gamma(S^{\prime},\;\cdot\;)
upgraded as a functor valued in
D(\Sigma\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Mod})
,
where
\Sigma\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{Mod}
is the category of
\Sigma
-modules,
and
R^{q}f_{\ast}^{\Sigma}G^{\prime}=H^{q}R\Gamma^{\Sigma}(S^{\prime},G^{\prime})

for any
q
and
G^{\prime}\in D(S^{\prime}_{\Sigma})
is

H^{q}(S^{\prime},G^{\prime})
with the natural
\Sigma
-action
by (
3.5.2
).

The forgetful functor is also compatible with derived pullback:

Proposition 3.5.2
.

The diagram

\begin{CD}D(S_{\Sigma})@>{Lf^{\Sigma,\ast}}>{}>D(S^{\prime}_{\Sigma})\\
@V{\For^{\Sigma}}V{}V@V{}V{\For^{\Sigma}}V\\
D(S)@>{}>{Lf^{\ast}}>D(S^{\prime})\end{CD}

commutes (where
f^{\Sigma,\ast}=(f^{\Sigma})^{\ast}
).

Proof.

The diagram

\begin{CD}\Ab(S_{\Sigma})@>{f^{\Sigma,\ast}}>{}>\Ab(S^{\prime}_{\Sigma})\\
@V{\For^{\Sigma}}V{}V@V{}V{\For^{\Sigma}}V\\
\Ab(S)@>{}>{f^{\ast}}>\Ab(S^{\prime})\end{CD}

commutes since
the two functors
\Ab(S_{\Sigma})\rightrightarrows\Ab(S^{\prime})
coming from the diagram
both send
\mathbb{Z}[X]
(with
X\in S
) to

\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Z}[\sigma^{-1}f^{-1}X]\cong\bigoplus_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\mathbb{Z}[f^{-1}\sigma^{-1}X]

by Proposition
3.5.1
.
Since
L_{n}f^{\ast}\mathbb{Z}[X]=0
for all
n\geq 1
by
[
Suz22b
, Lemma 3.7.2]
,
this derives.
∎

Next, let
S
be a site and
\Sigma
a group (but no action given).
Let
X_{0}\in S
.
Assume a group homomorphism
\Sigma\to\Aut_{S}(X_{0})
is given
(so we have an isomorphism
\sigma\colon X_{0}\overset{\sim}{\to}X_{0}
in
S

for any
\sigma\in\Sigma
in a compatible way).
For any
X/X_{0}\in S/X_{0}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma
,
we have a new object
\sigma^{-1}X/X_{0}\in S/X_{0}
defined by the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\sigma^{-1}X@>{\sigma}>{\sim}>X\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
X_{0}@>{\sigma}>{\sim}>X_{0}\end{CD}

in
S
.
Then
\Sigma
acts on the underlying category of the localization
S/X_{0}

by sending
X/X_{0}
to
\sigma^{-1}X/X_{0}
for
\sigma\in\Sigma
.
It preserves covering families.
Hence the previous paragraphs define a site
(S/X_{0})_{\Sigma}

and a premorphism (
3.5.1
), which we now denote by

\eta_{X_{0}}^{\Sigma}\colon(S/X_{0})_{\Sigma}\to S/X_{0}
.
Let
\For_{X_{0}}^{\Sigma}=\eta_{X_{0},\ast}^{\Sigma}
be the forgetful functor.

The functor from the underlying category of
(S/X_{0})_{\Sigma}
to the underlying category of
S

given by sending
X/X_{0}
to
X
and
(f,\sigma)\colon X\to Y
to the composite

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\to}}\sigma^{-1}Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sigma}}{{\to}}Y

defines a premorphism

\theta_{X_{0}}^{\Sigma}\colon S\to(S/X_{0})_{\Sigma}.

Its pushforward functor
\theta_{X_{0},\ast}^{\Sigma}

sends
C\in\Set(S)
to its restriction
C|_{X_{0}}\in\Set(S/X_{0})

with the natural
\Sigma
