\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}:=[\pi_{T_{1},\ast}\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast}\to\pi_{T_{3},\ast}][-1]\colon\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C),

where the morphism in the mapping cone is
\varphi_{13}-\varphi_{23}
.
Note that this functor is not induced from any functor
\Ab(T_{\bullet})\to\Ab(C)
;
it is genuinely on the level of complexes.
For
i=1,2
, the morphism
f_{i}\colon T_{i}\to S
induces a natural transformation

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\colon\pi_{S,\ast}f_{\bullet,\ast}\to\pi_{T_{i},\ast}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C),\quad\text{or}

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\colon\pi_{S,\ast}\to\pi_{T_{i},\ast}f_{\bullet}^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(S)\to\mathrm{Ch}(C).

The direct sum
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
defines a morphism

\pi_{S,\ast}\to(\pi_{T_{1},\ast}\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast})f_{\bullet}^{\ast}
,
whose composite with
\varphi_{13}-\varphi_{23}
is zero.
Hence this defines a morphism

(4.6.3)

(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\colon\pi_{S,\ast}\to\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}f_{\bullet}^{\ast}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(S)\to\mathrm{Ch}(C).

We define a cup product morphism for
R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}
:

Proposition 4.6.2
.

For
G,H\in D(T_{\bullet})
,
there exists a canonical morphism

R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}H\to R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(C)
functorial in
G,H
.

Proof.

First, for
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet})
, we construct a canonical morphism

(4.6.4)

\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}H\to\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)

in
\mathrm{Ch}(C)
functorial in
G,H
.
The left-hand side is the total complex of the complex of double complexes

(4.6.5)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\to(\pi_{T_{1},\ast}G\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast}G)\mathbin{\otimes}(\pi_{T_{1},\ast}H\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast}H)

\displaystyle\to(\pi_{T_{1},\ast}G\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast}G)\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{T_{3},\ast}H\oplus\pi_{T_{3},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}(\pi_{T_{1},\ast}H\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast}H)

\displaystyle\to\pi_{T_{3},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{T_{3},\ast}H

\displaystyle\to 0,

where these three non-zero terms are in horizontal (or external) degrees
0
,
1
and
2
.
The right-hand side is the total complex of the complex of double complexes

(4.6.6)

0\to\pi_{T_{1},\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\oplus\pi_{T_{2},\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\to\pi_{T_{3},\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\to 0,

where these two non-zero terms are in horizontal (or external) degrees
0
and
1
.
We define a morphism from the degree zero term of (
4.6.5
) to that of (
4.6.6
) by

(g_{1},g_{2})\mathbin{\otimes}(h_{1},h_{2})\mapsto(g_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}h_{1},g_{2}\mathbin{\otimes}h_{2}).

We define a morphism from the degree one term of (
4.6.5
) to that of (
4.6.6
) by

\bigl((g_{1},g_{2})\mathbin{\otimes}h_{3},g_{3}\mathbin{\otimes}(h_{1},h_{2})\bigr)\mapsto\varphi_{13}(g_{1})\mathbin{\otimes}h_{3}+g_{3}\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{23}(h_{2}).

(The other choice is to map it to

\varphi_{23}(g_{2})\mathbin{\otimes}h_{3}+g_{3}\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{13}(h_{1})
,
but this choice yields a homotopically equivalent morphism
thanks to the degree two term of (
4.6.5
),
so it will not make a difference on the derived category level.)
These two morphisms form a morphism of complexes from (
4.6.5
) to (
4.6.6
).
Therefore they define the morphism (
4.6.4
).

Now this induces a morphism

\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\sheafhom_{T_{\bullet}}(G,H)\to\sheafhom_{C}(\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G,\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}H).

By Proposition
2.2.1
, this derives to a morphism

R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}R\sheafhom_{T_{\bullet}}(G,H)\to R\sheafhom_{C}(R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G,R\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}H)

in
D(C)
functorial in
G,H\in D(T_{\bullet})
.
From this, we obtain a desired morphism.
∎

Now we put a support data by
T
.
For
i=1,2,3
, we denote the composite of the natural projection
T_{\bullet,c}\to T_{i}

and
\pi_{T_{i}}\colon T_{i}\to C
by
\bar{\pi}_{T_{i}}
.
Similarly, we denote the composite

T_{\bullet,c}\to T\to C
by
\bar{\pi}_{T}
.
Hence we have natural transformations

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{10}}}{{\leftarrow}}\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{13}}}{{\to}}\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{23}}}{{\leftarrow}}\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\colon\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet,c})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C),

Define a natural transformation of functors
\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet,c})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C)

by the composite

\varphi_{\bullet 0}\colon\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{10}}}{{\to}}\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast},

where the first morphism is the connecting morphism of mapping cone
and the second is the projection onto the first factor.
Define

(4.6.7)

\begin{gathered}\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}:=[\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T},\ast][-1]\cong[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1]\colon\\
\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet,c})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C),\end{gathered}

where the morphism in the first mapping cone is
\varphi_{\bullet 0}

and the morphism in the second mapping cone is

(t_{1},t_{2})\mapsto(\varphi_{13}(t_{1})-\varphi_{23}(t_{2}),\varphi_{10}(t_{1}))
.
We have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\bar{\pi}_{S,\ast}@>{\varphi}>{}>\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}\\
@V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V{}V\Big\|\\
\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}@>{}>{\varphi_{\bullet 0}}>\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}\end{CD}

of natural transformations between functors
\mathrm{Ch}(S_{c})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C)
,
where
\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}
on the upper and lower right corners mean
the essentially same functors defined on different domains

\mathrm{Ch}(S_{c})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C)
and
\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet,c})\to\mathrm{Ch}(C)
, respectively.
This induces a morphism

(4.6.8)

(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\colon\bar{\pi}_{S,!}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}

on the mapping fibers of the horizontal morphisms
and a morphism of distinguished triangles

(4.6.9)

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{S,!}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{S,\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\Big\|\\
R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\end{CD}

in
D(C)
functorial in
G\in D(S_{c})
.
Similarly, we have a morphism of distinguished triangles

(4.6.10)

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{S,!}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{S,\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\oplus R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\oplus R\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G.\end{CD}

For
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet,c})
, the diagram

\begin{CD}\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}H@>{}>{}>\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\\
@V{\mathrm{id}\mathbin{\otimes}\varphi_{\bullet 0}}V{}V@V{}V{\varphi_{\bullet 0}}V\\
\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}H@>{}>{}>\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\end{CD}

is commutative, where the upper horizontal morphism is (
4.6.4
)
and the lower one is
\varphi_{\bullet 0}\mathbin{\otimes}\mathrm{id}
followed by
the morphism (
2.2.1
) for
\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}
.
Taking mapping fibers of the vertical morphisms, we obtain a morphism

\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}H\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)

in
\mathrm{Ch}(C)
functorial in
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(T_{\bullet,c})
.
As in the final part of the proof of Proposition
4.6.2
,
this induces a canonical morphism

(4.6.11)

R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}H\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(C)
functorial in
G,H\in D(T_{\bullet,c})
.

The cup products in the previous section and this section are compatible:

Proposition 4.6.3
.

Let
G,H\in D(S_{c})
.
Then the diagram

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{S,\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{S,!}H@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{S,!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\\
@V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V{}V@V{}V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V\\
R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}H@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\end{CD}

in
D(C)
is commutative,
where the horizontal morphisms are (
4.5.4
) and (
4.6.11
).

Proof.

It is enough to show that the diagram

\begin{CD}\bar{\pi}_{S,\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\bar{\pi}_{S,!}H@>{}>{}>\bar{\pi}_{S,!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\\
@V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V{}V@V{}V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V\\
\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}H@>{}>{}>\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}\bar{f}_{\bullet}^{\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\end{CD}

in
\mathrm{Ch}(C)
is commutative for
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(S_{c})
.
For this, it is enough to show that the diagram

\begin{CD}\pi_{S,\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{S,\ast}H@>{}>{}>\pi_{S,\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\\
@V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V{}V@V{}V{(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})}V\\
\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}f_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}f_{\bullet}^{\ast}H@>{}>{}>\pi_{T_{\bullet},\ast}f_{\bullet}^{\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\end{CD}

in
\mathrm{Ch}(C)
is commutative for
G,H\in\mathrm{Ch}(S)
.
For this, it is enough to show that the diagram

\begin{CD}\pi_{S,\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{S,\ast}H@>{}>{}>\pi_{S,\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\\
@V{\varphi_{i}}V{}V@V{}V{\varphi_{i}}V\\
\pi_{T_{i},\ast}f_{i}^{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}\pi_{T_{i},\ast}f_{i}^{\ast}H@>{}>{}>\pi_{T_{i},\ast}f_{i}^{\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}H)\end{CD}

in
\Ab(C)
is commutative for
G,H\in\Ab(S)
and
i=1,2
.
But this is obvious.
∎

The cup product (
4.6.11
) breaks down into pieces as follows.
We have distinguished triangles

(4.6.12)

\displaystyle R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}][-1]G\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}G,

(4.6.13)

\displaystyle R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1]G\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}G\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}G

in
D(C)
functorial in
G\in D(T_{\bullet,c})
.
For
G,H\in D(T_{\bullet,c})
, we have morphisms

(4.6.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}][-1]H\\
&\to R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}][-1](G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\\
&\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H),\end{aligned}

(4.6.15)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1]H\\
&\to R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1](G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\\
&\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H).\end{aligned}

Proposition 4.6.4
.

Let
G,H\in D(T_{\bullet,c})
.
Denote

(\;\cdot\;)^{\vee}=R\sheafhom_{C}(\;\cdot\;,R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)).

Then the morphisms (
4.6.14
), (
4.6.11
) and (
4.6.15
) form
a morphism of distinguished triangles

\begin{CD}R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}][-1]G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
(R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}H)^{\vee}@>{}>{}>(R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}H)^{\vee}@>{}>{}>(R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1]H)^{\vee}\end{CD}

in
D(C)
between (
4.6.12
) and the dual of (
4.6.13
).

Proof.

This reduces to the corresponding underived diagram in
K(C)
for
G,H\in K(T_{\bullet,c})
,
which is routine to check.
∎

Let

(4.6.16)

\begin{CD}T_{3}^{\prime}@>{f_{32}^{\prime}}>{}>T_{2}^{\prime}\\
@V{f_{31}^{\prime}}V{}V@V{}V{f_{2}^{\prime}}V\\
T^{\prime}@>{}>{f_{01}^{\prime}}>T_{1}^{\prime}@>{}>{f_{1}^{\prime}}>S^{\prime}@>{}>{\pi_{S^{\prime}}}>C,\end{CD}

be another fibered site over the same category
I
,
where we are assuming that the lower rightmost term is still the same
C
.
We can do the above constructions for this fibered site,
yielding functors
\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},!}
and so on.

Assume that we are given a morphism of fibered sites
from (
4.6.16
) to (
4.6.1
)
such that the morphism on the lower rightmost terms is the identity
C\to C
.
The data of such a morphism is given by morphisms of sites

g\colon S^{\prime}\to S
,
h_{i}\colon T_{i}^{\prime}\to T_{i}
for
i=1,2,3

and
h\colon T^{\prime}\to T
satisfying the obvious commutativity relations.
Let
\bar{h}_{\bullet}\colon T_{\bullet,c}^{\prime}\to T_{\bullet,c}
be the naturally induced morphism.
We will encounter this situation in Section
10.5
,
where the diagram (
4.6.1
) uses henselizations
and the diagram (
4.6.16
) uses completions.

We have morphisms

(4.6.17)

R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G,\quad R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}G\to R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},!}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G,

morphisms of distinguished triangles

(4.6.18)

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},!}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G,\end{CD}

(4.6.19)

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}G\oplus R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}G\oplus R\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\bar{\pi}_{T^{\prime}_{\bullet},!}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T^{\prime}_{1},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\oplus R\bar{\pi}_{T^{\prime}_{2},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T^{\prime}_{3},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\oplus R\bar{\pi}_{T^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G,\end{CD}

\begin{CD}R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}][-1]G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{2}^{\prime},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3}^{\prime},\ast}][-1]\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{1}^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G,\end{CD}

(4.6.20)

\begin{CD}R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1]G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2},\ast}G\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R[\bar{\pi}_{T_{1}^{\prime},\ast}\to\bar{\pi}_{T_{3}^{\prime},\ast}\oplus\bar{\pi}_{T,\ast}][-1]\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},!}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{2}^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\end{CD}

and a commutative diagram

(4.6.21)

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}H@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},\ast}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},!}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}H@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{T_{\bullet}^{\prime},!}\bar{h}_{\bullet}^{\ast}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\end{CD}

in
D(C)
for
G,H\in D(T_{\bullet,c})
.

5.
Setup for two-dimensional local fields

Recall that
F
is a fixed perfect field of characteristic
p>0
throughout the paper.
Let
k
be a complete discrete valuation field with residue field
F
.
Let
K
be a henselian discrete valuation field of characteristic zero
with residue field
k
.
The field
k
has either characteristic
p
and
0
.
Let
\hat{K}
be the completion of
K
.
In this section, we fix some notation for two-dimensional local fields,
which will be used in both Sections
6
(where
k
has characteristic
p
)
and
7
(where
k
has characteristic zero).

5.1.
Relative étale sites for two-dimensional local fields

The ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{k}
of
k
has a canonical structure
as a complete
W(F)
-algebra,
where
W
denotes the ring of
p
-typical Witt vectors of infinite length.
For a perfect artinian
F
-algebra
F^{\prime}
,
define a
k
-algebra
\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})
by

(5.1.1)

\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})=(W(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}_{W(F)}\mathcal{O}_{k})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{k}}k.

The functor
F^{\prime}\mapsto\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})
is an
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebra.
If
F^{\prime}
is a field,
then
\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})
is a complete discrete valuation field with residue field
F^{\prime}
.
There is a notion of lifting of
\mathbf{k}
to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
:

Definition 5.1.1
.

A
lifting system
for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
consists of:

(1)

a functor
\mathbf{O}_{K}
from the category of perfect artinian
F
-algebras
to the category of
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-algebras, and

(2)

an isomorphism
\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}k\cong\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})

of
k
-algebras for any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
functorial in
F^{\prime}
,

satisfying:

(1)

\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
is flat over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
,

(2)

\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
is
\mathfrak{p}_{K}\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
-adically separated and henselian, and

(3)

\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})\to\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime\prime})
is finite
if
F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime\prime}
is étale.

For a field
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
the ring
\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
is a henselian discrete valuation ring
with maximal ideal generated by a prime element of
\mathcal{O}_{K}

and with residue field
F^{\prime}
.

If
k
has characteristic
p
,
there is the following canonical such data:
For a perfect field extension
F^{\prime}
of
F
,
if we write
k\cong F((t))
, then
\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})\cong F^{\prime}((t))
.
In particular,
\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})
is relatively perfect over
k
.
The same is true if
F^{\prime}
is a perfect artinian
F
-algebra.
Define
\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
to be the Kato canonical lifting of
\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})
over
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K}
.
Then this
\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
with the natural reduction map gives a lifting system.

Even if we start from a complete ring
A
as in Introduction,
compact support cohomology requires non-complete henselian local fields,
which is why we need to treat more general lifting systems than the canonical one.

If
k
has characteristic zero,
a lifting system (non-canonically) exists:
by fixing a continuous isomorphism
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K}\cong k[[\varpi_{K}]]
,
one may take
\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})=\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})[[\varpi_{K}]]
with the obvious isomorphism

\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}k\cong\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})
.

In general, fix a choice of a lifting system for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Set
\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime})=\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
.
We have natural morphisms
\mathbf{K}\leftarrow\mathbf{O}_{K}\to\mathbf{k}

of
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebras.
Applying them to the constructions in Section
4
,
we obtain a commutative diagram of morphisms of sites

\begin{CD}\Spec\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{et}}@>{j}>{}>\Spec\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}@<{i}<{}<\Spec\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}}\\
@V{\pi_{\mathbf{K}}}V{}V@V{\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K}}}V{}V@V{\pi_{\mathbf{k}}}V{}V\\
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}=\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}=\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}.\end{CD}

With respect to
i
and
j
,
we have functors
j_{!}\colon\Ab(\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{et}})\to\Ab(\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})

and
i^{!}\colon\Ab(\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})\to\Ab(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}})

as in Section
4.3

and a functor

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}=R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}Ri^{!}\colon D(\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})

by (
4.4.1
).
We have a distinguished triangle

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}G\to R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}G\to R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}j^{\ast}G

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
functorial in
G\in D(\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})
by (
4.4.2
).

5.2.
Invariance under completion

For any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
let
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}(F^{\prime})
be the
\mathfrak{p}_{K}\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
-adic completion of
\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})
.
Then the functor
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}
is a lifting system for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Let
\hat{\mathbf{K}}(F^{\prime})=\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
.
As in Section
5.1
, we have a commutative diagram of morphisms of sites

\begin{CD}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\mathrm{et}}@>{\hat{\jmath}}>{}>\Spec\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K,\mathrm{et}}@<{\hat{\imath}}<{}<\Spec\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}}\\
@V{\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}}}V{}V@V{\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}}}V{}V@V{\pi_{\mathbf{k}}}V{}V\\
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}=\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}=\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}.\end{CD}

We have natural morphisms
\mathbf{O}_{K}\to\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}

and
\mathbf{K}\to\hat{\mathbf{K}}
of
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebras.
They define morphisms of sites

\displaystyle\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}/\mathbf{O}_{K}}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K,\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}

\displaystyle\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}/\mathbf{K}}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{et}}

by Proposition
4.1.12
.
Hence we have morphisms

(5.2.1)

\begin{gathered}R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\to R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K},\ast}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}/\mathbf{O}_{K}}^{\ast},\quad R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\to R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}},\ast}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}/\mathbf{K}}^{\ast},\\
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\to R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K},!}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}/\mathbf{O}_{K}}^{\ast}\end{gathered}

such that the diagram

(5.2.2)

\begin{CD}R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}@>{}>{}>R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}@>{}>{}>R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}j^{\ast}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K},!}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}/\mathbf{O}_{K}}^{\ast}@>{}>{}>R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K},\ast}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}/\mathbf{O}_{K}}^{\ast}@>{}>{}>R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}},\ast}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}/\mathbf{K}}^{\ast}j^{\ast}\end{CD}

is a morphism of distinguished triangles,
where we used

\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}/\mathbf{K}}^{\ast}j^{\ast}\cong\hat{\jmath}^{\ast}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K}/\mathbf{O}_{K}}^{\ast}

for the lower triangle.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

The morphisms (
5.2.1
) are isomorphisms
and hence the diagram (
5.2.2
) is an isomorphism of distinguished triangles.

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.1.6
,
the statement reduces to the well-known isomorphisms

\displaystyle R\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}},\;\cdot\;)\overset{\sim}{\to}R\Gamma(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{K},\mathrm{et}},\pi_{\mathcal{O}_{\hat{K}}/\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}(\;\cdot\;)),

\displaystyle R\Gamma(K_{\mathrm{et}},\;\cdot\;)\overset{\sim}{\to}R\Gamma(\hat{K}_{\mathrm{et}},\pi_{\hat{K}/K}^{\ast}(\;\cdot\;)).

∎

6.
Two-dimensional local fields of mixed characteristic

Let
k
be a complete discrete valuation field of characteristic
p
with residue field
F
.
Let
K
be a henselian discrete valuation field of characteristic zero
with residue field
k
.
In this section, we formulate and prove a duality for
K
and
\mathcal{O}_{K}

with ind-pro-algebraic group structures on cohomology.

Let
\mathrm{Fr}\colon\mathbf{k}\to\mathbf{k}
be the natural transformation
given by the
p
-th power map on
\mathbf{k}
.
Set
\wp=\mathrm{Fr}-1
.
For
n\geq 1
, let
\mathbf{k}^{p^{n}}
and
\mathbf{k}^{\times p^{n}}
be
the (presheaf) images of
\mathrm{Fr}^{n}

on
\mathbf{k}
and
\mathbf{k}^{\times}
, respectively.
Let
\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}
be the functor
F^{\prime}\mapsto\Omega_{\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})}^{1}
.
The Cartier operator on
\Omega_{\mathbf{k}(F^{\prime})}^{1}
defines a natural transformation

C\colon\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}\to\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}
.
All these are in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
and hence in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

6.1.
Review of known results

The main arithmetic inputs are Kato’s calculations
of Galois cohomology and his duality pairings in terms of symbols and differential forms.
These calculations are classical, and we recall them here.

Recall our notation
\xi_{n}(F)=W_{n}(F)/\wp W_{n}(F)
,
where
\wp=\mathrm{Fr}-1
,
and
\xi(F)=\xi_{1}(F)
.

Proposition 6.1.1

(
[
Kat79
, §5, Theorem 1]
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then we have
H^{q}(K,\Lambda_{n}(r))=0
for
q\geq 4
.
The boundary maps give morphisms

(6.1.1)

H^{2}(K,\Lambda_{n}(2))\to H^{1}(k,\Lambda_{n}(1))\to H^{0}(F,\Lambda_{n})=\Lambda_{n}

and isomorphisms

(6.1.2)

H^{3}(K,\Lambda_{n}(2))\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{2}(k,\Lambda_{n}(1))\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{1}(F,\Lambda_{n})\cong\xi_{n}(F).

Here
H^{q}(k,\Lambda_{n}(1))
for positive characteristic
k

means flat cohomology,
which is isomorphic to
H^{q-1}(k,\nu_{n}(1))
.
The morphism (
6.1.2
) is compatible with the morphism (
6.1.1
)
in the following sense (see
[
Kat79
, §5, Proof of Theorem 1, Step 4]
):
Let
K_{\overline{F}}
be the unramified extension of
K

corresponding to an algebraic closure
\overline{F}
of
F
.
Then the composite

(6.1.3)

H^{3}(K,\Lambda_{n}(2))\cong H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}(K_{\overline{F}},\Lambda_{n}(2))\bigr)\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{1}(F,\Lambda_{n})\cong\xi_{n}(F)

of the natural morphisms and (
6.1.1
) for
K_{\overline{F}}
is (
6.1.2
).

In particular, for any integers
q,q^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}
with
r+r^{\prime}=2
and
q+q^{\prime}=3
,
we have canonical pairings

(6.1.4)

H^{q}(K,\Lambda_{n}(r))\times H^{q^{\prime}}(K,\Lambda_{n}(r^{\prime}))\to H^{3}(K,\Lambda_{n}(2))\cong\xi_{n}(F),

R\Gamma(K,\Lambda_{n}(r))\otimes^{L}R\Gamma(K,\Lambda_{n}(r^{\prime}))\to R\Gamma(K,\Lambda_{n}(2))\to\xi_{n}(F)[-3].

Let
e_{K}
be the absolute ramification index of
K

and set
f_{K}=pe_{K}/(p-1)
.
For each
r\geq 0
, we have the symbol map

(K^{\times})^{\mathbin{\otimes}r}\to H^{r}(K,\Lambda(r))
,

(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})\mapsto\{x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\}

from the tensor power of
K^{\times}
.

Assume that
K
contains a primitive
p
-th root of unity
\zeta_{p}

and fix a prime element
\varpi_{K}
of
K
.
Identify
\Lambda(r)
with
\Lambda
for any
r
using
\zeta_{p}
.
For
m\geq 0
, let
U_{K}^{(m)}=1+\mathfrak{p}_{K}^{m}
if
m\geq 1

and
U_{K}^{(m)}=K^{\times}
if
m=0
.
Define
U^{m}H^{1}(K,\Lambda)\subset H^{1}(K,\Lambda)
to be the image of
U_{K}^{(m)}

via the isomorphism
H^{1}(K,\Lambda)\cong K^{\times}/K^{\times p}

(where
K^{\times p}=(K^{\times})^{p}
means the subgroup of
p
-th powers in
K^{\times}
).
Define
U^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda)\subset H^{2}(K,\Lambda)
to be the subgroup generated by
elements of the form
\{x,y\}
with
x\in U_{K}^{(m)}
and
y\in K^{\times}
.
Set

\gr^{m}H^{q}(K,\Lambda)=U^{m}H^{q}(K,\Lambda)/U^{m+1}H^{q}(K,\Lambda)

for
q=1,2
.
For
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\mid m
,
let
b_{m}
be an element of
K
such that
v_{K}(b_{m})=m/p
.
For
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\nmid m
,
let
c_{m}
be an element of
K
such that
v_{K}(c_{m})=m
.

Proposition 6.1.2

(
[
Kat79
, §2, Proposition 1]
)
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle k^{\times}/k^{\times p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\tilde{x},\varpi_{K}\},

where
\tilde{x}
denotes any lift of
x
to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle k/k^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}b_{m}^{p},\varpi_{K}\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}^{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x\dlog(y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}c_{m},\tilde{y}\},

where
x\in k
and
y\in k^{\times}
.

(4)

If
m=f_{K}
, then

\displaystyle\xi(F)\oplus\xi(k)

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p},\tilde{\varpi}_{k}\}+\{1+\tilde{y}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p},\varpi_{K}\},

where
\varpi_{k}
is any prime element of
k
.

(5)

If
m>f_{K}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda)=0
.

Here we used the fact
\xi(F)\overset{\sim}{\to}\Omega_{k}^{1}/(C-1)\Omega_{k}^{1}

via
x\mapsto\tilde{x}\dlog(\varpi_{k})
.

Proposition 6.1.3

(
[
Kat79
, §6]
)
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda\oplus k^{\times}/k^{\times p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\varpi_{K}^{i}\tilde{x}\}.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle k/k^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\left(\frac{\zeta_{p}-1}{b_{f_{K}-m}}\right)^{p}\right\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{c_{f_{K}-m}}\right\}.

(4)

If
m=f_{K}
, then

\displaystyle\xi(k)

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(K,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}\}.

(5)

If
m>f_{K}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{1}(K,\Lambda)=0
.

The restriction of the pairing (
6.1.4
) to

U^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda)\times U^{m^{\prime}}H^{1}(K,\Lambda)

is zero if
m+m^{\prime}>f_{K}
.
Hence for
m+m^{\prime}=f_{K}
, it induces a pairing

(6.1.5)

\gr^{m}H^{2}(K,\Lambda)\times\gr^{m^{\prime}}H^{1}(K,\Lambda)\to\xi(F).

Let
\Res\colon\Omega_{k}^{1}\to F
be the residue map.

Proposition 6.1.4

(
[
Kat79
, §6]
)
.

The pairing (
6.1.5
),
via the isomorphisms in
Propositions
6.1.2

and
6.1.3
,
can be translated as follows.

(1)

If
m=0
, then the pairing becomes

\displaystyle k^{\times}/k^{\times p}\times\xi(k)

\displaystyle\to\xi(F),

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Res(y\dlog(x)).

(2)

If
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\mid m
, then the pairing becomes

\displaystyle k/k^{p}\times k/k^{p}

\displaystyle\to\xi(F),

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Res(xdy).

(3)

If
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\nmid m
, then the pairing becomes

\displaystyle\Omega_{k}^{1}\times k

\displaystyle\to\xi(F),

\displaystyle(\omega,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto-m\Res(y\omega).

(4)

If
m=f_{K}
, then the pairing becomes the sum of the pairing

\displaystyle\xi(F)\times\Lambda

\displaystyle\to\xi(F),

\displaystyle(x,i)

\displaystyle\mapsto ix

and the pairing

\displaystyle\xi(k)\times k^{\times}/k^{\times p}

\displaystyle\to\xi(F),

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto-\Res(x\dlog y).

We give here some auxiliary results for
k
:

Proposition 6.1.5
.

Denote the functor
\Hom_{\Ab}(\;\cdot\;,\xi(F))
by
(\;\cdot\;)^{\vee}
.
We have a morphism between exact sequences

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>k/k^{p}@>{d}>{}>\Omega_{k}^{1}@>{C}>{}>\Omega_{k}^{1}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>(k/k^{p})^{\vee}@>{}>{\mathrm{can}^{\vee}}>k^{\vee}@>{}>{\mathrm{Fr}^{\vee}}>k^{\vee}@>{}>{}>0,\end{CD}

where the left (resp. middle and right) vertical map is given by the pairing

(x,y)\mapsto\Res(ydx)
(resp.
(\omega,y)\mapsto\Res(y\omega)
).
We also have a morphism between exact sequences

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>k^{\times}/k^{\times p}@>{\dlog}>{}>\Omega_{k}^{1}@>{C-1}>{}>\Omega_{k}^{1}@>{}>{}>\xi_{1}(F)@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>\xi(k)^{\vee}@>{}>{\mathrm{can}^{\vee}}>k^{\vee}@>{}>{\wp^{\vee}}>k^{\vee}@>{}>{}>\Lambda^{\vee}@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

where the left (resp. two middle, resp. right) vertical map is given by the pairing

(x,y)\mapsto\Res(y\dlog(x))

(resp.
(\omega,y)\mapsto\Res(y\omega)
,
resp.
(x,i)\mapsto ix
).

Proof.

The exactness of the rows is standard.
The commutativity of the squares follows from the facts that

C(x^{p}\omega)=xC\omega
,

\Res(C\omega)=\Res(\omega)^{1/p}
in
F

and
\Res(\omega)^{1/p}=\Res(\omega)
in
\xi(F)
.
∎

Proposition 6.1.6
.

Let
n\geq 1
.
We have

R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\Lambda\cong\begin{cases}\Lambda&\text{if }q=0,\\
\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}&\text{if }q=1,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\nu_{n}(1)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p^{n}}&\text{if }q=0,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

The first isomorphism follows from the exact sequence

0\to\Lambda\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\wp}}{{\to}}\mathbf{G}_{a}\to 0

in
\Ab(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}})
and
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\mathbf{G}_{a}=0
for
q\geq 1
.
For the second, using the exact sequence

0\to\mathbf{G}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{n}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to\nu_{n}(1)\to 0

in
\Ab(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}})
, it suffices to show
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\mathbf{G}_{m}=0
for
q\geq 1
.
This is proved in
[
Suz21
, Proof of Proposition 9.1]
.
∎

6.2.
Duality statement

Let
\mathbf{O}_{K}
be a lifting system for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
Set
\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime})=\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}K
for
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
The cohomological dimension of
R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)
is
2

and there is a trace morphism:

Proposition 6.2.1
.

For any
q\geq 3
,
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\geq 1
, we have

R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)=0
.
There exists a unique morphism

(6.2.1)

R^{2}\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2)\to\Lambda_{n}

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
such that for any field
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
the composite

H^{2}(\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime}),\Lambda_{n}(2))\to\Gamma\bigl(F^{\prime},R^{2}\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2)\bigr)\to\Lambda_{n}

with the natural map is the map (
6.1.1
) for the field
\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Since
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)\in\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
is
the étale sheafification of the presheaf

F^{\prime}\mapsto H^{q}(\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime}),\Lambda_{n}(r)),

this follows from Proposition
6.1.1
.
∎

Hence we have canonical morphisms

R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2)\to\Lambda[-2]\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-2]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Here is the duality for
K
over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
:

Proposition 6.2.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
r+r^{\prime}=2
.

(1)

We have

R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.

(2)

The composite morphism

(6.2.2)

R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(r^{\prime})\to R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2)\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-2]

of (
2.2.1
) and (
6.2.1
) is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

We will prove this below.
After the proof, we will pass to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}

in Section
6.4
.

6.3.
Duality in the perfect artinian Zariski topology

We work with the Zariski version
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}

to write down the duality pairing
and prove a Zariski version of the duality.

Assume that
K
contains a primitive
p
-th root of unity
\zeta_{p}
.
We identify
\Lambda(r)
for any
r
with
\Lambda
by this choice of
\zeta_{p}
.
We fix a prime element
\varpi_{K}
of
K

and a prime element
\varpi_{k}
of
k
.
Let

\mathcal{E}_{K}=R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda\in D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}),\quad\mathcal{F}_{K}=R\varepsilon_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{K}\in D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}).

We have
H^{0}\mathcal{F}_{K}\cong\Lambda
.
Also,
\Gamma(F^{\prime},H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K})=H^{q}(\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime}),\Lambda)

for any
q
and
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
Hence
H^{q}\mathcal{F}=0
for
q\geq 4
and we have a canonical isomorphism

H^{3}\mathcal{F}_{K}\overset{\sim}{\to}\xi

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
by Proposition
6.1.1
.
By what we saw in (
6.1.3
),
this morphism agrees with the composite

H^{3}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}H^{2}\mathcal{E}_{K}\to R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda=\xi,

where the second morphism is (
6.2.1
).

Proposition 6.3.1
.

The composite

\mathcal{F}_{K}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\xi_{\infty}[-3]

obtained by applying
R\varepsilon_{\ast}
to (
6.2.2
)
is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

We will prove this below.
For
m\geq 0
and
q=1,2
, define

U^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}\in\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})

to be the functor

F^{\prime}\mapsto U^{m}H^{q}(\mathbf{K}(F^{\prime}),\Lambda)
,
and
\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}\in\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
its graded pieces.
For
m\geq 0
, Propositions
6.1.2
and
6.1.3
give isomorphisms

(6.3.1)

\gr^{m}H^{2}\mathcal{F}_{K}\cong\begin{cases}\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p}&\text{if }m=0,\\
\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\mid m,\\
\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\nmid m,\\
\xi\oplus\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}&\text{if }m=f_{K},\\
0&\text{if }m>f_{K}\end{cases}

and

(6.3.2)

\gr^{m}H^{1}\mathcal{F}_{K}\cong\begin{cases}\Lambda\oplus\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p}&\text{if }m=0,\\
\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\mid m,\\
\mathbf{k}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\nmid m,\\
\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}&\text{if }m=f_{K},\\
0&\text{if }m>f_{K},\end{cases}

where the image
\wp\mathbf{k}
of
\wp
and the quotients are taken in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
We have a pairing

H^{2}\mathcal{F}_{K}\times H^{1}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to H^{3}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\xi_{\infty}

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
For
m,m^{\prime}\geq 0
with
m+m^{\prime}>f_{K}
, this is zero on

U^{m}H^{2}\mathcal{F}_{K}\times U^{m^{\prime}}H^{1}\mathcal{F}_{K}

and, for
m,m^{\prime}\geq 0
with
m+m^{\prime}=f_{K}
, induces a pairing

(6.3.3)

\gr^{m}H^{2}\mathcal{F}_{K}\times\gr^{m^{\prime}}H^{1}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\xi_{\infty}.

By Proposition
6.1.4

and via the above isomorphisms,
this pairing can be translated into the following pairings:

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p}\times\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}

\displaystyle\to\xi_{\infty},

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Res(y\dlog(x)).

for
m=0
;

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}\times\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}

\displaystyle\to\xi_{\infty},

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Res(xdy).

for
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\mid m
;

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}\times\mathbf{k}

\displaystyle\to\xi_{\infty},

\displaystyle(\omega,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto-m\Res(y\omega).

for
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\nmid m
;
the sum of

\displaystyle\xi\times\Lambda

\displaystyle\to\xi_{\infty},

\displaystyle(x,i)

\displaystyle\mapsto ix

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}\times\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p}

\displaystyle\to\xi_{\infty},

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto-\Res(x\dlog y).

for
m=f_{K}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.3.1
.

We need to show that the induced morphism

H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\xi_{\infty}

for
q+q^{\prime}=3
is a perfect pairing.
For
q=0
and
3
, this follows from Proposition
3.4.6
.
For
q=1
or
2
, it is enough to show that the induced morphism

\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\gr^{m^{\prime}}H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\xi_{\infty}

for
m+m^{\prime}=f_{K}
is a perfect pairing.
The pairing
\Omega_{k}^{1}\times k\to\xi_{1}(F)
,

(\omega,y)\mapsto\Res(y\omega)
,
lifts to the perfect pairing

\Omega_{k}^{1}\times k\to F

of Tate vector spaces over
k
.
Therefore Proposition
3.4.5

implies that the induced morphisms

\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\mathbf{k}\to\xi_{\infty}

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
By the above descriptions of (
6.3.3
),
this implies the case
0<m<f_{K}
,
p\nmid m
.
With Proposition
3.4.6
,
all other cases follow from
Proposition
6.1.5
.
∎

6.4.
Proof of the duality

Now we return to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
and prove the duality.

Proof of Proposition
6.2.2
.

First assume that
\zeta_{p}\in K
and
n=1
.
For any
q
, the étale sheafification of
H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}
is
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{K}
.
Hence
H^{0}\mathcal{E}_{K}\cong\Lambda
and
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{K}=0
for
q\geq 3

(as
\xi
sheafifies to zero).
Also (
6.3.1
) and (
6.3.2
) show that for
q=1,2
,
the sheaf
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{K}
has a filtration
whose successive subquotients are isomorphic to either

\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p},\quad\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p},\quad\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1},\quad\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k},\quad\Lambda,\quad\mathbf{k}.

All these subquotients are isomorphic to either

\mathbf{G}_{a}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathbf{G}_{a}^{\oplus\mathbb{N}}
,
\Lambda
or their finite product.
Hence they belong to
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
Therefore
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{K}\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
Now applying Proposition
3.4.4

for
G=G^{\prime}=\mathcal{E}_{K}
and using
Proposition
6.3.1
,
we prove the proposition in this case.

Since
p\nmid[K(\zeta_{p}):K]
,
by the usual argument using norm maps,
we can see that the proposition for
K(\zeta_{p})
implies the proposition for
K

by taking a direct summand when
n=1
.
(This is where the closure by direct summands
for the definition of
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
is necessary.)
This implies the case
n\geq 1
.
∎

The proof above is showing slightly stronger results about

R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda
(when
\zeta_{p}\in K
):

Proposition 6.4.1
.

Assume
\zeta_{p}\in K
.
Then
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
for all
q
.
The kernel of the morphism (
6.2.1
) with
n=1
is a connected group in
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.

We now bring the duality to
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}}
:

Theorem 6.4.2
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
r+r^{\prime}=2
.

(1)

We have

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{K},\Lambda_{n}(r))\in D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)
.

(2)

The morphism

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{K},\Lambda_{n}(r))\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{K},\Lambda_{n}(r^{\prime}))\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-2]

obtained by applying
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
to the morphism (
6.2.2
)
is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
6.2.2
,
3.1.5
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.10
.
∎

6.5.
Duality for the ring of integers

We give a version of the above duality for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
instead of
K
.
The coefficient sheaves are now
p
-adic étale Tate twists.

For
n\geq 1
, we have a distinguished triangle

\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\to Rj_{\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2)\to i_{\ast}\nu_{n}(1)[-2]

in
D(\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})
.
Comparing this with the distinguished triangle

i_{\ast}Ri^{!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\to\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\to Rj_{\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2),

we know that
Ri^{!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\cong i_{\ast}\nu_{n}(1)[-3]
.
Hence

(6.5.1)

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\cong R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\nu_{n}(1)[-3]\cong\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p^{n}}[-3]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Composing this with the normalized valuation map to
\Lambda
, we obtain a canonical morphism

(6.5.2)

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\to\Lambda_{n}[-3]

such that the composite

R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\Lambda_{n}(2)\to R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)[1]\to\Lambda_{n}[-2]

with the natural morphism is the morphism (
6.2.1
).
For any
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have a product structure

\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r^{\prime})\to\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r+r^{\prime})

as in
[
Sat07
, Proposition 4.2.6]

(which is

j_{!}\Lambda_{n}(r)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r^{\prime})\cong j_{!}\Lambda_{n}(r+r^{\prime})\to\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r+r^{\prime})

if
r<0
).
Applying (
4.4.3
) to this, we obtain a canonical morphism

(6.5.3)

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r^{\prime})\to R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r+r^{\prime}).

Here is a duality for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
:

Proposition 6.5.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
r+r^{\prime}=2
.

(1)

We have

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r),R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
,
which are concentrated in degrees
\leq 2
,
\leq 3
, respectively.

(2)

The composite morphism

(6.5.4)

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r^{\prime})\to R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

of (
6.5.3
) and (
6.5.2
) is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

We will prove this below.
Since this reduces to Proposition
6.2.2
if
r>2

and both
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r)

and
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r^{\prime})
are zero if
r<0
,
we may assume that
r
(and hence also
r^{\prime}
) is
0,1
or
2
.
We again work with the Zariski topology first and then étale sheafify.

First, assume that
\zeta_{p}\in K
and
n=1
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}=R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r),\quad\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}=R\varepsilon_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}.

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}=R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(r),\quad\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}=R\varepsilon_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}.

We have a distinguished triangle

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}\to\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}\to\mathcal{F}_{K}.

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

Proposition 6.5.2
.

The objects
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}

are concentrated in degrees
\leq 2
and
\leq 3
, respectively.
The composite

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r^{\prime}}\to R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]\to\xi_{\infty}[-4]

induced by (
6.5.4
) is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.

Proof.

Let
R\Psi\Lambda=i^{\ast}Rj_{\ast}\Lambda\in D(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}})
and

R^{q}\Psi\Lambda=H^{q}R\Psi\Lambda
.
For
q=1,2
,
the filtration
U^{m}H^{q}(K,\Lambda)
of
H^{q}(K,\Lambda)
defines
a filtration
U^{m}R^{q}\Psi\Lambda

of the sheaf
R^{q}\Psi\Lambda

by varying the residue field
k

and taking the étale sheafification in
k

(which is the Bloch-Kato filtration
[
BK86
, Section (1.2)]
).
By taking the étale sheafification in
k
in Propositions
6.1.2
and
6.1.3
,
we know that its graded pieces
\gr^{m}R^{q}\Psi\Lambda
are given by

\gr^{m}R^{2}\Psi\Lambda\cong\begin{cases}\nu(1)&\text{if }m=0,\\
\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\mid m,\\
\Omega^{1}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\nmid m,\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

\gr^{m}R^{1}\Psi\Lambda\cong\begin{cases}\Lambda\oplus\nu(1)&\text{if }m=0,\\
\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\mid m,\\
\mathbf{G}_{a}&\text{if }0<m<f_{K},\,p\nmid m,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(see also
[
BK86
, Corollary (1.4.1)]
),
and
R^{0}\Psi\Lambda\cong\Lambda
and
R^{q}\Psi\Lambda=0
for
q\geq 3
.

We have a distinguished triangle

i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)\to\tau_{\leq r}R\Psi\Lambda\to\nu(r-1)[-r]

in
D(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Let
U^{m}H^{q}i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)\subset H^{q}i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)
be
the inverse image of
U^{m}R^{q}\Psi\Lambda

and
\gr^{m}H^{q}i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)
its graded pieces.
Then the morphism
i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)\to R\Psi\Lambda

and the above descriptions of
\gr^{m}R^{q}\Psi\Lambda
induce
the following isomorphisms for
\gr^{m}H^{q}i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)
:

\begin{array}[]{c|cc}\gr^{m}H^{q}i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(1)&q=0&q=1\\
\hline\cr m=0&\Lambda&\nu(1)\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\mid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\nmid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{G}_{a}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}\gr^{m}H^{q}i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(2)&q=0&q=1&q=2\\
\hline\cr m=0&\Lambda&\Lambda\oplus\nu(1)&0\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\mid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}&\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\nmid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{G}_{a}&\Omega^{1}\end{array}

Of course we have
i^{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(0)\cong\Lambda
.
The graded pieces for values of
q
and
m
not mentioned in the above tables are all zero.

Let
U^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
be the inverse image of

U^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}
via the morphism
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}\to\mathcal{F}_{K}
.
Let
\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
be the graded pieces for this filtration.
Then the above tables induce the following isomorphisms for
\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
:

\begin{array}[]{c|cc}\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{0}&q=0&q=1\\
\hline\cr m=0&\Lambda&0\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\mid m\end{array}&0&0\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\nmid m\end{array}&0&0\\
m=f_{K}&0&\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{1}&q=0&q=1&q=2\\
\hline\cr m=0&\Lambda&\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p}&0\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\mid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}&0\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\nmid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{k}&0\\
m=f_{K}&0&\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}&\xi\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{c|ccc}\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{2}&q=0&q=1&q=2\\
\hline\cr m=0&\Lambda&\Lambda\oplus\mathbf{k}^{\times}/\mathbf{k}^{\times p}&0\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\mid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}&\mathbf{k}/\mathbf{k}^{p}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}0<m<f_{K},\\
p\nmid m\end{array}&0&\mathbf{k}&\Omega_{\mathbf{k}}^{1}\\
m=f_{K}&0&\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}&\mathbf{k}/\wp\mathbf{k}\oplus\xi\end{array}

Again the graded pieces for values of
q
and
m
not mentioned in the above tables are all zero.
From these tables, it follows that the morphism

H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}\to H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}

is injective for all
q
,
and the cokernel of this injection is
H^{q+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}
.
Also
H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
and
H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}
are
finite successive extensions of Tate vector groups,
\Lambda
and
\xi
.
Therefore

\sheafext_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}^{j}(H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r},\xi_{\infty})=\sheafext_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}^{j}(H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r},\xi_{\infty})=0

for all
j\geq 1
by Propositions
3.4.5
and
3.4.6
.
Proposition
4.3.2
then gives a morphism of short exact sequences

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}@>{}>{}>H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}@>{}>{}>H^{q+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>(H^{q^{\prime}+1}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r^{\prime}})^{\vee}@>{}>{}>(H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{K})^{\vee}@>{}>{}>(H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r^{\prime}})^{\vee}@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

for
q+q^{\prime}=3
, where
(\;\cdot\;)^{\vee}
denotes
\sheafhom_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}}}(\;\cdot\;,\xi_{\infty})
.
The middle vertical morphism is an isomorphism
by Proposition
6.3.1
.
From the above tables of
\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
,
we can see that
H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}
is the exact annihilator
of
H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r^{\prime}}
in the pairing

H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{K}\times H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{K}\to\xi_{\infty}
,
namely that the left vertical morphism is an isomorphism.
This implies the result.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5.1
.

First assume that
\zeta_{p}\in K
and
n=1
.
The étale sheafifications of

H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}

and
H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}
are

H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}

and
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}
, respectively.
Hence the tables in the proof of Proposition
6.5.2
show that

H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r}

and
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}
are in
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
Thus

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{r},\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\prime r}\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Now Propositions
6.5.2
and
3.4.4
give the result in this case.

Next assume that
n=1
.
Let
K^{\prime}=K(\zeta_{p})
, with residue field
k^{\prime}

We may assume that the residue field of
k^{\prime}
is
F
.
For
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
, let
\mathbf{O}_{K^{\prime}}(F^{\prime})=\mathbf{O}_{K}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathcal{O}_{K^{\prime}}
.
Define
\Spec\mathbf{O}_{K^{\prime},\mathrm{et}}
,
\Spec\mathbf{K}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{et}}
,
\Spec\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{et}}
and so on similarly.
Let
f\colon\Spec\mathbf{O}_{K^{\prime},\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}
be the natural morphism.
Then we have canonical morphisms
\mathfrak{T}(r)\to f_{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)

and
f_{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)\to\mathfrak{T}(r)

by
[
Sat07
, Theorem 1.1.2]
.
The composite
\mathfrak{T}(r)\to f_{\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)\to\mathfrak{T}(r)

is multiplication by
[K^{\prime}:K]
by the projection formula
[
Sat07
, Corollary 7.2.4]

and hence an isomorphism.
Hence
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)
and
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathfrak{T}(r)

are direct summands of

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K^{\prime}},\ast}\mathfrak{T}(r)
and
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K^{\prime}},!}\mathfrak{T}(r)
,
respectively.
Now the same projection formula implies that the statement for
\mathcal{O}_{K^{\prime}}

implies that for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

The statement for general
n
follows from that for
n=1
.
∎

Theorem 6.5.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
r+r^{\prime}=2
.

(1)

We have

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{O}_{K},\Lambda_{n}(r)),R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{O}_{K},\Lambda_{n}(r)),\in D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)
.

(2)

The morphism

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{O}_{K},\Lambda_{n}(r))\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{O}_{K},\Lambda_{n}(r^{\prime}))\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

obtained by applying
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
to the morphism (
6.5.4
)
is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
6.5.1
,
3.1.5
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.10
.
∎

7.
Two-dimensional local fields with mixed characteristic residue field

Let
k
be a complete discrete valuation field of characteristic zero with residue field
F
.
Let
K
be a henselian discrete valuation field with residue field
k
.
We will give a duality for
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
We already have a duality for
k
by
[
Suz22b
]
.
Since
k
has characteristic zero,
we can just apply Verdier duality to pass to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.
We still need to make sure the duality statement can be stated
in the manner parallel to the duality in the previous section,
so that these two duality statements can be applied to
the local fields of arbitrary height one primes of two-dimensional local rings
in a coherent manner.
Since the statement for
k
in
[
Suz22b
]
treats slightly more general coefficients than
\Lambda_{n}(r)
,
we can prove a slightly more general result than needed.

7.1.
Nearby cycle duality

Let
D^{b}_{c}(k_{\mathrm{et}})\subset D^{b}(k_{\mathrm{et}})
be the full subcategory of objects
with constructible cohomology sheaves.

Proposition 7.1.1
.

Let
N\in D^{b}_{c}(k_{\mathrm{et}})

and set
M=R\sheafhom_{k_{\mathrm{et}}}(N,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(1))
.
View them as objects of
D(\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}})
via pullback.

(1)

We have
R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}N\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Alg}/F)
.

(2)

There exists a canonical morphism

R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-1].

(3)

The composite morphism

R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}N\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}M\to R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-1].

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Suz22b
, Theorem A]
,
with
\Spec F^{\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{et}}
replaced by
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}

in the same way as
\Spec F^{\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{et}}
replaced by
\Spec F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{et}}

in
[
Suz22b
, Theorem 2.1.5]
.
∎

Note that if
N
has order prime to
p
,
then
R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}N
is a complex of finite étale group schemes over
F
.

Let
j_{0}\colon\Spec K_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}
and

i_{0}\colon\Spec k_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}
be the natural morphisms.
Let
N\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})
.
Then
i_{0}^{\ast}N,Ri_{0}^{!}N\in D^{b}_{c}(k_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Set
M=R\sheafhom_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}}(N,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2))
.
We have a canonical morphism

Ri_{0}^{!}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)\cong\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(1)[-2]
.
The composite morphism

i_{0}^{\ast}N\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}Ri_{0}^{!}M\to Ri_{0}^{!}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(1)[-2]

is a perfect pairing in
D(k_{\mathrm{et}})
by Verdier duality
(see for example
[
Ill77
, Exposé I, Theorem 5.1]
).
Combining this with the above proposition, we get:

Proposition 7.1.2
.

Let
N\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})

and set
M=R\sheafhom_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}}(N,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2))
.

(1)

We have
R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}i_{0}^{\ast}N,R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}Ri_{0}^{!}M\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Alg}/F)
.

(2)

There exists a canonical morphism

R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}Ri_{0}^{!}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-3].

(3)

The composite morphism

R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}i_{0}^{\ast}N\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}Ri_{0}^{!}M\to R\pi_{\mathbf{k}\ast}Ri_{0}^{!}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-3]

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

This contains a duality statement for
D^{b}_{c}(K_{\mathrm{et}})

as a special case where
N
is the
Rj_{0,\ast}
of an object of
D^{b}_{c}(K_{\mathrm{et}})
.

7.2.
Duality with relative sites

We use the notation of Section
5.1
.
We fix a lifting system
\mathbf{O}_{K}
for
K
.
We have a commutative diagram of morphisms of sites

\begin{CD}\Spec\mathbf{K}_{\mathrm{et}}@>{j}>{}>\Spec\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}@<{i}<{}<\Spec\mathbf{k}_{\mathrm{et}}\\
@V{}V{f_{K}}V@V{}V{f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}}V@V{}V{f_{k}}V\\
\Spec K_{\mathrm{et}}@>{j_{0}}>{}>\Spec\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}@<{i_{0}}<{}<\Spec k_{\mathrm{et}},\end{CD}

where the vertical morphisms come from
Proposition
4.1.6
.
The functors
i^{\ast}
and
i_{0}^{\ast}

(resp.
Ri^{!}
and
Ri_{0}^{!}
) are compatible with each other:

Proposition 7.2.1
.

The natural morphism of distinguished triangles

\begin{CD}f_{k}^{\ast}Ri_{0}^{!}@>{}>{}>f_{k}^{\ast}i_{0}^{\ast}@>{}>{}>f_{k}^{\ast}i_{0}^{\ast}Rj_{0,\ast}j_{0}^{\ast}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
Ri^{!}f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}@>{}>{}>i^{\ast}f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}@>{}>{}>i^{\ast}Rj_{\ast}j^{\ast}f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}\end{CD}

is an isomorphism of triangles.

Proof.

The middle vertical morphism is obviously an isomorphism.
The right vertical morphism is given by

f_{k}^{\ast}i_{0}^{\ast}Rj_{0,\ast}j_{0}^{\ast}\cong i^{\ast}f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}Rj_{0,\ast}j_{0}^{\ast}\to i^{\ast}Rj_{\ast}f_{K}^{\ast}j_{0}^{\ast}\cong i^{\ast}Rj_{\ast}j^{\ast}f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}.

Hence it is enough to show that

f_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}Rj_{0,\ast}\to Rj_{\ast}f_{K}^{\ast}

is an isomorphism.
Let
F_{1}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
be a field
and set
K_{1}=\mathbf{K}(F_{1})
.
Consider the natural morphisms

\begin{CD}\Spec K_{1,\mathrm{et}}@>{j_{1}}>{}>\Spec\mathcal{O}_{K_{1},\mathrm{et}}\\
@V{}V{f_{K_{1}/K}}V@V{}V{f_{\mathcal{O}_{K_{1}}/\mathcal{O}_{K}}}V\\
\Spec K_{\mathrm{et}}@>{j_{0}}>{}>\Spec\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}.\end{CD}

By Proposition
4.1.6
,
it is enough to show that

f_{\mathcal{O}_{K_{1}}/\mathcal{O}_{K}}^{\ast}Rj_{0,\ast}\to Rj_{1,\ast}f_{K_{1}/K}^{\ast}

is an isomorphism for any
F_{1}
.
This follows from the fact that the inertia groups of
K
and
K_{1}
are both isomorphic to
\hat{\mathbb{Z}}
.
∎

Proposition 7.2.2
.

For any
N\in D(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})
, we have

R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}i_{0}^{\ast}N\cong R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}i^{\ast}N\cong R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}N,\quad R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}Ri_{0}^{!}N\cong R\pi_{\mathbf{k},\ast}Ri^{!}N\cong R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}N.

(where appropriate pullback functors are omitted).

Proof.

This follows from the previous proposition.
∎

In particular, we have canonical morphisms

(7.2.1)

R\pi_{\mathbf{K},\ast}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)\to R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)[1]\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-2]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Now we can state the duality in terms of

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}
and
R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}
:

Proposition 7.2.3
.

Let
N\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})

and set
M=R\sheafhom_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}}(N,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2))
.
View them as objects of
D(\mathbf{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})
via pullback.

(1)

We have

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}N,R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}N\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Alg}/F)
.

(2)

The composite morphism

(7.2.2)

R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},\ast}N\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}M\to R\pi_{\mathbf{O}_{K},!}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-3]

is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from previous propositions.
∎

Theorem 7.2.4
.

Let
N\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}})

and set
M=R\sheafhom_{\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{et}}}(N,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(2))
.

(1)

We have

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{O}_{K},N),R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{O}_{K},N),\in D^{b}(\mathrm{Alg}/F)
.

(2)

The morphism

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\mathbf{O}_{K},N)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\mathbf{O}_{K},M)\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-3]

obtained by applying
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
to the morphism (
7.2.2
)
is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
7.2.3
,
3.1.5
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.10
.
∎

8.
Preliminary calculations on regular two-dimensional local rings

Let
A
be a local ring with residue field
F
.
As in
[
Sai86
, §4]
, assume all of the following:

(1)

A
is regular, excellent, two-dimensional and henselian.

(2)

The fraction field
K
of
A
has characteristic zero.

(3)

A
contains a primitive
p
-th root of unity
\zeta_{p}
.

(4)

Either of the following holds:

(a)

(p)
is divisible by exactly one prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}

and
A/\mathfrak{p}
is regular, or

(b)

(p)
is divisible by exactly two prime ideals

\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\beta}
,
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}+\mathfrak{p}_{\beta}=\mathfrak{m}
,
and both
A/\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}
and
A/\mathfrak{p}_{\beta}
are regular.

This is the situation we encounter after the embedded resolution
of
(\Spec A,\Spec A/\sqrt{(p)})
.
In this section, we mostly recall the results of
[
Sai86
, §4]
.
Basically we describe
H^{q}(A[1/p],\Lambda)
through
embedding into
H^{q}(K,\Lambda)
or
H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{q}}^{h},\Lambda)
,
where we have Merkurjev-Suslin’s theorem
[
MS82
]
.
This description will be used as a local theory in Sections
9
and
10

Set
R=A[1/p]
.
For any
q\geq 0
, let
K_{q}
be the
q
-th Quillen K-group functor
and
\bar{K}_{q}
the cokernel of multiplication by
p
on
K_{q}
.
We use the notation of Section
2.6
.
Let
P^{\prime}\subset P
be the subset consisting of primes not dividing
p

(so it is
P\setminus\{\mathfrak{p}\}
in Case (
4a
)
and
P\setminus\{\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{p}_{\beta}\}
in Case (
4b
)).
In Case (
4b
), for
\nu=\alpha
or
\beta
,
we write
K_{\nu}^{h}=K_{\mathfrak{p}_{\nu}}^{h}
and

A_{\nu}^{h}=A_{\mathfrak{p}_{\nu}}^{h}
.

8.1.
First calculations

Proposition 8.1.1
.

The map

H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{q}(K,\Lambda)

is injective for all
q
.
We have
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)=H^{q}(K,\Lambda)=0
for all
q\geq 4
.
If
F
is algebraically closed, then

H^{q}(R,\Lambda)=H^{q}(K,\Lambda)=0
for all
q\geq 3
.

Proof.

Consider the localization exact sequence (combined with purity)

(8.1.1)

\cdots\to H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{q}(K,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}H^{q-1}(\kappa(\mathfrak{q}),\Lambda)\to H^{q+1}(R,\Lambda)\to\cdots.

We have

H^{q}(\kappa(\mathfrak{q}),\Lambda)\cong\begin{cases}\Lambda&\text{if }q=0,\\
\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}&\text{if }q=1,\\
F_{\mathfrak{q}}/\wp F_{\mathfrak{q}}&\text{if }q=2,\\
0&\text{if }q\geq 3.\end{cases}

In the sequence (
8.1.1
),
the injectivity of the first map
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{q}(K,\Lambda)
for
q\leq 2

(and the surjectivity of the second map for
q\leq 1
) is obvious.
For
q=3
, the boundary map

K_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}

is surjective (see
[
Wei13
, Chapter V, (6.6.1)]
,
[
Sai87
, Lemma (1.16)]
).
Hence

H^{2}(K,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}

is surjective.
The injectivity for
q=3
follows.
For
q=4
, let
A_{\overline{F}}
be the henselian lift
of the algebraic closure
\overline{F}
to
A

and
K_{\overline{F}}
its fraction field.
Then
K_{\overline{F}}
has
p
-cohomological dimension
2

by
[
Sai86
, Theorem (5.1)]
.
Hence the map

H^{3}(K,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}H^{2}(\kappa(\mathfrak{q}),\Lambda)

can be identified with the natural map

H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}(K_{\overline{F}},\Lambda(2))\bigl)\to H^{1}\left(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),\bigoplus_{\overline{\mathfrak{q}}\in\overline{P}^{\prime}}\kappa(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})^{\times}/\kappa(\overline{\mathfrak{q}})^{\times p}\right),

where
\overline{P}^{\prime}
is the set of height one primes of
A_{\overline{F}}
not dividing
p
.
This is surjective already before taking
H^{1}(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),\;\cdot\;)
,
and hence itself surjective.
We have
H^{4}(K,\Lambda)=0
by the fact on the cohomological dimension cited above.
These imply
H^{4}(R,\Lambda)=0
.
The statement for algebraically closed
F
is implicit in the above.
∎

Proposition 8.1.2
.

The étale Chern class map
[
Wei13
, Chapter V, Example 11.10]

\bar{K}_{2}(R)\to H^{2}(R,\Lambda)

is surjective.
In the natural commutative digram

\begin{CD}\bar{K}_{2}(R)@>{}>{}>\bar{K}_{2}(K)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{\wr}V{}V\\
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)@>{}>{}>H^{2}(K,\Lambda),\end{CD}

the group
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)
is identified with the image of the map

\bar{K}_{2}(R)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K)
.

Proof.

The isomorphism

\bar{K}_{2}(K)\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{2}(K,\Lambda)

is Merkurjev-Suslin.
We have a localization exact sequence

K_{2}(R)\to K_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}\to 0

(see
[
Wei13
, Chapter V, (6.6.1)]
,
[
Sai87
, Lemma (1.16)]
).
Hence we have an exact sequence

\bar{K}_{2}(R)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}\to 0.

Comparing this with the localization exact sequence

(8.1.2)

0\to H^{2}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{2}(K,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}\to 0

obtained in the previous proposition, we get the result.
∎

Proposition 8.1.3
.

The group
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)
is generated by
Steinberg symbols
\{x,y\}
with
x,y\in R^{\times}
.

Proof.

We have localization exact sequences

\displaystyle K_{2}(A)\to K_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0,

\displaystyle K_{2}(R)\to K_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}\to 0.

From these, since
\mathbb{Z}
is torsion-free, we have exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to\Im(K_{2}(A)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K))\to\bar{K}_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}\to\Lambda\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to\Im(K_{2}(R)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K))\to\bar{K}_{2}(K)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}\to 0.

The snake lemma then gives an exact sequence

0\to\Im(K_{2}(A)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K))\to\Im(K_{2}(R)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K))\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}\to\Lambda\to 0.

As
A
is local,
K_{2}(A)
is generated by symbols (
[
Wei13
, Chapter III, Theorem 5.10.5]
).
Consider the subgroup of
\Im(K_{2}(R)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K))
generated by
symbols of the following form:

\begin{cases}\{x,\varpi\}\quad\text{with}\quad x\in A^{\times}&\text{in Case \eqref{0061}},\\
\{x,\varpi_{\alpha}\},\{x,\varpi_{\beta}\},\{\varpi_{\alpha},\varpi_{\beta}\}\quad\text{with}\quad x\in A^{\times}&\text{in Case \eqref{0062}},\end{cases}

where
\varpi
(resp.
\varpi_{\alpha},\varpi_{\beta}
) is a generator of
\mathfrak{p}

(resp.
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{p}_{\beta}
).
This subgroup surjects onto the kernel of

\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times}/\kappa(\mathfrak{q})^{\times p}\to\Lambda
.
Thus
\Im(K_{2}(R)\to\bar{K}_{2}(K))
is generated by symbols.
∎

Proposition 8.1.4
.

The natural map

H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{q}}^{h},\Lambda)

is injective for all
q\leq 2
.

Proof.

The injectivity is obvious for
q=0
.
For
q=1
, an element of the kernel corresponds to a finite étale covering of
X

completely decomposed at the points of
P\setminus P^{\prime}
.
The purity of branch locus (
[
Sta21
, Tag 0BMA]
) shows that
such a covering extends to a finite étale covering of
\Spec A
.
A finite étale covering of
\Spec A
comes from a finite extension of
F
.
As it is completely decomposed at the points of
P\setminus P^{\prime}
,
it is trivial.

For
q=2
, first we have
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)\cong H^{2}(R,\mathbf{G}_{m})[p]

since
\Pic(R)=0
.
We have a localization exact sequence

H^{2}(X,\mathbf{G}_{m})\to H^{2}(R,\mathbf{G}_{m})\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}H^{3}_{\mathfrak{q}}(A_{\mathfrak{q}}^{h},\mathbf{G}_{m}),

where
H^{\ast}_{\mathfrak{q}}
denotes cohomology with support on the closed point.
We have

H^{2}(X,\mathbf{G}_{m})[p^{\infty}]=H^{2}(A,\mathbf{G}_{m})[p^{\infty}]=H^{2}(F,\mathbf{G}_{m})[p^{\infty}]=0

by the regularity of
A
and the purity for Brauer groups
(
[
Gro68
, Theorem (6.1), Corollary (6.2)]
).
The second map factors as

H^{2}(R,\mathbf{G}_{m})\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}H^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{q}}^{h},\mathbf{G}_{m})\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{q}\in P\setminus P^{\prime}}H^{3}_{\mathfrak{q}}(A_{\nu}^{h},\mathbf{G}_{m}).

Thus the map is injective.
∎

The injectivity holds for
q\geq 3
as well.
We will see this in the next two subsections.

8.2.
Structure of cohomology by symbols: good case

Assume (
4a
)
at the beginning of this section.
Set
B=A/\mathfrak{p}
and
k=\kappa(\mathfrak{p})
.
Let
\varpi
be a generator of
\mathfrak{p}
,
which we take as a uniformizer for
K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
.
For
q\geq 0
,
we have a symbol map

(R^{\times})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to H^{q}(R,\Lambda),\quad x_{1}\mathbin{\otimes}\dots\mathbin{\otimes}x_{q}\mapsto\{x_{1},\dots,x_{q}\}.

For
m\geq 1
, define
U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)

to be the subgroup of
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)

generated by symbols
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{q}\}

with
x_{1}\in 1+\mathfrak{p}^{m}
.
For
m=0
, we set
U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)=H^{q}(R,\Lambda)
.
Define

\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)=U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)/U^{m+1}H^{q}(R,\Lambda).

The map
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\Lambda)

maps
U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)
into the subgroup

U^{m}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\Lambda)

recalled in Section
6.1
.
Let
e_{A}=v_{\mathfrak{p}}(p)

and set
f_{A}=pe_{A}/(p-1)
,
where
v_{\mathfrak{p}}
denotes the normalized valuation of
A_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
.

Proposition 8.2.1
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle B^{\times}/B^{\times p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\tilde{x},\varpi\},

where
\tilde{x}
denotes any lift of
x
to
A
.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle B/B^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}\varpi^{m},\varpi\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle\Omega_{B}^{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x\dlog(y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}\varpi^{m},\tilde{y}\},

where
x\in B
and
y\in B^{\times}
.

(4)

If
m=f_{A}
, then

\displaystyle\xi_{1}(F)

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p},\varpi\},

where
\tilde{x}
denotes any lift of
x
to
A
.

(5)

If
m>f_{A}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda)=0
.

Proof.

The maps are well-defined and surjective by classical calculations
and Propositions
8.1.2

and
8.1.3
.
They are injective by the injectivity result in
Proposition
8.1.4

and by comparison with
Proposition
6.1.2
.
∎

Proposition 8.2.2
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda\oplus B^{\times}/B^{\times p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\varpi^{i}\tilde{x}\}.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle B/B^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{\varpi^{f_{A}-m}}\right\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{\varpi^{f_{A}-m}}\right\}.

(4)

If
m=f_{\mathfrak{p}}
, then

\displaystyle\xi_{1}(F)

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}\}.

(5)

If
m>f_{A}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda)=0
.

Proof.

Similar to (and easier than) the previous proposition.
∎

Proposition 8.2.3
.

Let
q=1
or
2
and
m\geq 0
.
Then
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\cap U^{m}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\Lambda)=U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

By the previous propositions,
the natural map

\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\Lambda)

is injective.
This implies the result.
∎

Proposition 8.2.4
.

Consider the maps

H^{2}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{2}(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\Lambda)\to\Lambda,

where the first map is the natural one and the second (
6.1.1
).
Their composite is zero.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
8.2.1
(
1
) and
6.1.2
(
1
).
∎

Proposition 8.2.5
.

We have
H^{3}(R,\Lambda)=0
.

Proof.

Using the same notation as the proof of
Proposition
8.1.1
,
for
R_{\overline{F}}:=R\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}A_{\overline{F}}
,
we have

H^{3}(R,\Lambda)\cong H^{1}(\Gal\bigl(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}(R_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\bigr).

We have

\gr^{m}H^{2}(R_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\cong\begin{cases}B_{\overline{F}}^{\times}/(B_{\overline{F}}^{\times})^{p}&\text{if }m=0,\\
B_{\overline{F}}/B_{\overline{F}}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{A},\,p\mid m,\\
\Omega_{B_{\overline{F}}}^{1}&\text{if }0<m<f_{A},\,p\nmid m,\\
0&\text{if }m\geq f_{A}\end{cases}

by Proposition
8.2.1
.
Applying
H^{1}(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),\;\cdot\;)
to these graded pieces for
m>0
yields zero.
For
m=0
, we have

H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),B_{\overline{F}}^{\times}/(B_{\overline{F}}^{\times})^{p}\bigr)\cong H^{2}(B,\mathbf{G}_{m})[p]\cong H^{2}(F,\mathbf{G}_{m})[p]=0.

The result then follows.
∎

In particular, the map

H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\Lambda)
is injective for all
q
.

Let
\hat{A}
be the completion of
A
.
We have corresponding rings
\hat{R}=R\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}\hat{A}
,

\hat{B}=\hat{A}/\mathfrak{p}\hat{A}
and

\hat{k}=\hat{\kappa}(\mathfrak{p})
.
Let
\hat{K}_{\mathfrak{p}}
be the complete local field of
\hat{A}
at
\mathfrak{p}\hat{A}
.
Its residue field is
\hat{k}
.
Even though
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)
changes by replacing
A
by
\hat{A}
,
the quotient
H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)/H^{q}(R,\Lambda)
does not:

Proposition 8.2.6
.

For any
q
, the natural map

\frac{H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)}{H^{q}(R,\Lambda)}\to\frac{H^{q}(\hat{K}_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)}{H^{q}(\hat{R},\Lambda)}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is obvious for
q=0
.
For
q=3
, this follows from
Propositions
6.1.1

and
8.2.5
.

Let
q=1
or
2
.
By Proposition
8.2.3
,
the image of
U^{m}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)

in
H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)/H^{q}(R,\Lambda)
defines a finite filtration
whose graded pieces are given by

\frac{\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)}{\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)}.

The same is true for

H^{q}(\hat{K}_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)/H^{q}(\hat{R},\Lambda)
.
We need to see that the natural map

\frac{\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)}{\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)}\to\frac{\gr^{m}H^{q}(\hat{K}_{\mathfrak{p}},\Lambda)}{\gr^{m}H^{q}(\hat{R},\Lambda)}

is an isomorphism.
For this, by Propositions
8.2.1
and
8.2.2
,
it suffices to observe that the cokernels of the natural maps

B^{\times}/B^{\times p}\hookrightarrow k^{\times}/k^{\times p},\quad B/B^{p}\hookrightarrow k/k^{p},\quad\Omega_{B}^{1}\hookrightarrow\Omega_{k}^{1},\quad B\hookrightarrow k

do not change by replacing
B
by
\hat{B}
and
k
by
\hat{k}
.
∎

8.3.
Structure of cohomology by symbols: bad case

Next, assume (
4b
).
Below we generally follow the notation of
[
Sai86
, Section 4]
.
For
\nu=\alpha
and
\beta
,
let
\varpi_{\nu}
be a generator of
\mathfrak{p}_{\nu}
.
Set
\bar{A}_{\nu}=A/\mathfrak{p}_{\nu}
and

k_{\nu}=\kappa(\mathfrak{p}_{\nu})
.
Let
e_{\nu}
be the normalized valuation of
p
in
K_{\nu}^{h}

and set
f_{\nu}=pe_{\nu}/(p-1)
.
Set
\nu^{\prime}=\alpha
if
\nu=\beta
and
\nu^{\prime}=\beta
if
\nu=\alpha
.
For an integer
j\geq 1
, let
\tilde{A}_{\nu}(j)
be
the image of
\varpi_{\nu^{\prime}}^{-j}\bar{A}_{\nu}
in

k_{\nu}/\wp k_{\nu}
.
Let
\tilde{U}_{k_{\nu}}^{(j)}
be the image of

1+\varpi_{\nu^{\prime}}^{j}\bar{A}_{\nu}
in

k_{\nu}^{\times}/k_{\nu}^{\times p}
.

Define
H^{1}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}
to be the kernel of the map
from
H^{1}(R,\Lambda)
to
H^{1}(K_{\beta}^{h},\Lambda)
.
Define
H^{1}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}
to be the quotient of

H^{1}(R,\Lambda)
by
H^{1}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}
.
Define
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}
to be the kernel of the map
from
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)
to
H^{2}(K_{\alpha}^{h},\Lambda)
.
Define
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}
to be the quotient of

H^{2}(R,\Lambda)
by
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}
.
We have a commutative diagram with exact rows

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}@>{}>{}>H^{q}(R,\Lambda)@>{}>{}>H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu^{\prime}}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)@>{}>{}>H^{q}(K_{\alpha}^{h},\Lambda)\oplus H^{q}(K_{\beta}^{h},\Lambda)@>{}>{}>H^{q}(K_{\nu^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)@>{}>{}>0,\end{CD}

where
q=2
(resp.
q=1
)
if
\nu=\beta
(resp.
\nu=\alpha
).

For
q=1
or
2
and
\nu=\alpha
or
\beta
,
the group
H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}
agrees with the groups
\Delta_{\nu}^{q}

in the notation of
[
Sai86
, (4.9)]

by Propositions
8.1.2

and
8.1.3
.
For
m\geq 0
, define

\displaystyle U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}=H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}\cap U^{m}H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda),

\displaystyle\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}=U^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}/U^{m+1}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}.

We have an injection
\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\nu}\hookrightarrow\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)
.

Proposition 8.3.1

(
[
Sai86
, (4.11)]
)
.

In Propositions
6.1.2

and
6.1.3
,
take
K=K_{\alpha}^{h}
,
\varpi=\varpi_{\alpha}
,

b_{m}=\varpi_{\alpha}^{m/p}
and
c_{m}=\varpi_{\alpha}^{m}/\varpi_{\beta}
.
Then, under the isomorphisms in
Propositions
6.1.2

and
6.1.3
,
the image of
\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\alpha}^{h},\Lambda)

is given as follows:

\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}\cong\begin{cases}k_{\alpha}^{\times}/k_{\alpha}^{\times p}&\text{if }m=0,\\
\bar{A}_{\alpha}/\bar{A}_{\alpha}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\alpha},\ p\mid m,\\
\Omega_{\bar{A}_{\alpha}}^{1}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\alpha},\ p\nmid m,\\
\xi(F)\oplus\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(f_{\beta})&\text{if }m=f_{\alpha},\end{cases}

\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}\cong\begin{cases}0\oplus\tilde{U}_{k_{\alpha}}^{(f_{\beta}+1)}&\text{if }m=0,\\
\bar{A}_{\alpha}/\bar{A}_{\alpha}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\alpha},\ p\mid m,\\
\bar{A}_{\alpha}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\alpha},\ p\nmid m,\\
