0&\text{if }m=f_{\alpha},\end{cases}

Proposition 8.3.2

(
[
Sai86
, (4.11)]
)
.

In Propositions
6.1.2

and
6.1.3
,
take
K=K_{\beta}^{h}
,
\varpi=\varpi_{\beta}
,

b_{m}=\varpi_{\beta}^{m/p}\varpi_{\alpha}^{f_{\alpha}/p}
and

c_{m}=\varpi_{\beta}^{m}\varpi_{\alpha}^{f_{\alpha}}
.
Then, under the isomorphisms in
Propositions
6.1.2

and
6.1.3
,
the image of
\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}\hookrightarrow\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\beta}^{h},\Lambda)

is given as follows:

\gr^{m}H^{2}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}\cong\begin{cases}\tilde{U}_{k_{\beta}}^{(f_{\alpha}+1)}&\text{if }m=0,\\
\bar{A}_{\beta}/\bar{A}_{\beta}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\beta},\ p\mid m,\\
\Omega_{\bar{A}_{\beta}}^{1}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\beta},\ p\nmid m,\\
0&\text{if }m=f_{\beta},\end{cases}

\gr^{m}H^{1}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}\cong\begin{cases}\Lambda\oplus k_{\beta}^{\times}/k_{\beta}^{\times p}&\text{if }m=0,\\
\bar{A}_{\beta}/\bar{A}_{\beta}^{p}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\beta},\ p\mid m,\\
\bar{A}_{\beta}&\text{if }0<m<f_{\beta},\ p\nmid m,\\
\tilde{A}_{\beta}(f_{\alpha})&\text{if }m=f_{\beta}.\end{cases}

Proposition 8.3.3
.

Consider the maps

H^{2}(R,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\nu=\alpha,\beta}H^{2}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)\to\Lambda^{2},

where the first map is the natural one and the second (
6.1.1
).
Their composite surjects onto the subgroup

(\Lambda^{2})_{0}\subset\Lambda^{2}
of elements with zero sum.

Proof.

By Propositions
8.3.1
,
8.3.2
and
6.1.2
(
1
),
the image is a rank one subgroup.
Hence it is enough to give an element of
H^{2}(R,\Lambda)

that maps to
(-1,1)\in\Lambda^{2}
.
Such an element is given by the symbol
\{\varpi_{\alpha},\varpi_{\beta}\}
.
∎

Proposition 8.3.4
.

For
\nu=\alpha
and
\beta
,
the map
H^{3}(R,\Lambda)\to H^{3}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It is enough to show this for
\nu=\alpha
.
The map can be written as

H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}(R_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\bigr)\to H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}((K_{\alpha}^{h})_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\bigr).

By Propositions
8.3.1

and
8.3.2
,
the group
H^{2}(R_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)
has a filtration
whose graded pieces are isomorphic to either

(8.3.1)

\bar{A}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ur}}/\bar{A}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ur}p},\;\Omega_{\bar{A}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ur}}}^{1},\;\tilde{A}_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ur}}(m),\;\tilde{U}_{k_{\nu}}^{\mathrm{ur}(m)},\;k_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ur}\times}/k_{\nu}^{\mathrm{ur}\times p}

for some
\nu
and
m
, where these are defined as the groups

\bar{A}_{\nu}/\bar{A}_{\nu}^{p},\;\Omega_{\bar{A}_{\nu}}^{1},\;\tilde{A}_{\nu}(m),\;\tilde{U}_{k_{\nu}}^{(m)},\;k_{\nu}^{\times}/k_{\nu}^{\times p}

with
A
replaced by
A_{\overline{F}}
.
Some classical calculations show that

H^{1}(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),\;\cdot\;)
of the first four groups in (
8.3.1
) is zero.
The map

H^{2}(R_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\twoheadrightarrow k_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{ur}\times}/(k_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{ur}\times})^{p}

is given by the tame symbol at
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}
.
This, with the valuation map at
\mathfrak{p}_{\alpha}
, induces an isomorphism

H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}(R_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\bigr)\overset{\sim}{\to}H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),\Lambda\bigr)\cong F/\wp F.

Similarly, we have

H^{1}\bigl(\Gal(\overline{F}/F),H^{2}((K_{\alpha}^{h})_{\overline{F}},\Lambda)\bigr)\overset{\sim}{\to}F/\wp F.

As these isomorphisms are compatible,
the result follows.
∎

In particular, the map

H^{q}(R,\Lambda)\to\bigoplus_{\nu=\alpha,\beta}H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)

is injective for all
q
.

Let
\hat{A}
be the completion of
A
and set
\hat{R}=R\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}\hat{A}
.
Let
\hat{K}_{\nu}
be the complete local field of
\hat{A}
at
\mathfrak{p}_{\nu}\hat{A}
.

Proposition 8.3.5
.

For any
q
, the natural map

\frac{\bigoplus_{\nu=\alpha,\beta}H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)}{H^{q}(R,\Lambda)}\to\frac{\bigoplus_{\nu=\alpha,\beta}H^{q}(\hat{K}_{\nu},\Lambda)}{H^{q}(\hat{R},\Lambda)}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is obvious for
q=0
.
For
q=3
, this follows from
Propositions
6.1.1

and
8.3.4
.

Let
q=1
or
2
.
The image of
\bigoplus_{\nu=\alpha,\beta}U^{m}H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda)

in

(\bigoplus_{\nu=\alpha,\beta}H^{q}(K_{\nu}^{h},\Lambda))/H^{q}(R,\Lambda)

defines a finite filtration whose graded pieces are given by

\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\alpha}^{h},\Lambda)/\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\alpha}

and
\gr^{m}H^{q}(K_{\beta}^{h},\Lambda)/\gr^{m}H^{q}(R,\Lambda)_{\beta}
.
Hence it is enough to see that for
\nu=\alpha,\beta
and
m\geq 1
, the groups

k_{\nu}/\bar{A}_{\nu},\quad k_{\nu}/(k_{\nu}^{p}+\bar{A}_{\nu}),\quad\Omega_{k_{\nu}}^{1}/\Omega_{\bar{A}_{\nu}}^{1},\quad\xi(k_{\nu})/\tilde{A}_{\nu}(m),\quad(k_{\nu}^{\times}/k_{\nu}^{\times p})/\tilde{U}_{k_{\nu}}^{(m)}

depend only on the completion
\hat{A}
.
But this is obvious.
∎

9.
Tubular neighborhoods of affine curves

In this section,
we first give a duality for cohomology of smooth affine curves over
F

with coefficients in vector bundles,
\Lambda
and
\nu(1)

(Section
9.1
).
This is more or less the classical coherent duality,
but we need to formulate it
in the style of fibered sites of Section
4.5
.
With this duality formulated in this style,
we give a duality for cohomology of
p
-adic tubular neighborhoods of smooth affine curves
for the rest of the section.
We build relative and fibered sites associated with tubular neighborhoods
and define nearby cycle functors in this setting
in Section
9.2
.
Then we introduce filtrations on nearby cycles by symbols
and prove a duality for the zero-th graded piece (over the relative étale site of the curve)
in Section
9.3
.
For the other graded pieces, in Section
9.4
,
we work over the relative Nisnevich site of the curve to prove the duality.
In Section
9.5
,
we combine these two pieces of the duality,
yielding the desired duality for cohomology of tubular neighborhoods.

9.1.
Relative sites for affine curves and duality

Let
B
be a smooth geometrically connected
F
-algebra of dimension
1

with function field
k
.
Set
V=\Spec B
.
Let
Y
be the smooth compactification of
V
.
Set
T=Y\setminus V
.
For each
x\in T
,
let
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{k_{x}}
be the completed local ring of
Y
at
x
,
with residue field
F_{x}
and fraction field
\hat{k}_{x}
.

For
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
, set

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})=B\mathbin{\otimes}_{F}F^{\prime},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}(F^{\prime})=\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{k_{x}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}_{F}F^{\prime},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})=\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{k_{x}}}\hat{k}_{x},

Note that
\hat{\mathbin{\otimes}}
in the second line is over
F
and not over
F_{x}
.
We have another functor

F_{x}^{\prime}\mapsto(\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{k_{x}}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}_{F_{x}}F_{x}^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{k_{x}}}\hat{k}_{x}

on
F_{x}^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
which is the functor (
5.1.1
) and
whose Weil restriction
\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}
to
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
is the above
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}
.
The functors
\mathbf{B}
,
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}
are
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebras,
and we have morphisms
\mathbf{B}\to\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}\leftarrow\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}
of
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebras.
Hence, for
\tau=\mathrm{et}
or
\mathrm{nis}
, we have sites

\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\tau}
and
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\tau}
and morphisms of sites

\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\tau}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}/\mathbf{B},\tau}}}{{\to}}\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\tau}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi_{\mathbf{B},\tau}}}{{\to}}\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\tau}.

We apply the constructions in Section
4.5
to
the morphisms

\bigsqcup_{x\in T}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\tau}\to\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\tau}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\tau}.

Denote the total site

(\bigsqcup_{x\in T}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\tau}\to\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\tau})

by
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\tau}
.
(The hat for the subscript
c
emphasizes
the complete local field
\hat{k}_{x}
rather than the henselian local field.)
The morphisms
\pi_{\mathbf{B},\tau}
and
\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\tau}
induce morphisms

\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\tau},\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\tau}\colon\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\tau}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\tau}.

We have a functor

\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\tau,\hat{!}}=\left[\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\tau,\ast}\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\tau,\ast}\right][-1]\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\tau})\to\mathrm{Ch}(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\tau}).

We have a morphism

(9.1.1)

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\tau,\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\tau,\hat{!}}H\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\tau,\hat{!}}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\tau})
functorial in
G,H\in D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\tau})
.
Let
\varepsilon\colon\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}

be the morphism defined by the identity functor.

The sheaf
\mathbf{G}_{a}
on
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}}
and on
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}}
naturally extends to
a sheaf on
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}
:
it is the triple

\left(\mathbf{G}_{a},(\mathbf{G}_{a})_{x\in T},\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}/\mathbf{B},\ast}\mathbf{G}_{a}\right).

We similarly have sheaves
\mathbf{G}_{m}
,
\Omega^{1}
,
\nu(1)
and
M
(a quasi-coherent sheaf on
B
).
We have exact sequences

(9.1.2)

\displaystyle 0\to\nu(1)\to\Omega^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C-1}}{{\to}}\Omega^{1}\to 0,

\displaystyle 0\to\mathbf{G}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to\nu(1)\to 0

in
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
, where
C
is the Cartier operator.
Hence
R^{n}\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1)=0
for
n\geq 2
.
Define

\xi(1)=R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1).

We have an exact sequence

(9.1.3)

0\to\nu(1)\to\Omega^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C-1}}{{\to}}\Omega^{1}\to\xi(1)\to 0

in
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}})
.
Define

\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}=\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\Omega^{1},\quad\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}=\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\Omega^{1},

which are Tate vector groups associated with

\Omega_{B}^{1}
,
\Omega_{k_{x}}^{1}
, respectively.
Now we calculate the sheaves

R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)
and
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)

and define a trace morphism (or the residue map) in the étale topology:

Proposition 9.1.1
.

We have
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)=R^{q}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)=0

for
q\geq 1
.
We have an isomorphism

\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)\cong\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}^{\times}/\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}^{\times p}

and a commutative diagram with exact rows

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>\Pic_{Y/F}^{0}[p]@>{}>{}>\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)@>{}>{}>\left(\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda\right)_{0}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{\mathrm{incl}}V\\
0@>{}>{}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}^{\times}/\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}^{\times p}@>{}>{\mathrm{incl}}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)@>{}>{}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda @>{}>{}>0,\end{CD}

where the left upper term
\Pic_{Y/F}^{0}[p]
is
(the perfection of) the part of the Jacobian of
Y
killed by
p
,
the right upper term
(\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda)_{0}
is the kernel
of the sum of the norm maps

\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\Lambda
,
and the right lower horizontal morphism

\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda

is the valuation map in each factor.

Proof.

The sheaf
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)
is
the étale sheafification of the presheaf

F^{\prime}\mapsto H^{q}(\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))
.
This presheaf as a functor in
F^{\prime}
commutes with filtered direct limits.
Let
\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime})=Y\times_{F}F^{\prime}

and
\mathbf{T}(F^{\prime})=\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime})\setminus\Spec\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})
.
Consider the localization distinguished triangles

R\Gamma(\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))\to R\Gamma(\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))\to\bigoplus_{x\in\mathbf{T}(F^{\prime})}R\Gamma_{x}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}),x}^{h},\nu(1))[1]

If
F^{\prime}
is algebraically closed, then

H^{q}(\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))

is isomorphic to
\Pic(\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}))[p]
if
q=0
;

\Lambda
if
q=1
; and zero otherwise.
Hence the étale sheafifications of the presheaves

F^{\prime}\mapsto H^{q}(\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))
are
\Pic^{0}_{Y/F}[p]
if
q=0
;

\Lambda
if
q=1
; and zero otherwise.
Similarly, the étale sheafification of

F^{\prime}\mapsto H_{x}^{q+1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}),x}^{h},\nu(1))

is
\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda
if
q=0

and zero otherwise.
Via these isomorphisms, the connecting morphism

\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda\to\Lambda

is given by the sum of the norm maps.
Hence we obtain the vanishing

R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)=0
for
q\geq 1

and the upper exact sequence of the statement.

On the other hand,
we have
R^{q}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)=0
for
q\geq 1

by applying
\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}
to the results of Proposition
6.1.6
.
We have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\Gamma(\mathbf{Y}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))@>{}>{}>\Gamma(\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\Gamma(\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{k_{x}}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))@>{}>{}>\Gamma(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime}),\nu(1))\end{CD}

for any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
From this, we obtain the desired commutative diagram.
∎

Proposition 9.1.2
.

We have
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)=0
for
q\neq 1
.
The distinguished triangle

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\Omega^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C-1}}{{\to}}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\Omega^{1}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
reduces to an exact sequence

0\to R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C-1}}{{\to}}\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}\to 0

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

It is enough to show that

C-1
is surjective on
\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}
.
But this follows from

R^{1}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)=0
.
∎

The sum of the residue maps
\Res\colon\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}
over
x\in T

is zero on
\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}
by the residue theorem.
Hence we have a morphism between exact sequences

\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},!}\nu(1)@>{}>{}>\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}@>{C-1}>{}>\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}@>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{\Res}V{}V@V{\Res}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>\Lambda @>{}>{}>\mathbf{G}_{a}@>{}>{\mathrm{Fr}^{-1}-1}>\mathbf{G}_{a}@>{}>{}>0.\end{CD}

With the inclusion
\Lambda\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{\infty}
, we have morphisms

(9.1.4)

R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to\Lambda_{\infty},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-1]

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
,
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
, respectively.
Here is a slightly different description of this morphism:

Proposition 9.1.3
.

The sum of the residue maps

\bigoplus_{x\in T}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)\to\Lambda

annihilates the image of
\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)
.
The obtained morphism
R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to\Lambda_{\infty}

via the exact sequence

\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)\to R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to 0

is the morphism (
9.1.4
).

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Now we calculate

R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}\xi(1)
and
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)

and define a trace morphism in the Nisnevich topology.
Proposition
9.1.2
also shows that

R^{q}\varepsilon_{\ast}R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)=0
for
q\geq 2
,
and we have an isomorphism and a morphism

(9.1.5)

R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\cong\Coker\left(C-1\text{ on }\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\to}}\xi

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
(remember
\xi=\mathbf{G}_{a}/(\mathrm{Fr}-1)\mathbf{G}_{a}
).

Proposition 9.1.4
.

For any
G\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}})
and
q\geq 2
, we have

R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}G=R^{q-1}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\mathrm{nis},\ast}G=R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}G=0.

Proof.

The statement
R^{q-1}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\mathrm{nis},\ast}G=0
is trivial
since fields have trivial Nisnevich cohomology.
For
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}G
,
it is enough to see that
H^{q}(B_{\mathrm{nis}},G)=0
for
q\geq 2
.
But this follows from the localization sequence
since
B
is one-dimensional.
∎

In particular, the functor
R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}
is right-exact.
The exact sequence (
9.1.3
) and the residue map induce an isomorphism and a morphism

(9.1.6)

R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)\cong\Coker\left(C-1\text{ on }\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\to}}\xi

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
.
With the inclusion
\xi\hookrightarrow\xi_{\infty}
, we have morphisms

(9.1.7)

R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)\to\xi_{\infty},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)\to\xi_{\infty}[-1]

in
\Ab(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
,
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
, respectively.
The two isomorphisms (
9.1.5
) and (
9.1.6
) are compatible with
the natural isomorphism between

R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)

and
R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)
by construction.

We have a representability result for the sheaves at hand:

Proposition 9.1.5
.

Let
G\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
be either

\Lambda
,
\nu(1)
or a finite projective
B
-module.
Then

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}G,R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Moreover,
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}G\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
for all
q
.

Proof.

If
G=M
is a finite projective
B
-module,
then
R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}M=\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}M
is
the underlying
F
-vector group
M
of countable dimension.
Hence it is in
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
For each
x\in T
,
the object

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}(M\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{k}_{x})=\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}(M\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{k}_{x})

is the underlying Tate
F
-vector group
M\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{k}_{x}\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
The object

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}M=\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}M\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{k}_{x}}{M}[-1],

in degree
1
, is a profinite-dimensional
F
-vector group whose dual has countable dimension.
Hence it is in
\mathcal{W}_{F}
.
These imply

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}G,R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}

by the exact sequences

0\to\Lambda\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to 0

and
0\to\nu(1)\to\Omega^{1}\to\Omega^{1}\to 0
.
For
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
,
one uses Proposition
9.1.1
,
and for
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\Lambda\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
,
it is enough to note that
\mathbf{B}/\wp\mathbf{B}
is the filtered union of
the quasi-algebraic groups

\frac{\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y}(pnT))\mathbin{\otimes}_{F}\mathbf{G}_{a}}{\wp\bigl(\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y}(nT))\mathbin{\otimes}_{F}\mathbf{G}_{a}\bigr)}

over
n\geq 0
.
∎

Now we give duality results for
\Spec B
:

Proposition 9.1.6
.

Let
M
be a finite projective
B
-module.
Set
N=\Hom_{B\mathrm{-module}}(M,\Omega_{B}^{1})
.
View them as sheaves of abelian groups on
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}
.
Consider the pairing
M\times N\to\Omega^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\xi(1)
.
Then the induced morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}M\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}N\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)\to\xi_{\infty}[-1]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{zar}})
is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

We have

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}M\cong M\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\mathbf{B},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}N\cong\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}N\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}{N\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\mathbf{B}}[-1].

The pairing (shifted by
1
) can alternatively be given by the natural morphisms

M\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\mathbf{B}\times\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}N\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}{N\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\mathbf{B}}\to\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{\mathbf{B}}^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\xi_{\infty}.

The pairing

M\times\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}N\mathbin{\otimes}_{B}\hat{k}_{x}}{N}\to\frac{\bigoplus_{x\in T}\Omega_{\hat{k}_{x}}^{1}}{\Omega_{B}^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Res}}{{\longrightarrow}}F

is a perfect pairing of Tate vector spaces over
F
by coherent duality.
Hence the result follows from
Proposition
3.4.5
.
∎

Proposition 9.1.7
.

Let
M
be a finite projective
B
-module.
Set
N=\Hom_{B\mathrm{-module}}(M,\Omega_{B}^{1})
.
View them as sheaves of abelian groups on
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}
.
Consider the pairing
M\times N\to\Omega^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\nu(1)[1]
,
where the last morphism is the connecting morphism for (
9.1.2
).
Then the induced morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}M\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}N\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)[1]\to\Lambda_{\infty}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
9.1.6
,
9.1.5
and
3.4.4
.
∎

Proposition 9.1.8
.

Let
(G,H)
be either pair of objects

(\Lambda,\nu(1))
or
(\nu(1),\Lambda)

of
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
Consider the natural pairing
G\times H\to\nu(1)
.
Then the induced morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}H\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-1]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

Using the exact sequences

0\to\Lambda\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to\mathbf{G}_{a}\to 0

and
0\to\nu(1)\to\Omega^{1}\to\Omega^{1}\to 0
,
this reduces to Proposition
9.1.7
.
∎

9.2.
Relative sites for tubular neighborhoods

Let
A
be a ring.
Assume all of the following:

(1)

A
is a two-dimensional regular integral domain.

(2)

A
contains a primitive
p
-th root of unity
\zeta_{p}
.

(3)

The radical
I
of the ideal
(p)
of
A
is principal.

(4)

B:=A/I
is a one-dimensional geometrically connected smooth algebra over
F
.

(5)

The pair
(A,I)
is complete.

This means that
\Spec A
is a
p
-adic tubular neighborhood of the curve
\Spec B
.
The conditions (
2
) and (
3
) are for simplicity.
We will apply the results of this section to two-dimensional local rings in the next section
after enough reduction steps where these conditions are satisfied.
In particular, the coefficient sheaf for the duality will be just
\Lambda
, not
\Lambda_{n}(r)
.
We do not try to give best general results for tubular neighborhoods of their own.

Note that
A
is excellent by
[
Gre82
, Proposition 2.3]
and
[
KS21
, Main Theorem 1]
.
Let
\varpi
be a generator of
I
.
Set
R=A[1/p]
.
We use the same notation as Section
9.1

applied to
B
.
For example, let
Y
be the smooth compactification of
V:=\Spec B
.

We build relative sites for
A
.
For any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
the
B
-algebra
\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})=B\mathbin{\otimes}_{F}F^{\prime}
is relatively perfect.
Define
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})
to be the Kato canonical lifting of
\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})
over
A
.
If
F^{\prime}
has only one direct factor,
then it satisfies the same conditions above as
A
,
with
F
,
B
,
I
replaced by
F^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})
and
I\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})
, respectively.
Set
\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})=\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}R
.
For a point
x^{\prime}
of
\Spec\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})
(
\subset\Spec\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})
),
let
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h}
and
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}
be
the henselian and strictly henselian local rings, respectively, of
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})
at
x^{\prime}
.
They are two-dimensional (resp. one-dimensional) excellent regular local rings
if
x^{\prime}
is a closed (resp. generic) point of
\Spec B^{\prime}
.
Set
\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h}=\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h}\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}R

and
\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}=\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}R
.

For any closed point
x\in Y
,
the complete local field
\hat{k}_{x}
of
Y
at
x
is relatively perfect over
B
.
Define
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}
to be the canonical lifting of
\hat{k}_{x}
over
A
.
It is a complete discrete valuation ring with prime element
\varpi
and residue field
\hat{k}_{x}
.
Let
\hat{K}_{\eta_{x}}
be the fraction field of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}
.
For any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
set
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})=\hat{k}_{x}\hat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{F}F^{\prime}
as in
Section
9.1
.
It is relatively perfect over
\hat{k}_{x}
.
Define
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})
to be the canonical lifting of

\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})
over
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}
.
There exists a unique
A
-algebra homomorphism
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})\to\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})

whose reduction
(\;\cdot\;)\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}B
is the natural map
\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})\to\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})
.
Set
\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})=\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}}\hat{K}_{\eta_{x}}
.
(The strange notation “
\eta_{x}
” is for consistency
when the results of this section are applied in Section
10.5
.)
For a point
x^{\prime}
of
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})
(
\subset\Spec\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})
),
let
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h}
and
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}
be
the henselian and strictly henselian local rings, respectively, of
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})
at
x^{\prime}
.
They are discrete valuation rings
with residue fields
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}
and
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{sep}}
, respectively.
(Note that
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})
is a finite product of complete discrete valuation fields.)
Set
\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h}=\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h}\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}R

and
\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}=\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}R
.

We have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}B@>{}>{}>\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\hat{k}_{x}@>{}>{}>\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})\end{CD}

All the morphisms are relatively perfect.
The Kato canonical lifting of this diagram to
A
is

\begin{CD}A@>{}>{}>\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}@>{}>{}>\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}(F^{\prime}),\end{CD}

The base change
(\;\cdot\;)\mathbin{\otimes}_{A}R
of this latter diagram gives a commutative digram

\begin{CD}R@>{}>{}>\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\hat{K}_{\eta_{x}}@>{}>{}>\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime}).\end{CD}

The functors

\mathbf{B},\hat{\mathbf{A}},\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}},\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}

are
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebras.
The one
\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}
is the canonical lifting system for
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}
.
We have a natural commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}@<{}<{}<\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}@>{}>{}>\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}\\
@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A@A{}A{}A\\
\hat{\mathbf{R}}@<{}<{}<\hat{\mathbf{A}}@>{}>{}>\mathbf{B}.\end{CD}

It defines a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x},\mathrm{et}}@>{j_{x}}>{}>\Spec\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}},\mathrm{et}}@<{i_{x}}<{}<\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}}\\
@V{\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}/\hat{\mathbf{R}}}}V{}V@V{\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}/\hat{\mathbf{A}}}}V{}V@V{}V{\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}/\mathbf{B}}}V\\
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathrm{et}}@>{}>{j}>\Spec\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{et}}@<{}<{i}<\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}}\end{CD}

of morphisms of sites.
Define
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}
and
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}
to be the total sites

\left(\bigsqcup_{x\in T}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x},\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathrm{et}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\bigsqcup_{x\in T}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}},\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{et}}\right),

respectively (and
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}
as before).
The morphisms
\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{R}}}
and
\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}}
induce morphisms

\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}}},\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}},

and the morphisms
\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}
and
\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}}
induce morphisms

\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}},\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}}}\colon\Spec\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}.

We have functors

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}=\left[\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\right][-1]\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})\to\mathrm{Ch}(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}),

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\hat{!}}=\left[\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\ast}\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}},\ast}\right][-1]\colon\mathrm{Ch}(\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})\to\mathrm{Ch}(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}).

We have a morphism

(9.2.1)

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}H\to R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
functorial in
G,H\in D(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})

and a morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\hat{!}}H\to R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\hat{!}}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
functorial in
G,H\in D(\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.

We will study the duality for

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}
and
R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}
.
For this, we use the following nearby cycle constructions.
The pairs
(j,j_{x})_{x\in T}
and
(i,i_{x})_{x\in T}
of morphisms induce morphisms

\Spec\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\jmath}}}{{\to}}\Spec\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\imath}}}{{\leftarrow}}\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}},

respectively.
Define

\displaystyle\Psi=i^{\ast}j_{\ast}\colon\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathrm{et}})\to\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{x}=i_{x}^{\ast}j_{x,\ast}\colon\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x},\mathrm{et}})\to\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}}),

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}=\bar{\imath}^{\ast}\bar{\jmath}_{\ast}\colon\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})\to\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}).

Their derived functors are described as follows.

Proposition 9.2.1
.

Let
\bar{G}\in D(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
Let
G
and
G_{x}
(where
x\in T
) be the natural projections
to
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathrm{et}}
and
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x},\mathrm{et}}
, respectively.
Then for any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
,
the sheaf
R^{q}\bar{\Psi}\bar{G}\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
is given by the triple

\left(R^{q}\Psi G,\,(R^{q}\Psi_{x}G)_{x\in T},\,R^{q}\Psi G\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}/\mathbf{B},\ast}R^{q}\Psi_{x}G_{x}\right).

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.5.2
.
∎

Proposition 9.2.2
.

Let
G\in D^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathrm{et}})
,

F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,

x^{\prime}\in\Spec\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})

and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(1)

The stalk of
R^{q}\Psi G\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}})
at
x^{\prime}
is given by

H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh},G|_{\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{et}})
.

(2)

The stalk of
R^{q}\varepsilon_{\ast}R\Psi G\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{nis}})
at
x^{\prime}
is given by

H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},G|_{\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{et}})
.

Here
G|_{\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{et}}\in D^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{\mathrm{et}})
is
pulled back to
D^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime},\mathrm{et}}^{h})
and
\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime},\mathrm{et}}^{sh})

and taken cohomology.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

Proposition 9.2.3
.

Let
G\in D^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x},\mathrm{et}})
,

F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,

x^{\prime}\in\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})

and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(1)

The stalk of
R^{q}\Psi_{x}G\in\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}})
at
x^{\prime}
is given by

H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh},G|_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{et}})
.

(2)

The stalk of
R^{q}\varepsilon_{\ast}R\Psi_{x}G\in\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{nis}})
at
x^{\prime}
is given by

H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},G|_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{et}})
.

Here
G|_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime}),\mathrm{et}}\in D^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{\mathrm{et}})
is
pulled back to
D^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime},\mathrm{et}}^{h})
and
\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime},\mathrm{et}}^{sh})

and taken cohomology.

Proof.

Obvious.
∎

As
\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}}}\mathbin{\circ}i
and
\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B}}

are equal as morphisms
\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
,
we have natural morphisms

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\ast}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}i^{\ast}

and
R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\hat{!}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}i^{\ast}
.
Hence we have two natural transformations

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}R\bar{\Psi},\,R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}R\bar{\Psi}\colon D(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}).

Proposition 9.2.4
.

The above two natural transformations are isomorphisms
when restricted to
D_{\mathrm{tor}}^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.2.4
,
we have
R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{A}},\ast}\overset{\sim}{\to}R\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}i^{\ast}

as
D_{\mathrm{tor}}^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
This implies that the first morphism is an isomorphism on
D_{\mathrm{tor}}^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
By Proposition
4.2.3
,
we have
R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}}},\ast}\overset{\sim}{\to}R\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}i_{x}^{\ast}
for any
x\in T

as
D(\hat{\mathbf{O}}_{K_{\eta_{x}},\mathrm{et}})\to D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
This implies that the second morphism is also an isomorphism on
D_{\mathrm{tor}}^{+}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
∎

For
G,H\in D(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
, we have a morphism

(9.2.2)

R\bar{\Psi}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\Psi}H\to R\bar{\Psi}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
functorial in
G,H

by (
2.2.1
) applied to
R\bar{\jmath}_{\ast}

and applying
\bar{\imath}^{\ast}
.
Hence we have a morphism

(9.2.3)

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}R\bar{\Psi}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}R\bar{\Psi}H\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}R\bar{\Psi}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
functorial in
G,H

by (
9.1.1
).

Proposition 9.2.5
.

The morphisms (
9.2.1
) and (
9.2.3
) are compatible,
that is, the diagram

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}H@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}R\bar{\Psi}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}R\bar{\Psi}H@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}R\bar{\Psi}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)\end{CD}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
is commutative.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
4.5.4
.
∎

9.3.
Duality for
\gr^{0}

We continue the notation from the previous subsection.
Set
\bar{\mathcal{E}}=R\bar{\Psi}\Lambda\in D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
,

\mathcal{E}=R\Psi\Lambda\in D(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}})
and

\mathcal{E}_{x}=R\Psi_{x}\Lambda\in D(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}})
,
where
x\in T
.
For any
q\geq 0
, the sheaf
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
is given by the triple

\left(H^{q}\mathcal{E},\,(H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x})_{x\in T},\,H^{q}\mathcal{E}\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}/\mathbf{B},\ast}H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x}\right)

by Proposition
9.2.1
.
By (
9.2.2
), the multiplication map
\Lambda\times\Lambda\to\Lambda
induces a morphism

(9.3.1)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\bar{\mathcal{E}}.

Proposition 9.3.1
.

The cohomology sheaves of

\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{E}_{x}
with
x\in T
and
\bar{\mathcal{E}}

are all zero in degrees
\neq 0,1,2
.

Proof.

Let
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
Let
x^{\prime}
be a closed point of
\Spec\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})
.
The stalk of
H^{q}\mathcal{E}
at
x^{\prime}
is

H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh},\Lambda)

by Proposition
9.2.2
.
The ring
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}
is a two-dimensional local ring
satisfying the conditions listed at the beginning of
Section
8
,
particularly (
4a
).
Its residue field is algebraically closed.
Hence
H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh},\Lambda)
is zero for
q\neq 0,1,2

by Proposition
8.1.1
.
Thus
H^{q}\mathcal{E}=0
for
q\neq 0,1,2
.

The stalk of
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x}
at
x^{\prime}
is

H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh},\Lambda)

by Proposition
9.2.3
.
The ring
\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}
is a henselian discrete valuation field
with residue field
\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{sep}}
.
Hence
H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x}=0
for
q\neq 0,1,2

by Proposition
6.1.1
.
The statement for
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
then follows.
∎

We will introduce a filtration on
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.
Consider the exact sequence

0\to\Lambda\to\mathbf{G}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to 0

in
\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
It defines a morphism

\{\;\cdot\;\}\colon\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to R^{1}\bar{\Psi}\Lambda

in
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
For any
q\geq 0
, the cup product then defines a morphism

\{\;\cdot\;,\dots,\;\cdot\;\}\colon(\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to R^{q}\bar{\Psi}\Lambda=H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

from the tensor power over
\mathbb{Z}
,
which we call the symbol map.
We can similarly define symbol maps

(\Psi\mathbf{G}_{m})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to R^{q}\Psi\Lambda=H^{q}\mathcal{E},\quad(\Psi_{x}\mathbf{G}_{m})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to R^{q}\Psi_{x}\Lambda=H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x}.

For any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
and any point
x^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh}
,
the morphism
(\Psi\mathbf{G}_{m})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to R^{q}\Psi\Lambda
on the stalk at
x^{\prime}

is given by the usual symbol map

((\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh})^{\times})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{sh},\Lambda);\quad(x_{1},\dots,x_{q})\mapsto\{x_{1},\dots,x_{q}\}.

The morphism
(\Psi_{x}\mathbf{G}_{m})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to R^{q}\Psi_{x}\Lambda
on the stalk at
x^{\prime}

is similarly described.

For
m\geq 1
, note that

1+I^{m}\bar{\imath}^{\ast}\mathbf{G}_{a}\subset\bar{\imath}^{\ast}\mathbf{G}_{m}\subset\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m}

in
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
Let
U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
be the subsheaf of
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

generated by the image of

\{1+\bar{\imath}^{\ast}\mathbf{G}_{a},\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m},\dots,\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m}\}
.
For
m=0
, we set
U^{0}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
to be the whole sheaf
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.
Define

\gr^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}=U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}/U^{m+1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}.

We similarly define sheaves

U^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{E}
,

\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{E}
,

U^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x}

and
\gr^{m}H^{q}\mathcal{E}_{x}

for each
x\in T
.
The cup product

H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to H^{q+q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

maps
U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times U^{m^{\prime}}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
to

U^{m+m^{\prime}}H^{q+q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

(see
[
BK86
, Lemma (4.1)]
).
A similar property holds for
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}_{x}
.
We have
H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\cong\Lambda
.

Now we describe the graded pieces.
Let
e_{A}
be the
\varpi
-adic valuation of
p

and set
f_{A}=pe_{A}/(p-1)
.
In what follows,
note the isomorphism
\dlog\colon\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}\overset{\sim}{\to}\nu(1)
.

Proposition 9.3.2
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\tilde{x},\varpi\}.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}\varpi^{m},\varpi\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle x\dlog(y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}\varpi^{m},\tilde{y}\},

where
x\in\mathbf{G}_{a}
and
y\in\mathbf{G}_{m}
.

(4)

If
m\geq f_{A}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}=0
.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions
8.2.1
and
6.1.2
.
∎

Proposition 9.3.3
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda\oplus\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\varpi^{i}\tilde{x}\}.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{\varpi^{f_{A}-m}}\right\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{\varpi^{f_{A}-m}}\right\}.

(4)

If
m\geq f_{A}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}=0
.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions
8.2.2
and
6.1.3
.
∎

Proposition 9.3.4
.

For any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
m\geq 0
,
the objects

\displaystyle R\bar{\pi}_{B,\ast}U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{B,\ast}\gr^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{B,\hat{!}}U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{B,\hat{!}}\gr^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}},

\displaystyle R\bar{\pi}_{B,\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{B,\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

are in
\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Moreover,
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{B,\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\in\mathcal{W}_{F}
for all
q
.

Proof.

This follows from the previous propositions
and Proposition
9.1.5
.
∎

By Propositions
9.3.1
and
9.3.2
(
1
), we have a canonical morphism

(9.3.2)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)[-2]

in
D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
With (
9.3.1
), we have canonical morphisms

(9.3.3)

\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)[-2]

in
D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.

We now split this pairing into the
\gr^{0}
part and the
U^{1}
part.
For
i=1,2
, define
\tau_{\geq i}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
to be the unique mapping cone of
the morphism
U^{1}H^{i}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-i]\to\tau_{\geq i}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.
Similarly, define
\tau_{\leq i}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
to be the unique mapping fiber of
the morphism
\tau_{\leq i}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\gr^{0}H^{i}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-i]
.

Proposition 9.3.5
.

Set
(\;\cdot\;)^{\vee}=R\sheafhom_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}}(\;\cdot\;,\nu(1))
.
Then there exists a unique way to extend
the morphism (
9.3.3
)
to morphisms of distinguished triangles

\begin{CD}H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>\tau_{\geq 1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
(\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}@>{}>{}>\bar{\mathcal{E}}^{\vee}[-2]@>{}>{}>(\tau_{\leq 2}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2],\end{CD}

\begin{CD}U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-1]@>{}>{}>\tau_{\geq 1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>\tau_{\geq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
(U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}@>{}>{}>(\tau_{\leq 2}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2]@>{}>{}>(\tau_{\leq 1}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2],\end{CD}

\begin{CD}\gr^{0}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-1]@>{}>{}>\tau_{\geq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-2]\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
(\gr^{0}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-1]@>{}>{}>(\tau_{\leq 1}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2]@>{}>{}>(\tau_{\leq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2],\end{CD}

\begin{CD}U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-2]@>{}>{}>H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-2]@>{}>{}>\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-2]\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
(U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-1]@>{}>{}>(\tau_{\leq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2]@>{}>{}>(H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2].\end{CD}

The obtained pairings

H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)
,

\gr^{0}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\gr^{0}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

and
\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

are induced by the usual cup products.

Proof.

To construct the first diagram,
note that the composite

H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to(\tau_{\leq 2}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-2]

factors through
(U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}
by a degree reason.
This corresponds to the cup product pairing

H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)
.
This is zero since the morphism
H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)
annihilates

U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.
Also, any morphism from
H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
to

(\tau_{\leq 2}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}})^{\vee}[-3]
is zero by a degree reason.
Then we obtain the first diagram by a formal argument on triangulated categories.
The rest of the diagrams can be obtained in a similar way,
using instead the fact that the cup product pairings

U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)
,

H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)
,

U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

are zero.
∎

By (
9.3.2
) and (
9.1.4
),
we have canonical morphisms

(9.3.4)

R\pi_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\pi_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)[-2]\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
With this and (
9.1.1
) and (
9.3.3
),
we obtain morphisms

R\pi_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\pi_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
.
Here is the duality for the
\gr^{0}
part:

Proposition 9.3.6
.

Let
q\in\{0,1,2\}
and set
q^{\prime}=2-q
.
Consider the cup product pairing

\gr^{0}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\gr^{0}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

in
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
.
The induced morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\gr^{0}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\gr^{0}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-1]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
,
where the last morphism is (
9.1.4
),
is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

After the identifications in Propositions
9.3.2
and
9.3.3
, the pairing

\gr^{0}H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

is the multiplication map

\Lambda\times\nu(1)\to\nu(1)
;
the pairing

\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\gr^{0}H^{0}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

is the multiplication map

\nu(1)\times\Lambda\to\nu(1)
;
and the pairing

\gr^{0}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\gr^{0}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)

is the morphism

\displaystyle\bigl((i,a),(j,b)\bigr)\in(\Lambda\oplus\nu(1))\times(\Lambda\oplus\nu(1))

\displaystyle\quad\mapsto\{\varpi^{i}\tilde{a},\varpi^{j}\tilde{b}\}\in\gr^{0}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle\quad\leftrightarrow(-1)^{ij}a^{j}b^{-i}\in\nu(1),

where we identified
\nu(1)
with
\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}
.
(Note that
H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
consists of
not only
\Lambda\oplus\nu(1)\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}})

but also
\Lambda\oplus\nu(1)\in\Ab(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}})
for each
x\in T

and a compatibility between them.)
Hence the result follows from
Proposition
9.1.8
.
∎

9.4.
Duality for
U^{1}
in the Nisnevich topology

Set
\mathcal{F}=R\varepsilon_{\ast}\mathcal{E}\in D(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{nis}})
,

\mathcal{F}_{x}=R\varepsilon_{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{x}\in D(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{nis}})

and
\bar{\mathcal{F}}=R\varepsilon_{\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\in D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}})
.
For any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, the sheaf

H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\in\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}})

is given by the triple

\left(H^{q}\mathcal{F},\,(H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{x})_{x\in T},\,H^{q}\mathcal{F}\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}/\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{x}\right)

by Proposition
4.5.2
.
The morphism (
9.3.1
) induces a morphism

\bar{\mathcal{F}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\bar{\mathcal{F}}.

The exact sequence

0\to\Lambda\to\mathbf{G}_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to 0

in
\Ab(\mathbf{R}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
induces a morphism

R\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to R\bar{\Psi}\Lambda[1]=\bar{\mathcal{E}}[1]

in
D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
, hence a morphism

R\varepsilon_{\ast}R\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to R\varepsilon_{\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[1]=\bar{\mathcal{F}}[1]

in
D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
, hence a morphism

\{\;\cdot\;\}\colon\varepsilon_{\ast}\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m}\to H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

hence a morphism

\{\;\cdot\;,\dots,\;\cdot\;\}\colon(\varepsilon_{\ast}\bar{\Psi}\mathbf{G}_{m})^{\mathbin{\otimes}q}\to H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}

for any
q\geq 0
.
Using this, for
m\geq 0
,
define a subsheaf
U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
of
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}

in the same way we defined
U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.
Define
\gr^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
similarly.
Note that since
R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}\mathbf{G}_{m}=0
by Hilbert’s theorem 90,
we know that
\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}\cong\nu(1)
also in
\Ab(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}})
.

Proposition 9.4.1
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\tilde{x},\varpi\}.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}\varpi^{m},\varpi\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle\Omega^{1}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle x\dlog(y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}\varpi^{m},\tilde{y}\},

where
x\in\mathbf{G}_{a}
and
y\in\mathbf{G}_{m}
.

(4)

If
m=f_{A}
, then

\displaystyle\xi(1)\oplus\xi

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle(x\dlog y,z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{1+\tilde{x}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p},\tilde{y}\}+\{1+\tilde{z}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p},\varpi\}.

(5)

If
m>f_{A}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}=0
.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions
8.2.1
and
6.1.2
.
∎

Proposition 9.4.2
.

Let
m\geq 0
.

(1)

If
m=0
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda\oplus\mathbf{G}_{m}/\mathbf{G}_{m}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle(i,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\varpi^{i}\tilde{x}\}.

(2)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\mid m
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{\varpi^{f_{A}-m}}\right\}.

(3)

If
0<m<f_{A}
and
p\nmid m
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}\frac{(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}}{\varpi^{f_{A}-m}}\right\}.

(4)

If
m=f_{A}
, then

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\to}\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{1+\tilde{x}(\zeta_{p}-1)^{p}\right\}.

(5)

If
m>f_{A}
, then
\gr^{m}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}=0
.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions
8.2.2
and
6.1.3
.
∎

We clarify the relation between
U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
and
U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proposition 9.4.3
.

Let
q\in\{1,2\}
.
Consider the spectral sequence

E_{2}^{ij}=R^{i}\varepsilon_{\ast}H^{j}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\Longrightarrow H^{i+j}\bar{\mathcal{F}}

and the induced exact sequence

0\to R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}H^{q-1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\varepsilon_{\ast}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to 0.

As subsheaves of
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
, we have

U^{f_{A}}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}=R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}H^{q-1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

By the previous propositions,
we know that
U^{f_{A}}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\cong\xi

and
U^{f_{A}}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\cong\xi(1)\oplus\xi
étale-sheafify to zero
and
H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}/U^{f_{A}}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
is an étale sheaf.
Such a subsheaf is necessarily
R^{1}\varepsilon_{\ast}H^{q-1}\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

The morphism (
9.3.3
) induces morphisms

\bar{\mathcal{F}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to R\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1)[-2]\to\xi(1)[-3].

For
q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
q^{\prime}=3-q
, this induces a pairing

(9.4.1)

H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\xi(1).

Consider its restriction

(9.4.2)

U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\xi(1).

Proposition 9.4.4
.

Let
q\in\{1,2\}
and set
q^{\prime}=3-q
.
Consider the morphism

\varepsilon_{\ast}U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}\varepsilon_{\ast}U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1)[1]\to\xi(1)

induced by the morphism

U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)[1]

in Proposition
9.3.5
.
The induced pairing

(9.4.3)

U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\varepsilon_{\ast}U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\times\varepsilon_{\ast}U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\xi(1)

agrees with the pairing (
9.4.2
).

Proof.

We only explain this for
q=2
.
The other case is similar.
Define
\tau_{\leq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
to be the canonical mapping fiber of the morphism

\tau_{\leq 1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}/U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}[-1]
.
Consider the commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{E}}[-2]\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
R\sheafhom_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}}(\bar{\mathcal{E}},\nu(1))[-2]@>{}>{}>R\sheafhom_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}}}(\tau_{\leq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{E}},\nu(1))[-2]\end{CD}

coming from the first three diagrams in
Proposition
9.3.5
.
Apply
H^{2}R\varepsilon_{\ast}
.
Since
\bar{\mathcal{E}}\cong\varepsilon^{\ast}\bar{\mathcal{F}}
,
we have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}@>{}>{}>\dfrac{H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\sheafext_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}}^{0}(\bar{\mathcal{F}},R\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1))@>{}>{}>\sheafext_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}}^{0}(\tau_{\leq 1}^{\prime}\bar{\mathcal{F}},R\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1)).\end{CD}

Applying the morphism
R\varepsilon_{\ast}\nu(1)\to\xi(1)[-1]

to the lower row, we have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}@>{}>{}>\dfrac{H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\sheafext_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}}^{-1}(\bar{\mathcal{F}},\xi(1))@>{}>{}>\sheafhom_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}}(U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},\xi(1)).\end{CD}

The pairing
H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\xi(1)

coming from this diagram is (
9.4.2
).
On the other hand, applying
H^{2}
to the fourth diagram of
Proposition
9.3.5
,
we have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\dfrac{U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}@>{}>{}>\dfrac{H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\sheafhom_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}}(U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},\xi(1))=\sheafhom_{\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{nis}}}(U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}},\xi(1)).\end{CD}

The pairing
U^{1}H^{2}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times U^{1}H^{1}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\xi(1)

coming from the left vertical morphism is (
9.4.3
).
Combining the above two diagrams, we get the result.
∎

Consider the projections

(9.4.4)

\displaystyle H^{q}\mathcal{F}\times H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}\to\xi(1),

(9.4.5)

\displaystyle H^{q}\mathcal{F}_{x}\times H^{q^{\prime}}\mathcal{F}_{x}\to\xi(1)

of the morphism (
9.4.1
) to

\Spec\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}}
and
\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x,\mathrm{et}}
(where
x\in T
), respectively.
We describe these pairings.
For
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
the pairing (
9.4.4
) on the stalk at a point
x^{\prime}\in\Spec\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})
is

\displaystyle H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)\times H^{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)

\displaystyle\quad\to H^{3}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)

\displaystyle\quad\to H^{1}(\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\nu(1))

\displaystyle\quad\cong\Gamma(\mathbf{B}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\xi(1)),

where
H^{3}(\hat{\mathbf{R}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)=0
if
x^{\prime}
is a closed point
by Proposition
8.2.5

and the second map is the Kato boundary map if
x^{\prime}
is a generic point.
The pairing (
9.4.5
) on the stalk at a point
x^{\prime}\in\Spec\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})
is

\displaystyle H^{q}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)\times H^{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)

\displaystyle\quad\to H^{3}(\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\Lambda)

\displaystyle\quad\to H^{1}(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\nu(1))

\displaystyle\quad\cong\Gamma(\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x}(F^{\prime})_{x^{\prime}}^{h},\xi(1)),

where the second map is the Kato boundary map.
Hence the restriction of (
9.4.1
) to

U^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times U^{m^{\prime}}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}

is zero if
m+m^{\prime}>f_{A}
.
Hence it induces a pairing

(9.4.6)

\gr^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\times\gr^{m^{\prime}}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to\xi(1)

for pairs
(m,m^{\prime})
with
m+m^{\prime}=f_{A}
and
m,m^{\prime}>0
.
This pairing can be completely calculated as follows.

Proposition 9.4.5
.

Via the isomorphisms in
Propositions
9.4.1
and
9.4.2
,
the pairing (
9.4.6
) becomes
the following pairings:

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}\times\mathbf{G}_{a}/\mathbf{G}_{a}^{p}

\displaystyle\to\xi(1),

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto adb.

for
q=2
,
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\mid m
;

\displaystyle\Omega_{B}^{1}\times\mathbf{G}_{a}

\displaystyle\to\xi(1),

\displaystyle(\omega,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto-mb\omega.

for
q=1
,
0<m<f_{K}
and
p\nmid m
.

Proof.

This is a local statement given in the proof of

[
Kat79
, §6, (14)]
.
∎

Hence we obtain a duality for these graded pieces:

Proposition 9.4.6
.

Let
q\in\{1,2\}
and
0<m<f_{A}

Set
q^{\prime}=3-q
and
m^{\prime}=f_{A}-m
.
Then the composite

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}\gr^{m}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\gr^{m^{\prime}}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)\to\xi_{\infty}[-1]

of the pairing (
9.4.6
) and the morphism (
9.1.7
) is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
9.4.5
and
9.1.6
.
∎

Proposition 9.4.7
.

Let
q\in\{1,2\}
and set
q^{\prime}=3-q
.
Then the composite

(9.4.7)

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}\frac{U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\frac{U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\hat{!}}\xi(1)\to\xi_{\infty}[-1]

of the pairing (
9.4.2
)
and the morphism (
9.1.7
) is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

This follows from the previous proposition.
∎

Now we bring this result in the Nisnevich/Zariski topology to the desired étale topology:

Proposition 9.4.8
.

Let
q\in\{1,2\}
and set
q^{\prime}=3-q
.
Consider the morphisms

U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to\nu(1)[1]

in
D(\mathbf{B}_{\hat{c},\mathrm{et}})
constructed in Proposition
9.3.5
.
The induced morphism

(9.4.8)

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)[1]\to\Lambda_{\infty}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
, where the last morphism is (
9.1.4
),
is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

We have

R\varepsilon_{\ast}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\cong R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\mathrm{nis},\ast}\frac{U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}{U^{f_{A}}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{F}}}

by Proposition
9.4.3
.
By Proposition
9.4.4
,
the morphism (
9.4.7
) can also be given by the composite

R\varepsilon_{\ast}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}U^{1}H^{q}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\varepsilon_{\ast}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}U^{1}H^{q^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\varepsilon_{\ast}\Lambda_{\infty}[1]\to\xi_{\infty},

where the first morphism is
R\varepsilon_{\ast}
of (
9.4.8
).
Hence the result follows from the previous proposition and
Proposition
3.4.4
.
∎

9.5.
Duality in full

We combine the duality for
\gr^{0}
and
U^{1}
into one:

Proposition 9.5.1
.

The morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\to R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)[-2]\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
induced by by (
9.3.3
) and (
9.1.4
)
is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
9.3.5
,
9.3.6
and
9.4.8
.
∎

We will interpret this result in terms of

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}
and
R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}
.
We have canonical isomorphisms

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda\overset{\sim}{\to}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}},\quad R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda\overset{\sim}{\to}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
by Proposition
9.2.4
.

Proposition 9.5.2
.

We have

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda,R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{F}\rangle_{F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}}
.
Moreover,
R^{q}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda\in\mathcal{W}_{F}

for all
q
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
9.3.4
.
∎

Proposition 9.5.3
.

We have
R^{q}\pi_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda=0
for
q\geq 3
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
9.3.2
and
9.1.1
.
∎

For any
x\in T
, we have a morphism

R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda\to\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda

by the Weil restriction of (
6.2.1
).
Hence the sum of the norm maps gives morphisms

\bigoplus_{x\in T}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda\to\Lambda.

Proposition 9.5.4
.

The above composite

\bigoplus_{x\in T}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda\to\Lambda

annihilates the image of

R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda
.
The obtained morphism

(9.5.1)

R^{3}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\infty}

via the exact sequence

R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda\to R^{3}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda\to 0

agrees with the morphism (
9.3.4
).

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda @>{}>{}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda @>{}>{}>R^{3}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda @>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\nu(1)@>{}>{}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{k}}_{x},\ast}\nu(1)@>{}>{}>R^{1}\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\nu(1)@>{}>{}>0.\end{CD}

Hence the result follows from Proposition
9.1.3
.
∎

Proposition 9.5.5
.

The image of the composite

R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda\to\bigoplus_{x\in T}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda

of the natural morphism and the morphism (
6.2.1
) is
the kernel
(\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda)_{0}

of the sum
\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda\to\Lambda
of the norm maps.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
9.1.1
.
∎

In particular, we have a commutative diagram

\begin{CD}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda @>{}>{}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}R^{2}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{K}}_{\eta_{x}},\ast}\Lambda @>{}>{}>R^{3}\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda @>{}>{}>0\\
@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V@V{}V{}V\\
0@>{}>{}>\left(\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda\right)_{0}@>{}>{}>\bigoplus_{x\in T}\mathfrak{R}_{F_{x}/F}\Lambda @>{}>{}>\Lambda @>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

with exact rows and surjective vertical morphisms.

Proposition 9.5.6
.

The composite morphism

R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda\to R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

of (
9.2.1
) and (
9.5.1
) is a perfect pairing.

Proof.

We have a commutative diagram

(9.5.2)

\begin{CD}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda @>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda @>{}>{}>\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]\\
@V{\wr}V{}V@V{\wr}V{}V\Big\|\\
R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\ast}\bar{\mathcal{E}}\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>R\bar{\pi}_{\mathbf{B},\hat{!}}\bar{\mathcal{E}}@>{}>{}>\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]\end{CD}

by Propositions
9.2.5
and
9.5.4
.
Hence the result follows from Proposition
9.5.1
.
∎

Define

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\hat{\mathbf{R}},\Lambda)=\acute{\mathsf{a}}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\ast}\Lambda,\quad R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\hat{\mathbf{R}},\Lambda)=\acute{\mathsf{a}}R\bar{\pi}_{\hat{\mathbf{R}},\hat{!}}\Lambda.

Theorem 9.5.7
.

(1)

We have

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\hat{\mathbf{R}},\Lambda),R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\hat{\mathbf{R}},\Lambda),\in D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Alg}_{u}/F)
.

(2)

The morphism

R\mathbf{\Gamma}(\hat{\mathbf{R}},\Lambda)\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\mathbf{\Gamma}_{c}(\hat{\mathbf{R}},\Lambda)\to\Lambda_{\infty}[-3]

obtained by applying
\acute{\mathsf{a}}
to the morphism (
9.5.2
)
is a perfect pairing in
D(F^{\mathrm{ind}\mathrm{rat}}_{\mathrm{pro}\mathrm{et}})
.

Proof.

This follows from Propositions
9.5.6
,
3.1.5
,
3.1.4
and
3.1.10
.
∎

Remark 9.5.8
.

The duality theorem in this section should be closely related to
the duality for relatively perfect nearby cycles (
[
KS19
]
) in the following manner.
Let
k_{0}
(
\ni\zeta_{p}
) be a mixed characteristic complete discrete valuation field
with prime element
\varpi
and residue field
F
.
Let
A_{0}
be a smooth algebra over
\mathcal{O}_{k_{0}}
with relative dimension
1

and geometrically irreducible fibers.
Then its
\varpi
-adic completion
A
satisfies
the conditions listed at the beginning of Section
9.2
.
The duality theorem in
[
KS19
]
gives a duality for
the relatively perfect nearby cycle functor

R\Psi^{\mathrm{RP}}\colon D(R_{0,\mathrm{Et}})\to D(B_{\mathrm{RPS}})
,
where
R_{0}=A_{0}[1/p]
and
B=A/\varpi A
.
This should be compatible with the nearby cycle functor

R\Psi\colon D(\hat{\mathbf{R}}_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(\mathbf{B}_{\mathrm{et}})
of this paper
in a suitable sense.

This paper might be simplified and
the results strengthened if we could just use the result of
[
KS19
]
.
This route does not seem possible at present, however,
since our
A
does not necessarily contain such a base
\mathcal{O}_{k_{0}}
.
Our theory is a fragment of a hypothetical theory of

p
-adic nearby cycles “without a base”.
It is hoped that there is such a theory applicable to the resolution of singularities

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\leftarrow}}Y
of Section
10.4
.

10.
Two-dimensional local rings

Let
A
be an excellent henselian normal two-dimensional local ring of mixed characteristic
(0,p)

with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
and residue field
F
.
We use the notation of Section
2.6
.
Let
\hat{A}
be the completion of
A
.

In Section
10.1
, we first formulate the duality for
A
over
\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
.
Its proof finishes at Section
10.6
.
For the proof, the case where the embedded resolution of
(\Spec A,\Spec A/\sqrt{(p)})
is already done
is treated in Section
10.3

using the results of Section
8
.
In Section
10.4
,
we take a resolution of
A
and localize the cohomology theory
along the special fiber (or the reduced exceptional divisor) of the resolution.
This gives a fibered site of the type of Section
4.6

consisting of relative étale sites of various henselian local rings and fields
and henselian neighborhoods of smooth affine curves over
F
.
In Section
10.5
,
we take the completions of these local pieces.
The duality for these completed local pieces are proved
by the duality results of Sections
6
,
9
and
10.3
.
In advance to that, we show the completion invariance
of the duality statement for
A
in an earlier Section
10.2
.
(It is necessary for
A
to be complete for the duality to hold,
but we may replace the henselian local fields at height one primes
by complete local fields.)

10.1.
Setup

As explained in Introduction, we need to vary the residue field of
A
.
Even though there is a canonical way to do so (see Introduction or Definition
10.1.2
below),
we need a slight more flexibility:

Definition 10.1.1
.

A
lifting system
for
A
consists of:

(1)

a functor
\mathbf{A}
from
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
to the category of
A
-algebras, and

(2)

an isomorphism
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})/\mathfrak{m}\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})\overset{\sim}{\to}F^{\prime}

of
F
-algebras functorial in
F^{\prime}
,

satisfying the conditions that:

(1)

\mathbf{A}
commutes with finite products,

(2)

\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
is flat over
A
for all
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,

(3)

if
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
is a field,
then
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
is an excellent henselian normal two-dimensional local ring
with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
, and

(4)

if
F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime\prime}
is an étale morphism in
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
then
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})\to\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime\prime})
is finite.

Condition
2
implies that
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})/\mathfrak{m}^{n}\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})

is the Kato canonical lifting of
F^{\prime}
over
A/\mathfrak{m}^{n}
for any
n
.
In particular,
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})\to\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime\prime})
is flat for all morphisms
F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime\prime}
in
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.
In Condition
4
,
F^{\prime}\to F^{\prime\prime}
being étale implies it is finite étale,
and
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})\to\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime\prime})
being finite implies it is finite étale.
If
F_{0}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
is a field,
then the restriction of
\mathbf{A}
to
F_{0}^{\mathrm{perar}}
is a lifting system for
\mathbf{A}(F_{0})
.
Such a non-complete lifting shows up from resolutions of
A
in Section
10.4
.

The Teichmüller map gives a canonical
W(F)
-algebra structure on
\hat{A}

(
[
DG70
, Chapter V, Section 4, Theorem 2.1]
).

Definition 10.1.2
.

The
canonical lifting system
for
A
is defined by

\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})=W(F^{\prime})\hat{\mathbin{\otimes}}_{W(F)}\hat{A}=\varprojlim_{n}(W_{n}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{W_{n}(F)}(A/\mathfrak{m}^{n}))

for all
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
.

This is indeed a lifting system for
A
.
If
F_{0}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
is a field,
then the restriction of
\hat{\mathbf{A}}
to
F_{0}^{\mathrm{perar}}

is the canonical lifting system for
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F_{0})
.
Below we fix a lifting system
\mathbf{A}
for
A
.
For any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
the completion of
\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})
with respect to
\mathfrak{m}\mathbf{A}(F^{\prime})

is canonically isomorphic to
\hat{\mathbf{A}}(F^{\prime})
.
In particular, we have a canonical morphism

\mathbf{A}\to\hat{\mathbf{A}}
of
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebras.

The lifting system
\mathbf{A}
is an
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebra
(Definition
4.1.1
).
Hence the inclusion morphisms

X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{A}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}\Spec A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{A}}}{{\hookleftarrow}}\Spec F

define an
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-scheme
\mathbf{X}
and sites and morphisms of sites

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{\mathbf{A}}}}{{\to}}\Spec\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i_{\mathbf{A}}}}{{\leftarrow}}\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}},

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{A}}\colon\Spec\mathbf{A}_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}},\quad\pi_{\mathbf{X}}\colon\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}.

We have
i_{\mathbf{A}}^{\ast}\cong\pi_{\mathbf{A},\ast}

by Proposition
4.2.3
.
Hence

R\Psi_{\mathbf{X}}:=i_{\mathbf{A}}^{\ast}Rj_{\mathbf{A},\ast}\cong R\pi_{\mathbf{X},\ast}.

Let
S\subset P
be a finite subset
and set
U_{S}=X\setminus S
.
The inclusion morphisms

U_{S}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{S}}}{{\hookrightarrow}}X\xleftarrow{\bigsqcup i_{\mathfrak{p}}}\joinrel\rhook\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{p}\in S}\Spec\kappa(\mathfrak{p})

define an
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-scheme
\mathbf{U}_{S}
, an
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebra
\bm{\mathfrak{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{p})

and sites and morphisms of sites

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{S}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{et}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bigsqcup i_{\mathfrak{p}}}}{{\longleftarrow}}\bigsqcup_{\mathfrak{p}\in S}\Spec\bm{\mathfrak{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{p})_{\mathrm{et}},

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S}}\colon\Spec\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}},\quad\pi_{\bm{\mathfrak{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{p})}\colon\Spec\bm{\mathfrak{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{p})_{\mathrm{et}}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}.

For each
\mathfrak{p}\in S
,
we have an
F^{\mathrm{perar}}
-algebra
\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}=\mathbf{O}_{X,\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
and
a site
\Spec\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p},\mathrm{et}}^{h}=\Spec\mathbf{O}_{X,\mathfrak{p},\mathrm{et}}^{h}

by the paragraphs after Proposition
4.1.13
.
Define

\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}(F^{\prime})=\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}(F^{\prime})\mathbin{\otimes}_{A_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}}K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}.

The natural morphisms define morphisms of sites and
a commutative diagram of morphisms of sites

\pi_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}}\colon\Spec\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p},\mathrm{et}}^{h}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}},\quad\pi_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}}\colon\Spec\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p},\mathrm{et}}^{h}\to\Spec F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}},

\begin{CD}\Spec\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p},\mathrm{et}}^{h}@>{\lambda_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}}>{}>\Spec\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p},\mathrm{et}}^{h}@<{i_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}}<{}<\Spec\bm{\mathfrak{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{p})_{\mathrm{et}}\\
@V{\pi_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}/\mathbf{U}_{S}}}V{}V@V{}V{\pi_{\mathbf{A}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}/\mathbf{X}}}V\Big\|\\
\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}}@>{}>{\lambda_{S}}>\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{et}}@<{}<{i_{\mathfrak{p}}}<\Spec\bm{\mathfrak{\kappa}}(\mathfrak{p})_{\mathrm{et}}.\end{CD}

We apply the constructions in Section
4.3
to
\lambda_{S}\colon\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}}\to\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{et}}
.
This defines a functor

R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}\colon D(\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lambda_{S,!}}}{{\longrightarrow}}D(\mathbf{X}_{\mathrm{et}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R\pi_{\mathbf{X},\ast}}}{{\longrightarrow}}D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}).

For any
F^{\prime}\in F^{\mathrm{perar}}
, define

R\Gamma_{c}(\mathbf{U}_{S}(F^{\prime}),\;\cdot\;)=R\Gamma(F^{\prime},R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}(\;\cdot\;))\colon D(\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}})\to D(\Ab).

For any
q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
G\in D(\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}})
,
the sheaf
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}G
is the étale sheafification of the presheaf

F^{\prime}\mapsto H_{c}^{q}(\mathbf{U}_{S}(F^{\prime}),G).

Therefore for any perfect field
F^{\prime}
over
F
with algebraic closure
\overline{F^{\prime}}
,
we have

(R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}G)(F^{\prime})=\left(\varinjlim_{F^{\prime\prime}}H_{c}^{q}(\mathbf{U}_{S}(F^{\prime\prime}),G)\right)^{\Gal(\overline{F^{\prime}}/F^{\prime})},

where
F^{\prime\prime}
runs through finite subextensions of
\overline{F^{\prime}}/F^{\prime}
.

For
G,H\in D(\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}})
,
we have a canonical morphism

(10.1.1)

R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},\ast}H\to R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}(G\mathbin{\otimes}^{L}H)

in
D(F^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}})
functorial in
G
and
H

by (
4.3.6
).
For
G\in D^{+}(\mathbf{U}_{S,\mathrm{et}})
, we have a canonical distinguished triangle

(10.1.2)

R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},!}G\to R\pi_{\mathbf{U}_{S},\ast}G\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in S}R\pi_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},\ast}\pi_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}/\mathbf{U}_{S}}^{\ast}G

by (
4.4.5
).
Applying
R\Gamma(F,\;\cdot\;)
yields a distinguished triangle

R\Gamma_{c}(U_{S},G)\to R\Gamma(U_{S},G)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in S}R\Gamma(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},G),

where the pullback of
G
to
K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h}
is denoted
by the same symbol
G
by abuse of notation.
Further localization gives a distinguished triangle

R\Gamma_{c}(U_{S},G)\to R\Gamma(K,G)\to\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in S}R\Gamma(K_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},G)\oplus\bigoplus_{\mathfrak{p}\in P\setminus S}R\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}}(A_{\mathfrak{p}}^{h},G)[1],

where
K
is the fraction field of
A
and

R\Gamma_{\mathfrak{p}}
denotes the cohomology with closed support.
Here is a trace morphism in this setting:

Proposition 10.1.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
.
We have
R^{q}\pi_{\mathbf{X},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)=0
for
q\geq 4
.
There exists a canonical morphism

(10.1.3)

R^{3}\pi_{\mathbf{X},\ast}\mathfrak{T}_{n}(2)\to\Lambda_{n}.

Proof.

Let
F^{\prime}
